**[p. 308]**

# III. Stamm. Echinodermata, Stachelhäuter [echinoderms].

In the systems of Cuvier and L. Agassiz, the animals now designated as Coelenteraten and Echinodermen are found united into a single type, the Radiata or Strahlthiere [radiate animals], for which the radiating arrangement of body parts repeated several times over was held to be the principal character. R. Leuckart was the first to point out the fundamental differences in the structure of the Anthozoen, Hydromedusen and Ctenophoren from that of the Echiniden, Seesterne [starfishes], Seelilien [sea lilies] and Holothurien, and separated the former, as the distinct type of the Coelenteraten, from the second group.

Even in their external appearance the Coelenteraten and Echinodermen show little agreement. The Echinodermen possess without exception the ability to produce a calcareous dermal skeleton consisting of regular small plates or of characteristically shaped calcareous bodies, whereas the calcareous secretions of the Coelenteraten are rather comparable to the internal skeleton of the Wirbelthiere [vertebrates]. The plated skeleton of the Echinodermen, frequently beset with spines, is pierced by numerous pores and openings, the outlets of regularly arranged internal organs. It is therefore also the faithful expression of the entire organisation of the animal and, from a systematic point of view, far more important than, for example, the calcareous shells of the Mollusken.

In contrast to the Coelenteraten, where the numbers four, six and eight prevail, in the Echinodermen all the more important body parts are as a rule repeated five times.

In the Seesterne one generally counts five arms radiating star-like from the centre; in the Seelilien five branched or simple arms arise almost invariably at the upper margin of the calyx; in the Echiniden or Seeigel [sea urchins] five principal water vessels and five nerve cords always proceed from the central axis of the body, dividing it into five equal or unequal segments. In the former case **[p. 309]** the Seeigel appears as a radially built spheroid; in the second case a bilateral symmetry arises through the unequal development of the segments. Even in the worm-shaped, laterally symmetrical Holothurien the dominant number five shows itself at least in the fivefold repetition of the more important organs.

While the Echinodermen thus in many cases display an eminently radiate structure, an axis can nonetheless be found in most forms which divides the entire body into two equal halves. This tendency towards bilateral arrangement is expressed still more definitely in the early juvenile stages. With few exceptions the Echinodermen arise from free-swimming bilateral larvae, provided with ciliated bands and sometimes with a calcareous framework formed of rodlets, which bear the greatest resemblance to those of certain Gliederwürmer [annelid worms]. In the interior of these larvae (Echinopaedium, Pseudoembryo), which are variously shaped according to the class, the embryo develops through the laying down of a body cavity and an ambulacral system and through the transformation of the mesoderm into radial segments. The circumstance that the latter is preceded by a veritable worm larva suggests the idea of a genetic relationship between Würmer [worms] and Echinodermen. As early as the year 1848 R. Leuckart pointed to morphological points of connection with the Gephyreen, and in the year 1851 Huxley declared the Rotifera to be "persistent forms" of the Echinodermen larvae. According to Haeckel, Gegenbauer and Sars the Seesterne originally arose through the fusion of five Gliederwürmer, and from these primitive Echinodermen (Asteroiden), which on this view would have to be regarded as colonies of worms, there branched off first the Crinoidea or Seelilien, then the Echinoidea or Seeigel, and lastly the Holothurioidea or Seegurken [sea cucumbers].

In contrast to these views, Agassiz and Metschnikoff trace the Echinodermen back to the Coelenteraten and emphasise in particular their relations to the Ctenophoren. Claus holds the Echinodermen to be a well-delimited unit and derives them from free-swimming bilateral stem forms, "which only after their fixation by the dorsal side, by means of asymmetrical growth processes, gradually brought about a radial configuration and segmentation of the internal organs, with the simultaneous formation of a corresponding radial protective skeleton of the skin". Palaeontology gives no information on the origin of the stem of the Echinodermen, for with the exception of the Holothurioideen all the classes appear already completely differentiated in the Silurian formation, and none of the organisms occurring with them can be claimed as their stem form.

**[p. 310]**

The Echinodermen differ from the Coelenteraten both in their external appearance and in their organisation. Among the Echinodermen there are no plant-like animal colonies arising through budding or self-division; rather, almost invariably each individual lives independently and reproduces by the sexual route. Through the possession of an intestine surrounded by walls, of a peculiar closed water-vascular system, through the development of nerves and sense organs, through special blood vessels, masticatory apparatus, ovaries, etc., the Echinodermen place themselves high above the Coelenteraten.

The most remarkable peculiarity encountered in all Echinodermen is the so-called **ambulacral or water-vascular system**. It carries a blood-like fluid, stands in close relation to the nutritive apparatus, but usually serves at the same time as an organ of locomotion and sometimes also of respiration. From a central ring surrounding the oesophagus, 5 vessels radiate outward, and these frequently fork again into lateral branches; from the latter then arise mobile, contractile and erectile tubes or tentacles, which on the surface mostly stand in grooves or rows. The feeding of the water apparatus takes place either through a special so-called stone canal, which receives its water through a finely porous madreporite plate situated on the surface (Echinoidea, Asteroidea), or through more or less numerous pores in the dermal skeleton, through which water reaches the body cavity and thence, by means of one or more canals, the ring vessel (Holothurioidea, Crinoidea).

In the Echinodermen the **dermal skeleton** usually forms a firm, coherent, rigid or flexible envelope; only exceptionally (Holothurien) is it restricted to isolated, regularly shaped calcareous bodies scattered in the skin. According to the arrangement of the elements of the dermal skeleton, which is subject to strict regularity, four principal groups or classes of Echinodermen result (1).

**1. Crinoidea (Seelilien, Haarsterne [feather stars]).**

Skeleton consisting of a plated calyx, which encloses the body cavity with the central organs, of more or less developed jointed arms, and frequently of a stem by which the animal is attached. No spines or other movable appendages present on the skeletal plates. Mouth and anus usually situated in the summit of the calyx.

---
(1) A systematic survey of the living and fossil Echinodermen is given by Dujardin and Hupé in their Histoire naturelle des zoophytes Echinodermes. Paris 1862.

**[p. 311]**

**2. Asteroidea (Seesterne).**

Body of star-shaped form, with a central disc from which 5 or more rays proceed. Skeleton with spine-like or wart-like appendages. Mouth and ambulacra on the underside.

**3. Echinoidea (Seeigel, Igelstrahler).**

Skeleton a test composed of regularly arranged small plates, of globular, heart-shaped or disc-shaped form. Surface beset with spines. Mouth on the underside. Ambulacra indicated by rows of pores running from the mouth to the summit.

**4. Holothurioidea (Seewalzen, Seegurken).**

Worm-shaped, bilateral bodies with leathery skin, in which regularly shaped calcareous bodies are scattered.

All the structures belonging to the calcareous skeleton of the Echinodermen, whether they lie in the skin or in the interior of the body, are distinguished by a very characteristic **structure**, which bears a constant character in all four classes with insignificant deviations. Excellent treatises on the structure of living Echinodermen have been published by Valentin (1) and W. B. Carpenter (2), and observations on the structure and state of preservation of fossil Crinoideen by A. Stelzner (3).

The apparently solid and compact small plates or calcareous plates of the Echinodermen, which in living or very little altered fossil remains show a conchoidal or uneven fracture, but in most petrified forms an excellent rhombohedral cleavage, consist of a network of crystal-clear calcareous rodlets which are deposited in an organic tissue. The latter can be decalcified by weak acid and in this state yields a faithful image of the calcareous skeleton itself.

All differences in the microstructure of the Echinodermen are determined by the dimension, form and arrangement of the meshes lying between the calcareous rodlets. If these are of equal size and shape, one obtains a fairly regular, delicate network consisting of parallel layers of sieve-like perforated plates, which are connected with one another by vertical little pillars. This structure recalls that of the Hexactinelliden-Spongien, except that the meshes of the lattice-work in the Echinodermen are as a rule smaller and of rounded form.

---
(1) Valentin in Agassiz L. Monographie d'Echinodermes vivans et fossiles. Neuchâtel 1838—41.
(2) Carpenter, W. B. Report of the 17th meeting of the British association for the advancement of Science in Oxford 1847 p. 117—127.
(3) Neues Jahrbuch für Mineralogie 1864 S. 565.

**[p. 312]**

This simplest structure is observed in the small plates and arm ossicles of the Crinoideen, the skeletal pieces of the Asteroideen, the test plates of the Echinoideen (Fig. 212 b) and the calcareous bodies in the skin of the Holothurien.

Fig. 212. a Horizontal section through an Echiniden spine (Fiji Islands), magnified. b Section parallel to the surface through a coronal plate of a recent Seeigel (*Sphaerechinus*), magnified.

Fig. 213. *Pentacrinus subteres* Goldf. White Jura. Reichenbach, Württemberg. a Median vertical section through the stem in the direction indicated in c, magnified 18/1. b Horizontal section, 18/1, cut in the direction indicated in d. c, d Stem in natural size.

Many stem ossicles of Crinoideen likewise show an entirely regular mesh-network (*Encrinus*, *Apiocrinus*, *Poteriocrinus*, etc.); frequently, however, one notices both in vertical and in horizontal section considerable deviations in the character of the network (Fig. 213). Coarse-meshed bands and zones alternate with finer-meshed ones, so that sections of such forms display peculiar patterns, whose principal lines, incidentally, already appear indicated in the external form or in the character of the articular faces. These differences are as a rule determined by tendinous bundles of fibres which traverse the stem ossicles in greater or lesser number and in very varied groupings. Among the genera with differentiated microstructure of the stem belongs, for example, *Pentacrinus* (Fig. 213).

By far the most beautiful and varied pictures are afforded by thin sections of Echiniden spines (Fig. 214 and 212 a). Here a radially radiating arrangement always prevails in the mesh-network, and indeed in such a way that the **[p. 313]** spines appear composed of vertical perforated lamellae, which are connected by horizontal cross-bars standing off at right angles and which radiate in a radial direction from the axis of the spine. In the centre the tissue frequently has a somewhat coarser-meshed character; indeed there are fossil (*Cidaris biformis* Mstr. from St. Cassian) and living forms with a completely hollow axis. In most of the Echiniden spines from the older formations examined by me, the radial lamellae stand at equal distances, and the tissue thereby acquires a fairly regular appearance. In recent or Tertiary forms, on the other hand, various differentiations occur (Fig. 212 a). For example, 2—3 lamellae group themselves together into one radius, which is connected with the neighbouring one by a fairly broad interspace bridged by cross-bars, or this intermediate radius is filled by apparently dense calcareous substance, which moreover has itself also arisen from a very fine lattice tissue. If, then, a change of structure occurs in the radial direction, which finds its expression on the surface in longitudinal ribs or granulations, in such cases a differentiation in the concentric direction also makes itself felt. One notices, according to the thickness of the spine, one or more rings of coarser-meshed or denser character, which evidently correspond to the successive layers which, as it seems, are deposited on the surface with periodic interruptions and thereby enlarge the spine. The diversity in the microstructure of the spines of one and the same genus (e.g. *Cidaris*) is so considerable that a more exact investigation would furnish good characters for the determination of species.

Through the process of petrifaction the characteristic microstructure is lost only in rare cases. Echinodermen remains from loose sandy or clayey Tertiary beds appear merely deprived of their organic content and give very clear pictures in thin sections **[p. 314]** under the microscope. Much more frequently, however, one finds the fossil Echinodermen skeletons completely impregnated with clear carbonate of lime. The intruding substance has filled all the meshes and interspaces, and since the latter occupy a larger space than the calcareous trabeculae, the infiltrated substance was able to crystallise unhindered. A spathic, rhombohedral character is therefore positively a characteristic feature of most fossil Echinodermen; it has, however, nothing to do with the original character of the skeleton, but is merely a secondary condition brought about by the process of fossilisation. A complete destruction of the microstructure by the infiltrated calcite takes place, it is true, only rarely; on the other hand, the excellent rhombohedral cleavage makes it difficult to prepare thin sections in any direction other than the cleavage planes, and moreover indistinct pictures frequently arise from the fact that the later-intruded calcite possesses exactly the same optical character as the calcareous rodlets of the skeleton. One therefore obtains, as a rule, the finest thin sections from pieces which are somewhat impregnated with iron oxide or other colouring substances. Favourable preservation is also shown, as a rule, by those specimens in which, instead of calcite, another substance, e.g. iron pyrites or brown iron ore, fills the meshes.

Fig. 214. Horizontal section through a spine of *Cidaris Anhaltina* Gieb. from the Oligocene of Lattdorf (magnified).

It was already demonstrated by Hessel (1) in the year 1826 that every single columnal and calyx ossicle of a Crinoid, every plate and every spine of a Seeigel corresponds to a single calcite individual; further, that in the columnals of the Crinoideen and in the spines of the Cidariten the crystallographic principal axis of the rhombohedra coincides with the longitudinal axis of these. Stelzner (2) and Quenstedt (3) have pursued these crystallographic phenomena further and attempted to explain them from the original skeletal structure.

Through the constancy with which the lattice-like microstructure recurs in all classes of the Echinodermen, one obtains an excellent means of recognising even the smallest fragments at least as Echinodermen remains, which is sometimes of importance in the microscopic examination of fine-grained limestones.

All the Echinodermen are inhabitants of the sea; they prefer to stay in the neighbourhood of the coasts in shallow water, yet numerous forms, and among them precisely those which stand nearest to the **[p. 315]** fossil ones, are also found in deep water and far from the mainland. Fossil remains are known in great numbers from all fossiliferous formations.

---
(1) Einfluss des organischen Körpers auf den unorganischen, nachgewiesen an Enkriniten, Pentakriniten und anderen Thierversteinerungen. Marburg 1826.
(2) Neues Jahrbuch für Mineralogie 1864 S. 565.
(3) Petrefaktenkunde Deutschlands Bd. IV 1874—76.

## 1. Classe. Crinoidea. Seelilien, Haarsterne. (1)

The Crinoiden are Echinodermen which are attached, either throughout life or in their youth, by means of a stem or else directly by the lower (dorsal) side of the body.

---
(1) Literature.

A. Principal works of more general content and more important treatises on living forms.

B. On fossil Crinoideen.

I. 1858 and Supplem. 1860. — Geological survey of Wisconsin. 1860. — 13th, 15th, 17th, 18th, 20th and 24th Report on the New York State Museum. 1858—1872.

**[p. 316]**

A globular or cup-shaped plated skeleton encloses the body cavity. On the upper (ventral) side of the calyx are situated, as a rule, the mouth and anus; around the former, or at the upper margin of the calyx, rise 5 or 10, more rarely 2, 4, 6 or more jointed arms beset with lateral appendages (Pinnulae). The generative organs lie outside the body cavity, in the arms.

Of this beautiful class, rich in forms, only a few genera still exist at present, of which several live at great depths of the sea and have become known only through the researches of the most recent times. Only the forms from the family of the Comatuliden are easily accessible to observation and are also frequently found on the European coasts; on these, therefore, rests everything that we know about the developmental history, reproduction, mode of life and nutrition of the Crinoideen.

In earlier periods of the earth, especially in the palaeolithic age, the Crinoideen were extraordinarily widespread; their fossil remains attracted attention as early as the 16th and 17th centuries, and long before living forms were known, the columnals and calyces of fossil genera were described and figured in detail under the names Trochitae, Entrochitae, Encrinitae, Pentacrinitae, Rädersteine [wheel-stones], Liliensteine [lily-stones], etc. The oldest authors, such as Agricola, Gessner, Bauhin, Lachmund and others, compared them with the Judensteine ["Jew-stones", i.e. spines of Seeigel]; Lister (1673), Lang, Luidius and others held them to be plants; most authors confined themselves to a description of the objects without deciding on their more specific zoological position.

---

**[p. 317]**

Rosinus (1720) was the first to point out the relationship of the fossil Crinoideen with the living Asteriden and especially with *Euryale*, and at the same time showed that the columnals were not independent bodies, as had hitherto been believed.

In the year 1733 Linck figured living *Comatula* species alongside Seesterne, and in 1755 Guettard described, as Palmier marin, the first specimen of a *Pentacrinus Asteria* L. (= *P. caput Medusae* Joh. Müll.) to reach Europe from Martinique, and declared it to be the living representative of all fossil Crinoideen with pentagonal stem. For the forms with round stem the living original was still to be found. This treatise marks an important advance; nevertheless it was only much later, through Blumenbach (1780), that the Crinoideen received their correct place in the system alongside the Seesterne and Ophiuren.

The two fundamental works on which all later studies on Crinoideen down to the present day are based are owed to two German naturalists. In the year 1821 J. S. Miller of Danzig published at Bristol, in the English language, his natural history of the Crinoideen, in which he not only furnishes an excellent description of all the species known to him, but also distributes them among 9 genera and sets up an ingenious terminology for designating the individual parts of the calyx. Still more path-breaking in their effect were the celebrated treatises of the Berlin anatomist and physiologist Joh. Müller on *Pentacrinus caput Medusae* and *Comatula*. Here one obtains for the first time exact information on the organisation of the animal, on the arrangement and physiological significance of the soft parts and on their relation to the hard skeleton. Joh. Müller replaced Miller's terminology by a new and more natural one, which with some modifications is still valid today.

Out of the exceedingly large number of works and treatises on fossil Crinoideen that were published in the course of this century, there stand out, by their wealth of fine observations and by their systematic importance, the writings of Goldfuss, Leop. v. Buch, Edw. Forbes, the two Austins, de Koninck, J. Hall, F. Roemer, d'Orbigny, Beyrich, Billings, Shumard, L. Schultze, Quenstedt, J. B. Meek, H. Angelin and Wachsmuth.

**[p. 318]**

Of outstanding importance for the interpretation of the fossil forms are also the ontogenetic works on living Crinoideen by J. V. Thomson, Wyv. Thomson, W. B. Carpenter, Al. Götte and Perrier, as well as the careful recent morphological-anatomical investigations of W. B. Carpenter, Sars, Greeff and especially of Hub. Ludwig and Herb. Carpenter.

Certain Crinoideen with well-developed stem, calyx and arms recall flowers (tulips and lilies) in their external appearance; the name Seelilien has therefore, not without justification, become established for them, even though numerous unstalked, sessile forms with stunted arms depart widely from the plant-like shape. In general, as in all Echinodermen, the fundamental number 5 prevails, especially in the composition of the calyx and the arms, yet here too there are various deviations from the rule. Entirely regular forms, consisting of 5 absolutely equal antimeres (*Callicrinus*, *Eucalyptocrinus*, *Encrinus*, *Eugeniacrinus*), are, despite the apparent regularity of these animals, rare, especially among the older representatives; as a rule that antimere above which the anus stands is distinguished from the others by a more or less deviant character.

Of the 3 principal parts of a Crinoid — calyx, arms and stem — the 2 latter may under certain circumstances be stunted or even entirely absent; the calyx, on the other hand, is always present and is unconditionally the most important structure of the whole body.

By the **calyx** (calyx) one designates the plated, mostly cup- or ball-shaped envelope of the body cavity, in which the central organs of the organism are found. It rests with its lower, narrowed pole either on the stem or directly on a firm substrate; the opposite, upper pole contains the mouth, which is either open or concealed beneath the cover, and as a rule the anal opening also. The upper side of the calyx (calyx cover) in the Crinoiden therefore corresponds to the lower or ventral side of the Asteriden and Echiniden, and the underside to the upper or dorsal side of the classes named. The single or double circle of small plates which rest directly on the stem and form the dorsal side of the calyx is called the **base** and is homologous with the apical shield of the Echiniden. Between the base and the calyx cover lie the sides, which are composed of several vertical rows of small plates forming either only one or several superposed horizontal circles. Those rows which lie in the direction of the arms and continue directly into them are called **Radialia**; the rows lying between them, if any **[p. 319]** such are present, **Interradialia**. In the continuation of one interradius, which is as a rule distinguished by a greater number of plates and is frequently the only one developed, lies the anus. It is therefore called the **anal interradius**. The ventral calyx cover is either plated or covered with a leathery skin in which small isolated calcareous bodies are scattered. It usually contains two openings, an excentric anus and a mostly central mouth. To the mouth lead 5 radial open furrows, which come from the arms; in many fossil forms, however, these open grooves are absent, as is the central mouth opening, or rather they lie beneath the plated cover. In rare cases the anal opening, which is always interradial, moves down onto the lateral faces.

Our knowledge of the internal organisation of the Crinoideen rests on the living genera *Comatula*, *Pentacrinus*, *Rhizocrinus* and *Hyocrinus*. Everything that can be said about the character of the soft parts therefore refers in the first instance to these genera. The viscera lie in the simple body cavity enclosed by the calyx. From the mouth opening the oesophagus leads into a fairly thick intestine, which is at first directed downward, then bends round near the base, makes a complete turn around the axis of the calyx, and finally returns to the cover in order to open there in the anal opening. On the inner side of its coil it gives off numerous outpocketings directed towards the ideal central axis, to which W. B. Carpenter ascribes the functions of the liver.

Immediately beneath the mouth opening the oesophagus is encircled by a ring-shaped water vessel (**ambulacral vascular ring**), which sends out 5 branches (**ambulacral vascular trunks**) in a radial direction towards the arms and moreover is in communication with the body cavity through short open hanging tubes. The 5 ambulacral vessels run in the above-mentioned open ambulacral or tentacle grooves of the calyx cover and fork 1—2 times near the periphery if more than 5 arms are present. The central vessel receives its water through the tube-shaped canals from the body cavity, into which it arrives through a greater or smaller number of calyx pores. According to H. Ludwig the embryo of *Antedon* at a certain stage shows only a single calyx pore, which communicates directly with the central ring of the water-vascular system through a tube (homologue of the stone canal). Both the ring vessel and the radial trunks send out upward small, closed-all-round, contractile and erectile tubes (mouth tentacles and **[p. 320]** marginal tentacles), of which the latter stand alternately on both sides of the ambulacral furrows.

Immediately above the radial ambulacral vessels, which are somewhat flattened above, run blood vessels, and above these a fine band-shaped layer of cells (according to H. Ludwig nerve trunks), which likewise unite into central rings above the water vessel. Finally, the surface of the ambulacral furrows is completely lined by a tall ciliated epithelium.

To the blood-vascular system belongs a fairly thick vessel ascending from the base of the calyx, the so-called **dorsal organ** or heart. It is divided by 5 radial partitions into 5 chambers and is surrounded by a fibrous sheath, from which solid **fibre cords** (according to Carpenter also nerves) penetrate in a radial direction into the calyx plates and arms, where they run in closed-all-round canals through all the ossicles to the tips of the arms.

The whole body cavity, so far as it is not filled by the organs described hitherto, is traversed by numerous strands of connective tissue which form a veritable mesh-network, in which calcareous bodies can be deposited. Near the mouth 5 branches of the central part of the body cavity, free from mesh tissue, separate off and extend beneath the water vessels into the arms; likewise the part of the body cavity lying outside the intestine continues to the tips of the arms and their ramifications.

The **arms** themselves arise mostly at the upper margin of the calyx and show quite extraordinary deviations in the different orders, families and genera. They are composed of numerous calcareous pieces (**arm ossicles, Brachialia**), which are connected with one another by articular faces. The ventral side, turned towards the mouth, is hollowed out by the so-called **ambulacral or tentacle groove**. In the Cystoideen the arms are very weakly developed, sometimes also bent back and grown fast by their dorsal side onto the calyx. In the Blastoideen they are replaced by 5 so-called pseudo-ambulacral fields of entirely different character. Usually one counts 5 arms, which either remain simple or fork and send out more or less numerous lateral branches; almost everywhere the arms — both the main trunks and the side branches — bear on the ventral side jointed appendages (**Pinnulae**), which sit on the outer margin of the ambulacral furrow and form alternating rows alongside it.

The tentacle or ambulacral grooves of the arms, lateral branches and pinnules all lead to the 5 ambulacral furrows of the disc **[p. 321]** and thence to the mouth. They are covered uppermost by an epithelial layer, beneath which lie the nerve cord, then the blood vessels and beneath these the radial water vessel, from which last alternating tentacle tubes proceed to right and left. The usually fairly large section of the tentacle furrow lying beneath the water vessel is occupied by the two superposed outpocketings of the body cavity, which are usually separated by a meshed intermediate tissue. In the middle of the latter runs a genital cord, which remains sterile in the axis of the arms, of the branches and in the disc, but in the pinnules develops either eggs or spermatozoa.

The **stem** or column (columna) of the Crinoideen consists of numerous roundish-cylindrical or pentagonal, more rarely elliptical pieces (**columnals, articuli**), which are pierced in the middle by a round or pentagonal tube, the so-called **nutrient canal**, and connected with one another by articular faces. The central canal leads from the base of the stem into the so-called dorsal organ of the calyx and, like the latter, is enveloped in a firm fibrous layer and provided with blood vessels. Here and there the column is beset with **cirri** (auxiliary arms), which are likewise composed of cylindrical ossicles and traversed by a central canal.

Sometimes the lower end of the stem spreads out into a jagged disc or thickens into a knobby root beset with runners, by which the animal grows fast onto a substrate; frequently, however, fine lateral cirri serving for attachment also proceed from the lower end, or the stem tapers downward without allowing any point of attachment to be recognised.

As organs of nutrition the arms at all events play a prominent role, even though they do not, as was formerly supposed, serve as grasping organs. In the healthy state they are spread out almost horizontally and only on dying (perhaps also under threat of danger) either roll themselves up or lay themselves side by side like the petals of a flower, forming above the calyx a pyramid closed all round. It is in this form that the fossil specimens still provided with arms are most frequently found. The supply of food takes place, according to the observations of W. B. Carpenter and Perrier, through the lively movement of the epithelial lining of the tentacle furrows, whereby a current of water carrying along the food particles (Diatomeen, Infusorien, microscopic Crustaceen and other organic corpuscles) arises towards the mouth.

**[p. 322]**

Of particular importance for the understanding of certain morphological phenomena in fossil Crinoideen, as well as especially for the ascertainment of their relationship and their genetic connection, is the developmental history (**ontogeny**) of the living forms. Unfortunately our knowledge in this respect is restricted to the genus *Antedon* (*Comatula*), which, incidentally, is especially instructive as a very differentiated form deviating widely from the fundamental type. Already the important discovery of J. V. Thomson, according to which the free-swimming, unstalked *Comatula* is in its youth attached by a jointed stem and is not unlike a *Pentacrinus*, gave a hint that the presence or absence of a stem is without essential influence on the organism as a whole and therefore must not be made use of in systematics. No less important facts result from the gradual development of the calyx and of the arms in the Comatuliden.

According to the more recent investigations of Wyville Thomson and W. B. Carpenter, which are supplemented by the observations of Al. Götte, the first cleavage stages of the fertilised eggs still take place at the base of the pinnules; the larva (Echinopaedium, Pseudoembryo) slipping out of the vitelline membrane possesses 4 ciliated bands, at the hinder pole a tuft of cilia and soon also a laterally situated mouth; it has entirely the appearance of certain Anneliden embryos. In the interior of this larva the actual embryo now develops, which gradually absorbs the larva. Very early, while still in the gastrula stage of the pseudoembryo, there arise in the subepithelial tissue of the skin 10 sieve-like perforated calcareous platelets, which group themselves in the anterior half of the larva, in the region of the intestine, 5 at a time into two symmetrical rings lying one above the other; after these there develops at the lower pole a series of perforated calcareous rings, which close below with a larger sieve plate. Thus one sees the calyx and stem already laid down in the larva, but all the platelets and rings are still completely isolated and separated by interspaces. Soon, however, the rings of the stem are connected by vertical bundles of small calcareous needles, and the 10 calyx platelets also enlarge in all directions. The still barrel-shaped pseudoembryo, beset with 4 ciliated bands, now begins to lengthen at the hinder end, the cilia disappear, the sarcode covering the skeleton is transformed into an epidermis, the lateral mouth and the original anus become obliterated, and there arises a stalked body with a thickened upper end, which attaches itself by means of the sieve plate (Fig. 215). In the calyx the two perforated **[p. 323]** rings of plates have attained a considerable size and almost touch one another with their lateral faces. The lower ring of plates (Basalia, Fig. 215 b) rests on the uppermost ring of the stem (Centrodorsale cd); the upper ring, consisting of triangular plates (oral plates, Oralia o), surrounds the newly formed mouth situated in the centre of the summit. At the base of the oral plates, and indeed in the corners where two of them meet, three hollow, movable tentacles now break forth in each case (thus 15 in all), whose cavities communicate with a ring canal on the inner side of the base of the Oralia. At the foot of these tentacles five new plates (Radialia) now form in the corners of the Basalia and Oralia as the first rudiment of the arms (Fig. 215 r), and at the same time the five corners draw out somewhat, so that from the ring vessel five radial canals lead to the beginnings of the arms. On the first radial plates, which grow rapidly in breadth and push the Oralia upward until the Radialia touch one another laterally and form a third ring of plates, there then arise rows of further elongated, narrow skeletal pieces of somewhat greater thickness than the three plates described hitherto. These are the arm ossicles (Brachialia), whose multiplication proceeds rapidly. The stem grows in length by the interpolation of new ossicles beneath the so-called centrodorsal plate at its upper end. The *Pentacrinus* stage of *Antedon* is now reached; the small, arm-bearing, long-stalked Seelilie has grown fast, possesses a calyx of 5 Basalia, 5 Radialia and 5 Oralia, as well as 5 simple arms.

Fig. 215. Larva of *Antedon rosaceus* (after Wyville Thomson). b Basalia, r Radialia, o oral plates, cd centrodorsal plate.

With the further development of the arms a reduction of the Oralia sets in, and after the former have fully developed their skeleton and have forked, the Oralia have entirely disappeared. At the same time changes also begin at the dorsal end. The uppermost columnal (Centrodorsale) expands at the expense of the Basalia, covers itself with cirri and finally overgrows the Basalia so completely that they disappear entirely externally and remain only as a fused sieve plate (rosette) in the interior of the calyx above the Centrodorsale. The last change consists in the stem detaching itself from the thickened Centrodorsale and the finished animal acquiring free locomotion.

**[p. 324]**

From the developmental history of *Antedon* it thus results that the Basalia, Oralia and the stem represent the earliest-formed parts of the skeleton; only later follow the Radialia and lastly the Brachialia. On the development of the fossil Crinoideen there are as yet almost no observations at all; only Ch. Wachsmuth (1) has demonstrated by means of a very rich material that in young specimens the Basalia are regularly the most completely developed skeletal parts, whereas the Interradialia and Analia appear latest. The latter sometimes increase in number during the whole lifetime of an individual; abnormal development or sudden change of the specific characters also shows itself most frequently in the anal interradius.

The Crinoideen can be divided, according to the development of the arms and the structure of the calyx, into three orders: Eucrinoidea, Cystoidea and Blastoidea, of which the two latter belong entirely to the three oldest formations, whereas of the former 8 genera still exist at present.

The **state of preservation** of the fossil Crinoideen is seldom so favourable that, even in the best-preserved pieces, all the important generic or specific characters can be established. Only very rarely are stem, calyx and arms found still in connection, and even in such exceptional occurrences the folded-together arms usually conceal the calyx cover, which is important from the systematic point of view. Usually the Crinoiden fall apart into separate pieces after death, and of these by far the most frequently found are isolated columnals, more rarely isolated calyces deprived of their arms, and most rarely calyces with arms and stem. The enormous quantity of fossil columnals in certain deposits, which thereby become veritable Crinoid or Trochite limestones, together with the extraordinary rarity of calyces and arms, can only be explained by the fact that the latter fall apart more easily and, on account of their lesser strength, could be destroyed more rapidly than the more robust columnals.

Almost always the fossil Crinoideen show, through infiltrated calcite, an excellent spathic character, which nevertheless seldom completely destroys the lattice-like microstructure. If, on the other hand, silica takes the place of the original skeleton, the finer structure is lost. Impressions and internal casts of Crinoideen occur fairly frequently, especially in shale and sandstone. In such deposits are also found the so-called **screw-stones**, **[p. 325]** i.e. stems of Crinoideen whose calcareous skeleton has been completely dissolved, whereas the mass of mud that penetrated into the central canal and between the articular faces has been preserved and now forms, in the cylindrical cavity, an axis which connects a series of parallel, thin, mostly striated horizontal discs.

---
(1) Proceed. Acad. nat. hist. Philad. 1878 p. 229.

### 1. Ordnung. Eucrinoidea. Armlilien.

(Crinoidea auct. sensu str., Brachiata J. Müll., Actinoidea F. Roem., Encrinidae Quenst.)

**Mostly long-stalked, rarely unstalked, free or attached by the underside, Seelilien with well-developed, free, movable arms proceeding from the upper margin of the calyx. Calyx consisting of regularly arranged small plates.**

Since the general structure and the development of this order, distinguished by its strong arms, have already been discussed in the preceding section, there remains only a more special consideration of the skeleton and of the terminology customary for it. Of the three principal parts, calyx, arms and stem, the first is the most important from the systematic point of view.

#### 1. The calyx (calyx)

consists in all Eucrinoideen of a moderate number of small plates arranged in vertical rows and horizontal circles, for which J. S. Miller first introduced an original terminology based on comparison with the skeleton of the Wirbelthiere. The lowest ring of plates, the dorsal base of the calyx, was called the pelvis (Pelvis), the principal lateral plates rib-pieces (Costalia), the plates lying between them inter-rib-pieces (Intercostalia), the arm-bearing plates shoulder-blades (scapulae), and the plates situated between the shoulder-blades Interscapularia. These designations, not always carried through consistently, were adopted by most authors until they were replaced by Joh. Müller (1) with a more natural and sharper terminology founded on the radiate structure of the skeleton. According to this, the plates which compose the base (the so-called pelvis) of the calyx are called **Basalia**, the rows of plates running towards the arms **Radialia**, the plates lying between them **Interradialia**, and since an interradius in whose continuation the anal opening lies is usually distinguished by a deviant **[p. 326]** number and arrangement of plates, its plates receive the name **Interradialia analia** or simply **Analia** (azygos plates). In the living Crinoideen the interradial plates are absent; the calyces thereby appear completely **regularly five-rayed** (regular) and can be divided into two symmetrical halves only by a line drawn through mouth and anus (radial axis). If in fossil forms all the interradii are uniformly filled by interradial plates (e.g. *Eucalyptocrinus*), the calyces are likewise designated as **regular**; as a rule, however, they become **irregular** either because Interradialia appear only in the anal interradius, or because the latter are more numerous or differently arranged than those of the other interradii (Fig. 216). Irregular calyces can, moreover, also arise through unequal development of the Radialia (*Haplocrinus*, *Pisocrinus*).

---
(1) Ueber den Bau von Pentacrinus caput Medusae. Sep.-Abz. S. 31.

a) The **base** has as a rule the form of a bowl, a funnel or a short cone and rests directly on the stem. Only very rarely are the basal plates entirely absent (*Plicatocrinus*) or overgrown by the Radialia and completely concealed (*Antedon*, *Eugeniacrinus* and *Phyllocrinus*), so that the lower Radialia follow directly on the uppermost columnal. Most frequently the base consists of 5 plates of equal or unequal form and size, but not infrequently one also finds 4 or 3 Basalia, whereas 2 Basalia are observed only exceptionally. Here and there all the Basalia also fuse into a single undivided piece. Almost always the base can be divided into 2 symmetrical halves by a line cutting through the middle, and this so-called dorsal axis always shows a law-governed position relative to that line which divides the calyx radii into 2 symmetrical halves (radial axis) (1). In very many fossil Crinoideen there are found beneath the first radial ring two circles of plates, both of which belong to the base. One thus distinguishes **monocyclic** and **dicyclic** bases. If only a single circle of 5 plates is present, the Radialia always alternate **[p. 327]** with the Basalia, i.e. the latter stand interradially; in the forms with dicyclic base, on the other hand, only the upper circle alternates with the Radialia, whereas the plates of the lower one stand mostly in the direction of the Radialia. Joh. Müller designated in the dicyclic base the plates of the **lower** ring as *Basalia*, those of the **upper** as *Parabasalia*, for which latter de Koninck later proposed the name *Subradialia* and Quenstedt the confusing designation **Zwischenradiale**.

Fig. 216. Projection of a Eucrinoid calyx with tripartite base (b), with 5 × 3 simple Radialia (r), 4 equal interradii (ir) and a 5th, unequal anal interradius (a).

---
(1) Beyrich, E., Ueber die Basis der Crinoidea brachiata. Monatsber. d. k. Akad. Berlin 1871.

In a substantial treatise Herb. Carpenter has discussed in detail the homologies between the base of the Crinoideen and the apical shield of the Echiniden. In the latter the apex is normally composed of two alternating circles of 5 plates each, of which the inner circle (the so-called genital plates) always occupies an **interradial** position, whereas the outer circle (the so-called ocellar plates) closes off the ambulacra above, and thus has a **radial** position. H. Carpenter now regards the 5 ocellar plates of the Echiniden as homologues of the 5 first radial plates in the calyx of the Crinoideen, in which he agrees with Lovén, Wyv. Thomson and almost all other authors. Very divergent, on the other hand, are the views on the homology of the genital plates. That these correspond, in the Crinoideen with monocyclic base, to the basal plates cannot be doubted; in the dicyclic base, on the other hand, there remains the choice between the Basalia and the Parabasalia (according to the terminology of J. Müller). Herbert Carpenter decides for that ring which occupies an **interradial** position, and thus, in departure from the views held hitherto, identifies the Basalia of the monocyclic base with the so-called Parabasalia of the dicyclic base, because these latter always occupy an interradial position. If one follows this excellently founded view, it results that the Parabasalia correspond to the Basalia in the monocyclic forms, but not to the **infrabasal** plates lying beneath them, hitherto erroneously designated as Basalia. If one were invariably to call the interradially situated plates of the dorsal disc Basalia, the definition of the calyx plates would, it is true, gain a far greater certainty than hitherto, for the Infrabasalia are very variable and sometimes such small structures that they are easily overlooked; moreover, by Carpenter's terminology the corresponding, homologous **[p. 328]** pieces would always be designated alike, whereas according to Müller's, as Beyrich has already demonstrated in *Encrinus* and *Apiocrinus*, equivalent parts are frequently given unequal names; since, however, misunderstandings would easily be provoked by such a terminological change, it is expedient to retain, in the dicyclic base, the designation **Parabasalia** for the upper ring of plates, but to call the plates of the lower ring, which are absent in the monocyclic base, **Infrabasalia** (underbasals Carp.) (Fig. 218).

Fig. 217. *Poteriocrinus* with dicyclic base and a single ring of Radialia.

The lower ring of plates of the dicyclic Crinoideen base (the Infrabasalia) appears not to be represented in the apical shield of the Echiniden; they are evidently intercalated pieces which in many cases can almost as well be reckoned to the stem as to the calyx.

In the unstalked fossil genera *Marsupites* (Fig. 219) and *Agassizocrinus* (Fig. 220) with dicyclic base, the two basal rings enclose in the former a thin central plate, in the latter a thickened knob, which correspond to the uppermost columnal (Centrodorsale) of the young *Comatula* and of the other Crinoideen, as well as to the unpaired central plate in the apex of the Echiniden.

b) Above the base the sides of the calyx are formed from the **radial plates** (r Fig. 216. 218. 219), between which plates are frequently intercalated in the anal interradius (a Fig. 216. 218) or also in all the interradii (ir Fig. 216). The number of the Interradialia varies even in very closely allied forms; the Radialia, on the other hand, always show a definite regularity in arrangement and number. **[p. 329]** With rare exceptions there are, corresponding to the arms, five radial rows present, which form now only a single, now several superposed horizontal circles (zones, rings), and which are designated according to their sequence as Radialia of the first, second, third, etc. order, R¹ always indicating the lowest circle. Likewise Interradialia of the first, second, third, etc. order (IR¹, IR², etc.) are distinguished. Frequently the radii divide upward in such a way that the upper end faces of the Radialia of one ring slope off roof-like and bear two parallel or diverging rows of plates (Fig. 216). Such calyx plates with roof-like end faces are called *Radialia axillaria*, and the two rows of plates arising by forking *Radialia distichalia* or simply *Distichalia* (dist.), in which case again Distichalia of the first, second, third, etc. order are distinguished. Sometimes the distichal rows also fork in the same way as the radial rows, and then those plates which possess two roof-like sloping end faces are called *Distichalia axillaria*. If the distichal radii do not border directly on one another but are separated by intermediate plates, the latter are called *Interdistichalia*.

Fig. 218. Stalked Eucrinoid (*Euspirocrinus*) with dicyclic base and anal interradius. a Interradialia analia, b Parabasalia, ib Infrabasalia, pr proboscis.

Fig. 219. a *Marsupites ornatus*. Calyx in natural size. b Analysis of the calyx (cd Centrodorsale, ib Infrabasalia, b Parabasalia, r Radialia).

Those Radialia which show themselves to be arm-bearing by a simple or by two axillary **articular faces** are called *Radialia articularia*.

If *Interradialia* are developed, the vertical rows either remain simple or they likewise multiply upward, so that two and more parallel rows arise. The latter occurs frequently, especially in the anal interradius.

Fig. 220. *Agassizocrinus laevis* Troost. From the Carboniferous limestone of Illinois.

c) The upper side of the calyx, **calyx cover** (tegmen calycis) or ventral side, spreads out as a horizontal or vaulted leathery skin or as a plated vault between the bases of the arms. In all living Crinoideen there is found in the summit a **central mouth opening** and an **excentric**, interradial **anal opening** (Fig. 221). From the mouth 5 open ambulacral **[p. 330]** furrows radiate towards the bases of the arms, and between these there lie in the angles of the mouth, in the young Comateln, in *Hyocrinus*, *Rhizocrinus* and *Holopus*, 5 triangular **oral plates**, which form a low pyramid and which in the Comateln disappear again completely with advancing development.

Fig. 221. Calyx cover of *Hyocrinus*, magnified. o oral plates, pst mouth (Peristoma), s marginal platelets, c dorsal canal in the arm ossicles, am ambulacral furrows in the arms and in the plated cover, an anus.

The so-called **interpalmar fields** lying between the ambulacral furrows are, in *Hyocrinus* (Fig. 219), plated with small but solid calcareous platelets; in *Pentacrinus* covered with very thin porous calcareous plates; in most of the Comatuliden and in *Rhizocrinus* covered with a membranous perisome in which delicate calcareous bodies lie scattered.

In most fossil Crinoideen from younger deposits (from the Trias upward) the calyx cover most probably had the same character as in the living forms, but presumably the loose joining and delicate character of the calyx-cover plates stood so much in the way of their preservation that up to now only a single fossil *Pentacrinus* species with plated summit has been found. As a rule the calyx cover is completely destroyed.

A structure of the calyx cover corresponding to the living *Hyocrinus*, or still more to the *Comatula* larva, is possessed by the Devonian *Coccocrinus rosaceus* F. Roem. (Fig. 222). It consists merely of 5 large, triangular, interpalmar oral plates, which leave free a mouth opening in the centre and ambulacral furrows between them. One of the 5 oral plates shows at its broad base a notch for an excentric anal opening. We have here, therefore, an embryonic stage of *Comatula* in persistent form, unless perhaps the central mouth opening was originally covered by small plates.

Fig. 222. Calyx cover of *Coccocrinus rosaceus* F. Roem. From Devonian limestone of the Eifel, at twice natural size. (After Schultze.)

In the closely allied genus *Haplocrinus* (Fig. 223) the summit is likewise formed of 5 triangular oral plates, which abut closely against one another laterally, so that the sunken ambulacral furrows are bounded by the bevelled sides of the oral plates and are completely closed towards the interior. In the centre the oral plates (probably originally plated over) leave no opening free, and likewise a lateral anal opening is absent.

Fig. 223. *Haplocrinus mespiliformis* Goldf. From Devonian limestone of the Eifel.

**[p. 331]**

If *Haplocrinus* and the closely related genera *Triacrinus* and *Pisocrinus* thus already differ considerably from the embryonic stage, and still more from the definitive structure of the calyx cover of the living Crinoideen, then in the overwhelming majority of the fossil forms from palaeolithic deposits still much more complicated phenomena present themselves.

The family of the Cupressocriniden is distinguished by the possession of a so-called "consolidation apparatus" (Fig. 224), which, consisting of 5 large interpalmar plates, forms a plated ring, or a pyramid open in the middle, at the base of the arms and encloses a large central opening. One of these plates possesses an egg-shaped notch at the calyx margin. F. Roemer and L. Schultze have ascribed to this peculiar framework the purpose of holding together the calyx, burdened by the thick arms, and at the same time of serving for the attachment and covering of the organs of the body cavity. According to their position and form, however, the plates of the consolidation apparatus evidently correspond to the interpalmar oral plates in *Comatula* and *Hyocrinus*, only they have been shifted out of the angles of the mouth towards the periphery. The lateral opening can with the greatest probability be interpreted as the anus, the central one as the mouth. It was observed by Wachsmuth that the central opening in *Symbathocrinus* is not open but roofed over by small plates, and that similar small plates also cover the furrows leading to the arms between each two oral plates.

In the large palaeozoic family of the Cyathocriniden (Fig. 225) the only rarely preserved calyx cover likewise possesses 5 large triangular oral plates, one of which is at the same time characterised as the anal plate by its divergent form and size, as well as by a notch at its peripheral base. The oral plates form, exactly as in *Haplocrinus*, 5 ambulacral furrows closed below, and surround a fairly wide central mouth opening. The latter, as well as the ambulacral furrows and anal opening, frequently even the whole ventral side, are covered by small plates, so that beneath **[p. 332]** this easily detached covering of small plates the oral plates lie partly or even entirely hidden and usually come into view only at the periphery or in the middle of the interpalmar fields. Often the excentric anal opening is situated at the end of a tubular prolongation (proboscis) of the plated apical cover.

Fig. 224. Calyx of *Cupressocrinus* with the so-called consolidation apparatus, seen from above.

Fig. 225. Calyx cover of *Cyathocrinus malvaceus*; a completely preserved, b after removal of the covering plates lying over the mouth opening, the ambulacral furrows (am) and the oral plates. (a anal oral plate, s marginal plates over the tentacle furrow of the arms.)

Fig. 226. *Lecythocrinus Eiflianus* Müll. with tubularly elongated anus.

The Poteriocrinidae, Crotalocrinidae, Taxocrinidae (Fig. 226) and Ichthyocrinidae behave similarly to *Cyathocrinus*; in these the anus is almost always drawn out like a proboscis and sometimes attains a considerable thickness. The oral plates, however, appear here to be developed only exceptionally, and, if present at all, are hidden beneath the vaulted, easily destroyed cover of small plates.

In a group of palaeozoic Eucrinoideen very rich in forms (Platycrinidae, Actinocrinidae, Melocrinidae, Rhodocrinidae, Calyptocrinidae) (Fig. 227 and 228) the oral plates are entirely absent and the ventral side of the calyx is arched over by a solid cover which consists of a greater or smaller number of small plates immovably joined to one another, which frequently show a regular arrangement. This plated cover sometimes spreads out horizontally between the arms, sometimes it is highly vaulted, now and then even drawn out flask-like or inflated balloon-like. It never contains more than a single, mostly excentric, more rarely central or subcentral opening, which either lies at the upper end of a plated tube or proboscis-like prolongation (proboscis), or is surrounded only by a ring of slightly raised small plates, sometimes also closed by a larger movable anal plate. Open ambulacral furrows never occur on calyx covers of this kind.

Fig. 227. *Hexacrinus elongatus* Goldf. Calyx with cover, a from the side, b from above.

Fig. 228. *Dorycrinus quinquelobus* Hall sp. with preserved calyx cover and excentric anus.

**[p. 333]**

In general, according to the foregoing, the condition of the calyx cover in the fossil Eucrinoideen from the older formations differs considerably from that of the living ones. Only exceptionally does one observe a central mouth opening, from which open ambulacral furrows proceed, beside an excentric anal opening. Usually, in well-preserved specimens, both the central opening with its oral plates and the ambulacral furrows show themselves concealed by a covering of small plates, or mouth and ambulacral furrows are totally absent and the solid cover contains only a single opening.

Concerning the interpretation of this opening, opinions have only very recently become clear. As long as the arms were held to be prehensile organs and the feeding arrangement of the fossil Crinoideen was imperfectly known, the apical opening was interpreted predominantly as the mouth (Austin, de Koninck) or as mouth and anus at the same time (F. Roemer, White, Bronn, Billings). In the forms in which a central mouth opening, oral plates and ambulacral furrows are present but hidden beneath the plated cover, the single opening in the apex can, by analogy with the living Crinoideen, only be the anus. Its interpretation accordingly remains doubtful only in the forms with immovably plated cover without oral plates beneath it. That this too, however, must be regarded solely as the anus follows from the important observations of Rofe and Billings on the entry of the ambulacral vessels beneath the calyx cover in *Actinocrinus* and related genera. All Crinoideen provided with a firm calyx cover namely show at the base of each arm a small gap which falls in **[p. 334]** the prolongation of the ambulacral furrow of the arm concerned. In *Actinocrinus* (Fig. 229) and, as Meek and Wachsmuth have later shown, in a series of other genera (*Batocrinus*, *Strotocrinus*, *Agaricocrinus* etc.) a tube plated all round passes from the tentacle groove of each arm beneath the calyx cover; all the tubes of one radius unite into a trunk, and these five main trunks run at a slight distance from the calyx cover towards the centre, where they all open into a central ring. This ring vessel is surrounded by small plates and, according to Wachsmuth, is provided in each interradius with a pore-like opening on the underside. Since in the living *Pentacrinus* also the ambulacral furrows of the disc are surrounded by three tiny rows of small plates, a lower (subambulacral) and two lateral (adambulacral) ones, to which are added the larger, movable marginal plates or supraambulacral plates, no doubt can prevail as to the significance of the plated tunnel tubes and of the ring vessel. They correspond to the ambulacral vessel system of the living Crinoideen, and the 5 interradial pores in the central ring to the mouths of the so-called stone canals in *Rhizocrinus* and *Comatula*. In all recent Crinoideen the central water vessel surrounds the gullet immediately beneath the mouth opening. But there is no reason to assume a different organisation for the fossil forms, and so the supposition first expressed by L. Schultze, that the mouth in the palaeozoic Crinoideen with a firm plated cover lies beneath it, that is subtegminally, appears entirely justified. But if this is correct, then the supply of food must have taken place, as in the living forms, in the ambulacral furrows, and indeed above the water vessel in the epithelial groove. By Wachsmuth, in fact, impressions of radial furrows leading to the centre have been directly observed above the ambulacral tubes on internal casts of *Actinocrinus* (Fig. 229 c).

Fig. 229. *Actinocrinus proboscidialis* Hall. a Calyx cover, partly broken open in order to show the plated tubes of the ambulacral vessels (a) coming from the arms. b Plated upper side of the subterranean ambulacral tubes. c Apex of an internal cast with the impressions of the supply canals (n) from the arms to the mouth (o), anus (an).

A confirmation of Schultze's view may also be offered by the peculiar organ, first discovered by Meek and Worthen, which in *Actinocrinus*, *Platycrinus*, *Ollacrinus*, *Habrocrinus* and others occupies the axis of the body cavity (Fig. 229 and 230). This is a wide, rolled-in, *Bulla*-like, finely porous cylinder, which begins beneath the centre of the apex, widens, then near the base narrows to a tube which bends round and, after several spiral turns, returns to the cover. Most **[p. 335]** probably this calcified organ corresponds to the intestinal wall in the living Crinoideen (1).

Fig. 230. *Batocrinus Verneuilianus* Shumard. Calyx broken open on one side in order to show the well-preserved internal folded organ. Carboniferous limestone. Burlington, Iowa. (After Meek and Worthen.)

From the preceding discussions there results, with respect to the condition of the calyx cover, a principal difference between recent and palaeolithic Crinoideen. In the former the ventral cover always contains the mouth opening; in the latter, on the other hand, over the oral plates or over the subtegminal mouth and the ambulacral tubes there stretches a plated vault, which is by no means homologous with the calyx cover in the mature recent Crinoideen, but rather, as Götte already supposed, corresponds to the apical cover of the *Comatula* embryo, beneath which, at an early stage of development, a subtegminal mouth is likewise found.

d) The intimate connection of the calcareous plates which compose the calyx allows them no mobility; they form either a solid shell closed all round or, when the calyx plates are only loosely connected with one another or lie isolated in the skin and therefore easily fall out, a shell solidly bounded only laterally and below. The calyx plates proper touch one another on all sides in sutural surfaces, between which there is only an extremely fine organic membrane. According to the plate connection of the calyx two large groups can be distinguished:

α) The Tesselaten, to which all palaeozoic forms belong, consist of thin or moderately thick small plates which join one another with flat, simple sutural surfaces.

β) In the Articulaten, to which the recent and most of the mesolithic and kaenolithic Eucrinoideen belong, the calyx plates are very thick and fit together only laterally with flat sutural surfaces; the surfaces turned upwards and downwards, on the other hand, are as a rule **[p. 336]** more or less strongly hollowed out or vaulted, so that they interlock in joint-like fashion. Sometimes an empty space also remains free in the interior between two similarly cut-out surfaces (*Apiocrinus*). All calyx plates of the Articulaten are traversed by radial fibre strands running towards the arms, which proceed from the so-called dorsal organ or heart.

---
(1) A remarkable phenomenon, very characteristic for some palaeozoic Crinoideen species from the genera *Platycrinus*, *Cromyocrinus*, *Ollacrinus* and *Actinocrinus*, consists in the occurrence of a cap-shaped, pointed gastropod shell (*Capulus*, *Platyceras*) on the calyx margin of the forms named, which possess solid plated covers with a single opening not tubularly prolonged. The *Capulus* shells sit with their base in such a way that they completely conceal the calyx opening, and since this phenomenon is frequently repeated, especially in *Platycrinus hemisphaericus*, in *Cromyocrinus geminatus* and other species, Austin put forward the supposition that the gastropods in question had been sucked out or consumed by the Crinoideen. This circumstance was urged as a weighty proof for the interpretation of the calyx opening as the mouth. However, Meek and Worthen have shown that the *Platyceras* shells with their lower margin as a rule follow all the elevations and irregularities of the Crinoideen test and must therefore without doubt have lived parasitically on them for a long time. They can therefore also not have served the latter as food.

e) Calyx pores of slit-shaped nature are found in the plated, balloon-shaped or tubularly prolonged cover of *Poteriocrinus*, *Coeliocrinus*, *Zeacrinus* and related forms; by Wachsmuth, furthermore, regularly arranged pores were observed at the base of the arms of *Actinocrinus*, *Batocrinus*, *Steganocrinus*, *Eucladocrinus* and *Ollacrinus*. These correspond to the respiratory pores in the perisome of the living Crinoideen, whose number and position vary considerably according to the genera. To be noted also are the pores, standing in connection with radial canals, on the lateral surface of the stem in *Crotalocrinus*, *Cytocrinus* and other genera in which the calyx pores are absent. Wachsmuth supposes that here the stem performs the function of the calyx pores.

### 2. The arms (brachia)

of the Crinoideen form an immediate continuation of the radialia and rest invariably by means of an articular surface on an uppermost calyx radiale. They consist of simple or double, and then always alternating, longitudinal rows of calcareous joints (brachialia, arm pieces Br.) and are accordingly called either uniserial or biserial. Frequently the brachialia possess a wedge-shaped form and lie over one another in such a way that alternately one joint turns its broad side to the right, its narrow side to the left; the following one, on the other hand, in the reverse position, the broad to the left, the narrow to the right. Zigzag sutures thereby arise between the joints, and arms of this kind are called alternating-serial. The alternating-serial condition of the arms is only a transition from uniseriality to biseriality. In the biserial arms the wedge-shaped joints reach only to the middle. Every biserial or alternating-serial arm begins uniserially. In general the arms are rarely simple, but mostly forked once or several times. Those arm joints above which the forking occurs are called brachialia axillaria and are distinguished at the upper end by two roof-shaped articular surfaces. One can then again distinguish Br. axill. of the first, second, third etc. order.

By Miller the main branches of the ramified arms were called hands (manus) and their side branches fingers (digiti). If no **[p. 337]** forking takes place, the arms are sometimes provided on both sides with alternating thin side branches. The latter, as well as the side and main branches, are moreover as a rule provided on the ventral side with short, thread-like, jointed appendages (pinnulae, Fiederfädchen, Fig. 231 a b c), in which in the living forms the generative organs develop. In some fossil genera (*Cyathocrinus*, *Taxocrinus* etc.) which lack the pinnulae, the genital organs perhaps came to maturity in the finer side branches. The wedge-shaped or three-sided small plates which are frequently found between the base of two branches are called clavicularia, those following above them interaxillaria. Interbrachalia are the small plates which, mostly immediately above the calyx cover, connect the neighbouring arms.

Fig. 231. a *Habrocrinus comtus* Ang. with uniserial arms, b *Scaphiocrinus aequalis* Hall, with alternating-serial arms, c *Callicrinus costatus* His. with biserial arms.

The arms are provided along their whole length, into the outermost ramifications, on the ventral side with a fairly deep groove (ambulacral furrow, tentacle groove), which in the lower, dorsal part contains the radial outpouchings of the body cavity, above them the genital strand, the water vessel, the blood vessels, the nerve band **[p. 338]** and uppermost an epithelial layer which serves at the same time for the supply of food. On the narrowed lateral margins of the ambulacral furrows there stand as a rule alternating pinnulae, which show exactly the same structure as the arm joints; in addition the tentacle grooves are covered, in some recent (*Pentacrinus*, *Rhizocrinus*) and in many fossil genera, by small alternating, mostly movable plates (marginal plates).

In a considerable number of palaeozoic Eucrinoideen pinnulae appear to be completely absent (Cyathocrinidae, Taxocrinidae, Crotalocrinidae, Ichthyocrinidae). In these forms the ambulacral grooves of the arms and of their side branches are completely roofed over by two rows of small alternating plates, and to these marginal plates there are frequently added two further rows of covering plates, so that the ventral groove of the arms is protected by four rows of small plates (Fig. 232). According to Ch. Wachsmuth, in *Cyathocrinus malvaceus* the triangular plates of the inner rows are movably connected with the four-sided ones of the outer rows by a tooth-shaped projection and could probably be raised and laid down.

Sometimes one also observes how the plated ambulacral tubes of the calyx continue into the arms and in the latter are separated by a partition from the dorsal part of the arm furrow (*Cyathocrinus*, *Cupressocrinus*).

In the living and many fossil Eucrinoideen (though not in all), all arm joints are traversed in their dorsal calcareous body by one (rarely by two) canals, which contain elastic fibre strands (according to Carpenter also nerve cords) proceeding from the so-called dorsal organ of the body cavity.

Fig. 232. Plated ventral furrows of the arms; a and b of *Cyathocrinus ramosus* Ang. with marginal plates, c of *Gissocrinus arthriticus* His. with marginal plates and covering plates (enlarged).

The connection of the arm joints is effected either by articular surfaces or by so-called syzygial sutures. In the first case each of the adjoining surfaces of two arm joints possesses one or also two raised, mostly oblique ridges or crests, which fit exactly upon one another, so that a certain mobility is thereby made possible. But thereby also larger or smaller interspaces arise, which are filled by elastic interarticular substance and on the ventral side by muscle masses. The insertion points of the muscles are frequently indicated by depressions. By syzygial **[p. 339]** sutures two joints are immovably connected like the calyx plates; the two smooth or striated surfaces lie immediately against one another and are separated only by a thin membrane, but not by interarticular substance or muscles. The arm joint which lies beneath such a syzygial suture is called, after Joh. Müller, hypozygal, the upper one epizygal. The brachialia connected by means of syzygies separate with difficulty and not rarely fuse completely with one another. They also as a rule count physiologically as a single joint, in that only the epizygal ever bears pinnulae.

Concerning the boundary between arms and calyx great uncertainty prevails in the literature. Joh. Müller designated in *Comatula* and *Pentacrinus* the first axillary plate as the uppermost (third) radiale and let the arms begin only above it. All authors down to L. Schultze have followed this example; indeed de Koninck, whose terminology was almost universally accepted, always lets the arms begin above the first forking of the radii, even when the distichal rows are quite immovably connected with one another and evidently belong to the calyx wall. de Koninck distinguishes in such cases the immovable "pièces brachiales" from the actual movable arm joints (articles brachiaux). F. Roemer already raised objections against this convenient but unnatural delimitation, and Ludw. Schultze substantiated these objections in a decisive manner. According to Schultze the arms begin invariably above the first articular surface of a firm calyx piece (radiale articulare). The articular facet is always easily recognisable either by a raised transverse crest and depressions for muscle masses, or by a semicircular, horseshoe-shaped notch, or by oblique truncation of the upper margin, or by both together. By the ring of articular facets an important horizon for the Crinoiden body is marked. All calyx pieces proper situated beneath it are immovably connected; the arm pieces lying above it, on the other hand, are movable and laterally more or less free (1).

---
(1) In the special part I have consistently carried through this delimitation of calyx and arms wherever this was possible on the basis of my own observation or from figures. Many formulae of the authors had thereby to be altered and reduced to Schultze's terminology. A very weighty circumstance in favour of the delimitation proposed by Schultze may also be the circumstance that the first radiale of the *Comatula* larva, according to Carpenter's excellent account, possesses the same form and nature as the calyx plates, while the two following pieces, hitherto designated as radialia 2 and 3, already in their earliest development agree entirely with the true brachialia.

**[p. 340]**

### 3. The stem or column (columna).

For the majority of the Eucrinoiden the considerable development of the stem forms a characteristic feature. In some genera (*Pentacrinus*) it becomes several feet long and is abundantly beset with side tendrils (cirri, auxiliary arms). Frequently it consists of completely equal, round, pentagonal or elliptical joints; often these become lower towards the top, and in some genera high joints alternate with low ones, thicker, somewhat more prominent ones with thinner, receding ones. When the uppermost stem joint is distinguished from the others by particular size or divergent form, it is designated as the centrodorsale (by some authors wrongly also as the basis) and is then sometimes reckoned to the calyx. The lower end either thickens into the root, which in some genera (*Apiocrinus*, *Millericrinus*) attains a mighty development, or it tapers to a more or less fine point, near which the stem is frequently beset with lateral tendrils. The growth of the stalk in length and thickness takes place partly by enlargement of the individual joints by means of deposition of fresh calcareous layers on all sides, partly by interpolation of new joints at the upper end.

The central canal (nutrient canal) of the stem has mostly a five-lobed or roundish form; into it, in the living forms, the chambered dorsal organ continues, so that one can distinguish in it a central and five peripheral vessels, which altogether are surrounded by firm elastic fibre cords. In *Pentacrinus* the five peripheral fibre cords assume a leaf-shaped form and are completely enclosed and penetrated by the calcareous skeleton. While the stem joints are already held together by these firm tendons, they possess a further connection in their upper and lower articulation surfaces, between which in the living state an elastic interarticulation substance is spread out cushion-like. The latter as a rule finds favourable points of attachment in the radially striated or rough nature of the articular surfaces; in some genera, moreover, individual joints now and then show themselves connected merely by syzygies. Movable articular connection of the stem joints by means of a raised articular crest is observed only exceptionally in a few genera (*Rhizocrinus*, *Bourgueticrinus*). The joints so connected never rest on one another with parallel surfaces, but on each articular surface a small ridge (crest) rises in a radial, oblique or transverse direction, running straight or angularly across the nutrient canal. These (1—2) small ridges usually cross one another at an oblique angle **[p. 341]** and thus permit a swaying of the joints in different directions. The joints of the lateral tendrils are, like those of the stem, connected by articulation surfaces or syzygies and all traversed by a central canal.

## Classification.

The first attempt at a classification of the forms here comprised as Eucrinoidea was made by J. S. Miller with principal consideration of the connection of the calyx plates. He accordingly distinguished 4 divisions: 1. Crinoidea articulata (*Apiocrinus*, *Pentacrinus*, *Encrinus*); 2. Cr. semiarticulata (*Poteriocrinus*); 3. Cr. inarticulata (*Cyathocrinus*, *Actinocrinus*, *Rhodocrinus*, *Platycrinus*); 4. Cr. coadunata (*Eugeniacrinus*). Although in this division the most closely related forms are united with a happy touch, yet the characters employed for the systematics are partly unessential, partly incorrect, and therefore Miller's classification too is not serviceable. Likewise the division of the Eucrinoideen (called *Pinnastella* by Austin, *Pinnigrada* by Forbes) proposed by the two Austins (1) into the 2 orders *Cionacineti* and *Liberidae*, of which the former contain all stalked and fixed, the latter all free-swimming forms, is entirely contrary to nature; moreover the proposed families lack any characterisation. F. Roemer (2) likewise distinguishes *Astylida* and *Stylida*, whereby however on the one hand closely related forms such as *Comatula* and *Pentacrinus* are torn apart, on the other hand quite heterogeneous genera such as *Marsupites*, *Agassizocrinus* are united with the Comatuliden.

Joh. Müller divided the Eucrinoideen known to him into 2 main groups: 1. *Articulata*, living and mesolithic genera with thick, joint-like connected calyx plates and mostly membranous perisome of the calyx cover; 2. *Tesselata*, palaeozoic forms with a solid plated cover and bowl- or cup-shaped calyx, whose thin plates are connected by simple sutures. To these was further added the group of the *Costata* with the single fossil genus *Saccocoma*.

Müller's division furnishes excellently delimited, natural groups and has, with small modifications, been adopted also in the present book. A special systematic work, however, which embraced the whole order of the Eucrinoideen and delimited the individual families more sharply, is up to now still completely lacking, for the systems adopted by Pictet and Dujardin et Hupé are antiquated **[p. 342]** and no longer usable. Numerous family names were indeed set up by Austin, F. Roemer and Angelin, but none of the authors named has supplied a diagnosis for them; moreover the works of Angelin refer only to a limited part of the Eucrinoideen. It was therefore of necessity required, in the following section, to make independently the attempt to arrange systematically the 3 main divisions of the *Tesselata*, *Articulata* and *Costata* and to divide them into families, whereby the important recent works of Angelin and Wachsmuth, as well as F. Roemer's division, were preferentially taken into account (1).

---
(1) Annals and Magazine of natural history 1842 vol. X p. 106.
(2) Lethaea geognostica. 3. Aufl. 1851. Bd. II S. 224.

## Synopsis of the suborders and families of the Eucrinoideen.

### 1. Suborder: Tesselata. Joh. Müll.

Calyx plates thin, immovably connected by simple sutures. Mostly IR present. Basalia 5 or by fusion 1, 2, 3 and 4. Base frequently dicyclic. Calyx cover solidly plated, more rarely formed by 5 oral plates. Mouth subtegminal. Anal opening subcentral or excentric, often a proboscis developed.

*A. Calyx cover consisting exclusively of 5 large oral plates or of the latter and a small number of covering plates over the mouth and the ambulacral furrows; the oralia form either a pyramid or a so-called consolidation apparatus. Arms simple, uniserial.*

**1. Family. Haplocrinidae. F. Roem.**
