Between R¹ and ² a large six-sided IR, upon which between R² 2 somewhat smaller ones each and between R³ three IR follow. In the anal interradius between R² stand 3 and above them 4–5 small plates. On the straight upper face of R³ rest 4 (or 5?) Dist. All interspaces in the upper part of the calyx are filled by small, strongly sculptured IR **[p. 373]** and Interdist. Each distichal row serves as support for an arm consisting of uniserial joints. These are deeply notched on the ventral side and beset at their base with numerous, closely crowded side-twigs. The side-twigs of the neighbouring arms cross one another and form in the interbrachial spaces a closed pavement; only further up do the arms become completely free and fork into numerous very long branches beset with fine, pinnule-like side-twigs. All parts of the arms consist of quite low, uniserial joints with marginally radiately striated articular facets. St. long, round; joints low, equal, with a very wide, round nutrient canal. *Sc. elegans* Zenk. Upper Silurian. Bohemia (Carlstein near Prague, Ét. E).

**Corymbocrinus** Ang. (*Clonocrinus* Quenst.). K. bowl-shaped, base funnel-shaped, depressed. B 4, visible only from below, forming a short hollow cone. R¹ transverse, bent over below, R² narrow, R³ axillary. Dist. 2 each, the lower larger, the upper arm-bearing, between them mostly 2 Interdist. IR 5 × (1/1), the lower very large, lying between R² and ³ and touching R¹. A. 5 × 2, very long, several times split, biserial, uniserial at their base. Pinn. long. Interbrachialia 5 × (1/1), following upon the IR. St. round, consisting of low joints. Articular facets radiately striated with the exception of a narrow outer zone. Nutrient canal five-lobed. Upper Silurian. England and Gotland. *Eucalyptocrinus polydactylus* M'Coy.

**Abacocrinus** Ang. (*Actinocrinites* p. p. Goldf.). K. egg-shaped, somewhat hollowed out at the base. B 4, unequal. R¹ unequal, five- to seven-sided, between them the lowest large IRA; R² six-sided, R³ axillary. IR 4 × (2/1). IRA numerous, the lowest large, octagonal, the following in several longitudinal rows. Dist. 2 each, the upper axillary. Interdist. 2 each. Interbrachialia numerous, small. A. 5 × 2, very long, several times forked, biserial, at first uniserial. Pinn. very long. St. round. Upper Silurian. Gotland. *Actinocrinites tesseracontadactylus* Goldf.

#### 22. Family. Polypeltidae. Ang.

*Base dicyclic. IB 8, PB 16. R 10 × 2. IR numerous.*

**Polypeltes** Ang. K. cup-shaped. IB numerous, at least 8, low. PB 16, high, unequal. R 10 (?) × 1, axillary. Arms 10 (?) × 2, forked towards the top, alternate-seriate. Pinn. long. IR numerous, in a row doubled towards the top. Interaxillaria in a single row. St. round, thick; articular facets smooth outside, the inner ring radiately striated. Nutrient canal five-lobed. Upper Silurian. Gotland. *P. granulatus* Ang.

#### 23. Family. Uintacrinidae. Zitt.

*K. unstalked, free, inequilateral. The 5 B enclose a small five-sided centrodorsal plate. R 5 × 3, Dist. 2 or more. IR numerous, the lowest between R². A. 5 × 2, long, uniserial, indistinctly separated from the calyx, connected by Interbrachialia.*

**Uintacrinus** Grinnell (Fig. 261). K. large, bulging, hemispherical, consisting of numerous thin small plates, unstalked. Base formed of a small five-sided centrodorsal plate and a ring of five-sided, equal, pointed B **[p. 374]** of small size. R¹ broad, six-sided, R² four- or six-sided, R³ axillary. Between R¹ and ² stand the large IR¹, upon which 2 IR² and above these 1–2 further zones of interradial small plates of 2 or more small plates each follow; the uppermost zone stands between the Rad. dist., of which 2 or more stand upon R³. The Dist. change quite gradually into rounded Brachialia, which are connected partly by articulations, partly by syzygial sutures, and form 10 uncommonly long, simple and uniserial arms. In the angles of the Dist. lie 2 rather large Interdist. above one another. From the 2nd or 4th joint of the distichal rows there arise on both sides alternating rows of small plates directed obliquely inwards and upwards, which become ever smaller towards the top and thereby produce entirely the appearance of side-arms, yet do not consist of arm-joints with a ventral groove. These are closely crowded and touch those issuing from the neighbouring arm, so that the interbrachial fields are completely filled. Towards the top, where the arms become free, these lateral rows of small plates change into jointed Pinn. Upper Cretaceous of Kansas and Westphalia.

Fig. 261. *Uintacrinus Westfalicus* Schlüt. From the Upper Cretaceous of Recklinghausen (Westphalia). a from the side, b from below. (Nat. size.) After Schlüter.

#### 24. Family. Glyptocrinidae. Zitt.

*K. high, with dicyclic base, consisting of 5 (or 3) IB, 5 PB, 5 × 3–4 R, 1–3 zones of Rad. dist. and a varying number of IR. The IR¹ stand between R¹ and R². A. uni- or biserial, with fine Pinn. Calyx cover plated with numerous small platelets.*

**a) With 5 Infrabasalia.**

**Glyptocrinus** Hall (*Icosidactylocrinus* Owen, *Trochocrinus* Portlock) (Fig. 262). K. cup-shaped, with 5 ribs running in the direction of the R, dichotomous towards the top, and raised radial ornamentation on the small plates. IB 5, very small, occasionally scarcely developed. PB 5, of equal size. R 5 × 3, the upper axillary. The lowest large IR inserted between R¹ and ², above them several (usually 3) vertical rows of smaller IR. Calyx cover with many small small plates and excentric anal opening. A. 5 × 2 × 2, uniserial, with fine **[p. 375]** but long Pinn. St. round or pentagonal. Nutrient canal pentagonal, joints alternately thicker and thinner. Articular facets with five-leaved pattern. Lower Silurian. Canada, Ohio, New York, Wales etc. Upper Silurian. Wisconsin.

**Glyptaster** Hall. Like the preceding, but IB distinctly developed. Anal interradius broader than the others. A. biserial. Upper Silurian.

The Devonian genus *Clonocrinus* Oehlert (non Ang. non Quenst.) differs only by the alleged absence (probably weak development) of the IB.

**Thylacocrinus** Oehlert. K. wide, globular, rather large. Base dicyclic. IB 5, small, pentagonal. B 5, hexagonal. R 5 × 3, between them an IRA¹. R² larger than R¹; R³ axillary. Dist. 2 each. A. 24, very long, biserial, undivided. Devonian.

**? Cupulocrinus** d'Orb. (*Scyphocrinus* Hall non Zenk.) B 5, five-sided. R 5 × 4, the upper axillary. R¹ unequal, 4 seven-sided, the 5th eight-sided; R² and ³ low, four-sided. IR 5 × (2/1), the lowest between R² and ³. St. round. Lower Silurian.

Fig. 262. *Glyptocrinus decadactylus* Hall. Lower Silurian. (Trenton Group.) Cincinnati. Ohio. Nat. size.

**Lampterocrinus** Roem. (*Balanocrinus* Troost). K. pear-shaped. IB 5, wedge-shaped. PB 5, five-sided, pointed, with the exception of that of the anal interradius. R 5 × 4, six-sided, gradually decreasing in size towards the top. R⁴ small, roundish, notched, with arm opening. IR 4 × {3/2/1}, the IR¹ between R². IRA¹ between R¹, above it 6 smaller small plates. Calyx cover highly vaulted, covered with small small plates. Anus central, tubular. St. pentagonal. Upper Silurian. Tennessee.

**Eucrinus** Ang. K. bowl-shaped. Base dicyclic. IB 5, small, irregularly five-sided. PB 5, short, six-sided, thickened at the base. R¹ broad, six-sided, between them the narrower lowest IRA¹. R² transverse, broad; R³ axillary. Dist. 2 each, IR and IRA in varying number. A. 5 × 2, simple, biserial. Pinn. very long. Upper Silurian. Gotland.

**b) with 3 Infrabasalia.**

**Sagenocrinus** Austin (*Actinocrinus* p. p. Phill.). Base dicyclic. IB 3, unequal, small, forming a pentagon. PB 5, unequally pointed, between them the lowest, somewhat narrower anal interradial. R 5 × 3. R¹ broad, heptagonal, between them a broad IRA². Dist. 4 each. IR numerous. A. 10 × 2, uniserial, several times forked. Pinn. short. Upper Silurian. *S. (Actinocrinus) expansus* Phill. Dudley in Wales and Gotland.

#### 25. Family. Rhodocrinidae. F. Roem. (emend. Beyrich) (1).

*K. bowl-shaped or globular, with dicyclic base, composed of 5 IB, 5 PB, 5 × 3 R, 1–3 zones of Rad. dist. and a large number of IR.* **[p. 376]** *The lower IR form with the R¹ a ring of 10 alternating small plates.*

---
(1) According to verbal communications from Herr Prof. Beyrich.

**a) Calyx equilateral (regular).**

**Ollacrinus** Cumberl. (*Gilbertsocrinus* Phill.). K. globular or bowl-shaped. Base deeply sunken. IB 5, small. PB six-sided, truncated above. R¹ seven-sided, R² six-sided, R³ axillary, above it 2 Rad. dist. each, between them an Interdist. All interradii completely alike, formed of 7–14 small plates. IR¹ five-sided, axillary, between R¹; above it several IR² and ³. Calyx cover plated, weakly vaulted, with excentric anal opening, at the margin with 5 tubular, hollow, mostly simply forked, horizontal or downward-bent prolongations, which are plated all round and stand above the interradial fields. Arms (5 × 4) very thin, biserial, mostly hanging down and bent over, but occasionally also erect, resting on the inner face of the uppermost axillary Rad. dist. Between and somewhat above them the arm-shaped prolongations of the calyx come forth. Carboniferous limestone. England and North America.

As *Goniasteroidocrinus* (= *Trematocrinus* Hall) American forms from the Carboniferous limestone and Devonian were separated from *Ollacrinus* by Lyon and Casseday, in which the arm-like processes at the upper margin of the calyx possess a considerable length and fork into 2 long prongs. According to Wachsmuth, however, these are connected with *Ollacrinus* by imperceptible transitions. The same author has observed in the interior an axial canal (stomach tube) surrounded by firm walls, which runs spirally downwards, narrows and, in the neighbourhood of the base, turns upwards again.

**b) Calyx inequilateral (irregular). Anal interradius different from the others.**

**Rhodocrinus** Mill. K. bowl-shaped or globular. Small plates often provided with radial sculpture. Base flat or sunken. IB 5, small, pointed. PB of equal size, truncated towards the top, six-sided. R¹ seven-sided, R² six-sided, R³ axillary. Between R¹ lie large six-sided IR¹ and above these 5–9 smaller IR in 2–3 vertical rows. Rad. dist. 2 each, the upper with a half-moon-shaped articular notch, between them 1 Interdist. each. Calyx cover finely plated. Anus excentric, approaching the margin. Arms 5 × 2, robust, uniserial, mostly several times forked. St. round, nutrient canal five-lobed. Silurian. Devonian. Carboniferous limestone.

Typical species: *Rhod. verus* Mill. (Carboniferous limestone); *Rhod. gonatodes* Müll. Devonian.

**Acanthocrinus** F. A. Roem. (emend. Joh. Müller and Hall). K. bowl- or top-shaped. IB five-sided, well developed; PB large, truncated above, with a thick spiny or knobby process. Rad. dist. 3; in the interradii numerous (c. 15 and more) small plates. A. connected at their base by Interbrachialia, later becoming free, biserial, several times forked, occasionally hanging down. Devonian. *A. longispina* F. A. Roem. Lower Devonian. Clausthal, Coblenz. *A. nodulosus* Hall. North America.

**Rhipidocrinus** Beyrich (*Rhodocrinus* p. p. Goldf. et auct.) (Fig. 263). K. broad, bowl-shaped; small plates mostly provided with sculptures. Base flat or somewhat sunken.

**[p. 377]**

IB very small, intimately united, forming a pentagon above the stem. PB six-sided, truncated above. R1 five-sided, between them 4 large, seven-sided IR1 and one eight-sided IRA1, upon which follow several IR and IRA standing in 3 zones. Rad. dist. 2, the upper one axillary, between them one or more Interdist. A. 5 × 2, resting on the inner surface of the axillary Dist. (the outer surface bears the first lateral branch), fan-shaped, broad, built up of low, very broad, uniserial joints, beset on both sides with densely crowded, strong, biserial side branches, which bear Pinn. Calyx cover finely plated, weakly vaulted, anus excentric. St. thick, round, with low joints. Alimentary canal five-lobed. Devonian. *Rh. crenatus* Goldf. sp. Eifel.

Fig. 263. *Rhipidocrinus crenatus* Goldf. sp. Devonian. Gerolstein. Eifel. a complete specimen with arms (natural size, after Schultze). b calyx, seen from above, with lateral anal opening. c base from within, showing the 5 Infrabasalia, 2 Parabasalia and a lower Radiale. d stem from the side. e articular surface of a stem joint.

? ***Hadrocrinus*** Lyon. Devonian. Ohio (Trans. Am. Philos. Soc. vol. 13).

? ***Trybliocrinus*** Gein. Carboniferous limestone. Asturias (Neues Jahrb. 1867 S. 284 t. 3).

***Thysanocrinus*** Hall. K. top-shaped or pear-shaped, composed as in *Rhodocrinus*; of the PB, however, 4 are pointed above and only the anal interradius is truncated. Radii frequently distinguished by a rounded keel, which forks on R3 and continues into the Dist. A. 5 × 2 very thin, several times forked, biserial. Lower and Upper Silurian. Devonian. *Th. pyriformis* Bill. Silurian; *Th. quinquelobus* Schultze. (Devonian.)

**E. Calyx regular; calyx cover drawn out flask-shaped, narrowed above, with central anal opening, formed of large, regularly arranged polygonal plates. Arms** **[p. 378]** **20, biserial, lying between rib-like projections of the upper calyx margin or in special niches, not rising above the calyx cover.**

### 26. Family. Calyptocrinidae. Ang.

*K. regularly radially built, with 4 B, 5 × 3 R, 5 × 1 Rad. dist. and 5 × 3 IR. All interradii alike. Calyx cover flask-shaped, elongated, with central anal opening, laterally covered with regularly arranged plates. The 20 biserial arms are never longer than the summit of the calyx and lie either in pairs between rib-like projections of the upper calyx margin or in special niches, which are formed by walls standing vertically on the calyx cover.*

***Callicrinus*** Ang. (*Calliocrinus* d'Orb.) (Fig. 264). K. cup-shaped, small plates ornamented with ribs or spines, base deeply excavated. B 4 somewhat unequal, small, wedge-shaped, visible only from below, forming a cone directed obliquely inward and upward, whose truncated end is pierced by a five-cornered hole. R1 large, the lower half bent over and forming the greater part of the hollow cone of the calyx base. R2 narrow, four-sided; R3 broad, axillary. IR 5 × (2/1), of which the lower ones are large, between R2 and 3, the two upper ones high, hatchet-shaped, joined by a vertical suture. Dist. 2 each (20 in all), the upper ones axillary. Interdist. 1 each, narrow and high. A. 10 × 2 simple, biserial, Pinn. very long. Calyx cover extended into a flask-shaped, plated neck provided at its end with a central opening. St. round, alimentary canal pentagonal. Upper Silurian. Gotland.

Fig. 264. *Callicrinus costatus* His. sp. Upper Silurian. Gotland. a calyx with arms. b calyx with well-preserved summit, without arms. c base from within. d base from below. (Natural size, after Angelin.)

? ***Lyriocrinus*** Hall. K. bowl-shaped. B 5 (?), R 5 × 4, R4 axillary, above them 2 Rad. dist. each, between which one Interdist. IR 5 × (2/1), above these 2 small IBr. A. 5 × 2 undivided, biserial, equally spaced. St. round, articular surfaces of the joints radiately striated. Central canal round. Upper Silurian. North America (Lockport).

**[p. 379]**

***Eucalyptocrinus*** Goldf. (Fig. 265). K. bowl-shaped, with deeply excavated base. B 4 small, forming the apex of the hollow cone in the basal depression, pierced by a five-lobed hole. R1 large, their lower half bent over and belonging to the basal funnel. R2 four-sided, sometimes absent; R3 axillary. IR 5 × (2/1), constituted as in *Callicrinus*. Dist. 2 each; the lower ones large, five-sided or trapezoidal, the upper ones sometimes distinctly composed of 3 pieces. Interdist. 1 each, following on R3. A. 10 × 2 simple, biserial, uniserial at the base. Pinn. long. Calyx cover highly vaulted, drawn out into a thin, plated tube; on the Interradialia and Interdistichalia rise 10 wing-like calcareous leaves, which form 10 niches, in each of which lie 2 laterally fused arms. The vertical niche walls are frequently twice as high as the calyx; they consist of two unequal principal pieces, which thicken toward the outside and attach themselves with their thin inner margin to the central summit tube or to the vaulted calyx cover; the upper vertical piece is expanded and thickened at the outer and upper margin and ends at the same height as the central tube, whose flat summit, provided with a quadrangular **[p. 380]** opening, is formed of 6–8 plates. The central opening itself appears sometimes protected by 5 or more quite small, probably movable little plates. Upper Silurian. Gotland, England and Wales; North America (Niagara Group). Devonian. Eifel.

Fig. 265. *Eucalyptocrinus rosaceus* Goldf. Devonian. Gerolstein. Eifel. a complete specimen with arms. b ideal section of a calyx (*b* Basalia, *r¹* first Radiale, *γ* lower, *δ* upper niche piece). d calyx cover. e calyx without arms (*b* Basalia, *r¹ᵘ·²* 1st and 2nd Radiale, *ir* Interradialia, *dist.* Distichalia radialia, *int. dist.* Interdistichalia). After L. Schultze.

Fig. 266. *Hypanthocrinus regularis* His. Upper Silurian. Gotland. Complete calyx with arms. In one niche the arms have been removed in order to show the inner plated calyx cover.

***Hypanthocrinus*** Phill. (Fig. 266). Essentially like the preceding genus, but the base not funnel-shaped and depressed; B 4 six-sided. Moreover the plated tube of the cover projects above the summit and the niche walls of the arms consist of only one vertical principal piece. Upper Silurian. Gotland, England and Wales; North America.

### Appendix to the Tesselaten.

1. Incompletely known genera of doubtful position.

*Reteocrinus* Billings. Silurian. *Closterocrinus* Hall. Silurian. *Cyrtidocrinus* Ang. Upper Silurian. *Asterocrinus* Münst. (*Zygocrinus* Bronn.). Devonian. *Mycocrinus* Schultze. Devonian. *Tribrachiocrinus* Austin. *Tetramerocrinus* Austin. Silurian. *Acrocrinus* Yandell. Carboniferous limestone. *Sycocrinus* Austin. Carboniferous limestone.

2. Insufficiently characterised genera, founded on parts of the calyx, arms or stem joints.

a) On parts of the calyx.

*Calyx* Rouault. Silurian. *Condylocrinus* Eichw. Silurian. *Proteuryale* Roem. Calyx cover, perhaps of *Rhodocrinus*. Devonian. *Aspidocrinus* Hall. Silurian.

b) On arm pieces.

*Syringocrinus* Billings. Silurian. *Brachyocrinus* Hall. Silurian.

c) On stem pieces or stem joints.

*Ancyrocrinus* Hall. Devonian. *Cysticrinus* Roem., *Sphenocrinus* Eichw., *Grammacrinus* Eichw., *Condylocrinus* Eichw., *Sphenocrinus* Eichw., *Cophinus* Koenig. With the exception of *Ancyrocrinus* all from Silurian deposits.

## 2. Suborder. Articulata. Joh. Müll.

**Calyx plates mostly very thick, joined by joint-like excavated and vaulted or flat sutural surfaces. IR very rarely present; base mostly monocyclic, consisting of 5 small plates. Calyx cover as a rule a membranous perisome, rarely plated, with open ambulacral grooves, central mouth opening and excentric anus. Oral plates present in the mouth angles or absent. In the interior of the calyx plates run canals for fibre bundles, arising from the dorsal organ, in a radial direction toward the arms, and continue in the dorsal part of the arm joints as far as the outermost arm tips.**

**[p. 381]**

The Articulaten according to the above definition comprise the divisions Articulata and Coadunata of J. S. Miller. To them belong all recent Eucrinoideen and, with the exception of the two genera *Marsupites* and *Uintacrinus*, all fossil ones from the Trias upward. They are in general easily recognised by their regularly radially built calyces with a simple base (only *Apiocrinus* sometimes possesses small Interradialia and only *Encrinus* a dicyclic base), as well as by their mostly thick calyx plates. The most constant character of the calyx, however, is furnished by five canals proceeding from the heart (probably also containing nerves and blood vessels) for the reception of fibre bundles, which traverse all Basalia and Radialia and continue as the dorsal canals of the arm joints. These canals are found also in the few Articulaten genera with thin calyx plates (*Plicatocrinus*, *Cotyloderma*), which in their external habit cannot be distinguished from the Tesselaten. Beyrich first discovered these canals in the fossil genera *Encrinus* and *Apiocrinus*; they were later demonstrated by Carpenter in *Comatula* and *Pentacrinus* and thereafter by Sars and Ludwig in *Rhizocrinus*. Their course is in the main the same in all genera. In the median line of the Basalia one observes a groove, partly open, partly covered, which forks upward into 2 branches. Each branch now enters through roundish openings into the lower margin of the R1 and traverses them as an internal canal. Each Radiale thus receives two such canals, which come from two basal plates. As a rule the two canals converge so strongly that they already meet in the middle of the first Radiale and run in a parallel direction close beside one another (Fig. 267). Sometimes, however, they also remain separated by a considerable interval (Fig. 268). On the upper articular surface of the R1 one always observes one larger common opening or two separate smaller openings of radial canals, which now in a parallel direction run through the lower simple arm joints (R2 and 3 auct.), diverge again in the first axillary Brachiale and enter as simple canals into the arms and their ramifications. In *Encrinus* each **[p. 382]** canal sends off in the first Axillare a lateral branch in a diagonal direction toward the other side, so that here all arm joints are pierced by 2 parallel canals (Fig. 268). In all Articulaten all the radial canals radiating from the base are brought into connection with one another in the first Radiale by a ring canal.

Fig. 267. Course of the inner fibre canals in the calyx and the arms of the genus *Apiocrinus*.

Fig. 268. Course of the inner fibre canals in the calyx and the arms of *Encrinus*. (After Beyrich.) (The canals of Figs. 267 and 268 are dotted where they run in the interior of the plates, but indicated by a black line where they can be observed superficially on the inner side of the calyx.)

The characters of the calyx cover are not usable for the fossil forms, since up to now only a single time has a well-preserved plated cover been observed on a Liassic *Pentacrinus*. Calcareous plates are indeed also very rarely entirely absent in the living Articulaten (only in some Comatuliden), but they are exceedingly thin, porous and so loosely fitted together that they easily fall apart and are destroyed.

In the development of the arms the Articulaten show great differences; in the dorsal calcareous body of the joints, however, the canals described above are never absent.

As among the Tesselaten, there are also among the Articulaten stalked and unstalked forms.

### 1. Family. Encrinidae. F. Roem.

*K. low, bowl-shaped, with dicyclic base. IB 5 very small, hidden beneath the uppermost stem joint. PB 5 large. R 5. Arms 5 × 2 or 5 × 4 strong, undivided, lying close beside one another, forming a pyramid, biserial or alternating. St. round.*

***Encrinus*** (Agricola, Lachmund, Lamarck) Miller (Fig. 269–271). (*Entrochos* and *Trochites* Agricola, *Encrinites* auct., *Chelocrinus*, *Dadocrinus*, ? *Calathocrinus* v. Mey., ? *Tetracrinites* Catullo, *Flabellocrinus* Klipst., ? *Cassianocrinus* Laube). The low, plate-shaped calyx (basin, *patina*) consists of a dicyclic base and 5 R; these 15 small plates are firmly joined to one another by syzygial sutures. IB 5 very small, pointed, hidden beneath the stem, forming a five-rayed star. PB 5 five-sided, pointed above, mostly visible only from below. R 5 broad, five-sided, thick; upper articular surface straight, truncated, with a straight transverse ridge, which connects the two lateral corners with one another and outside of which there is a slightly depressed furrow. Above the R follow 2 simple arm joints. The **[p. 383]** lower ones (R2 auct.) are joined to R1 by an articular surface, to the axillary Br2 (R3 auct.) by smooth syzygial sutures, striated only at the outer margin. The two roof-shaped upper surfaces of the Br2 are again provided with articular ridges.

Fig. 269. *Encrinus liliiformis* Mill. Muschelkalk. Braunschweig.

Fig. 270. Analysis of the calyx and the arms of *Encrinus*. *a* and *a¹* calyx (*patina*) from within and from below. *b* Parabasale from within, somewhat worn. *r* Radiale 1 from within. *br* first arm joint (R2 auct.) from below (articular surface), *br¹* the same from above (syzygial suture). *br¹·²* Brachiale 1 and 2 fused with one another, from below and from within. *br²* axillary articular surface of Brachiale 2. *β* uniserial, *β\** biserial arm joint with double canal. *p* a pinnule joint (enlarged).

R1 shows on the inner side a median notch, probably for the passage of the ambulacral vessel; beside it there are fringed, lip-shaped processes with rough surface for the reception of muscle bundles. In Br1 the ambulacral hole pierces the inner margin of the plate and the muscle attachments are weaker. In Br2 there are beside the median ridge 2 slight incisions or 2 closed openings, which unite beneath the muscle lobes. Upon the axillary Br2 follow distichal series of 3–4 uniserial or alternating arm joints, which further up mostly become completely biserial. The 10 simple, rarely repeatedly divided arms are beset on both sides with strong, jointed Pinn. Malformations in the development of the arms (8, 16, 20 etc.) occur frequently.

St. round, long, consisting in the lower part of high, fairly equal joints, in the upper part mostly of alternating higher and lower joints of various thickness, or also of equal joints which gradually become ever lower. Articular surfaces either entirely or only at the margin coarsely radiately striated, with central, round or five-parted alimentary canal, from which sometimes 5 spoke-shaped, striated radii proceed.

W. thickened and irregularly spread out; frequently the lower end of the stem also appears dome-shaped.

**[p. 384]**

The course of the canals in the interior of the calyx plates and lower arm plates is shown in Fig. 271. All distichal arm joints are traversed by 2 canals running close beside one another, which take their origin at the two openings on each articular surface of the axillary Br2 and pierce the arm joints in a straight direction. When distichy is fully developed, the 2 holes lie on the inner narrow bevelled surfaces with which the alternating arm joints touch one another.

Fig. 271. Course of the inner canals in the calyx and the first arm joints of *Encrinus*.

The stem joints of this genus, very widespread in the Trias, sometimes form considerable rock beds (Encriniten or Trochiten limestones); the crowns are found most frequently in the Muschelkalk of Braunschweig.

The genus *Chelocrinus* was established by H. v. Meyer for forms with repeatedly split (25-, 50-fingered) arms, in which the stem is moreover provided with whorled cirri. *Dadocrinus* Meyer comprises the Encriniten with uniserial arms (*E. gracilis* v. Buch); *Calathocrinus* Meyer is based on a single, poorly preserved, juvenile calyx.

? ***Porocrinus*** Dittmar (non Billings). Thick, round, porous stem joints from the Alpine Keuper of Hallstadt.

### 2. Family. Eugeniacrinidae. Zitt.

(Coadunata Mill.)

*K. consisting only of 5 thick, laterally intimately united R. B entirely rudimentary, probably enclosed in the upper part of the Radialia. Above R 2 simple Br, of which the upper one is axillary. Arms 5 × 2 uniserial, thick and undivided. St. round, with massive, spreading root.*

***Eugeniacrinus*** Mill. (*Symphytocrinus* p. p. Koenig) (Fig. 272. 273). K. small, pear-shaped, bowl-shaped, frequently resembling a clove, formed solely of 5 (rarely 4) large, intimately fused radial joints, which rest directly on the uppermost stem joint. Body cavity shallow, closed at the bottom, with 5 radial furrows running from the centre to the middle of the upper margin of the R. Articular surfaces of the R straight, deeply notched, divided by a median ridge into 2 halves, with a transverse ridge, in the middle of which the inner canal opens. Between the projecting lateral corners of the articular surfaces the first low arm joint (R2 auct.) lies wedged in, and upon this the axillary 2nd arm joint (R3 auct.). The 10 short, simple, uniserial arms, curved inward above, which are extremely rarely preserved, consist of thick four-edged joints with a deep ventral furrow and are beset with fine Pinn. As a rule they stand directly on the oblique articular surfaces of the 2nd axillary arm joint (R3 auct.); in *Eug. caryophyllatus*, on the other hand (Fig. 272), they emerge **[p. 385]** laterally from small articular surfaces and the middle piece of the axillary arm joint is prolonged into a large triangular process, which arches over the calyx cover (Fig. 273e). These peculiarly shaped pieces, always occurring isolated, were described by Goldfuss under the name *Pentacrinus paradoxus*.

Fig. 272. *Eugeniacrinus caryophyllatus* Mill. From the Upper Jurassic, restored, without arms. (After Fraas.)

Fig. 273. *Eugeniacrinus caryophyllatus* Mill. Upper Jurassic. Streitberg. Franconia. a calyx with the uppermost stem joint (Centrodorsale), nat. size. b calyx from above; c the same from below, enlarged. d lowest arm joint (R2 auct.) from within, d\* the same from above. e second arm joint from within (nat. size). f–h *E. nutans* Goldf., from the same locality. f the 2 first arm joints fused with one another, from outside, f\* from within. g an arm joint from 4 sides. h coiled-up arm from the back and from the side.

The short stem is formed of a few long, cylindrical or barrel-shaped joints with a fine, round central canal and granulated or coarsely striated articular surfaces, smooth in the middle. The lowest stem joint is thickened into a solid root and attached.

The central canal of the stem enters the massive calyx formed of the fused R, widens upward quite gradually and sends out, at a short distance beneath the floor of the body cavity, 5 interradial canals, which very soon fork into 2 branches, which enter the R, unite there with the converging branch of a neighbouring basal canal and then run close beside one another through the middle of the R to the upper articular surface. At the point of union of the fork branches there is a ring canal, which connects all the radial canals. The course of these canals proves that originally (perhaps in early youth?) Basalia were present and that their rudiments are perhaps still enclosed by the upper part of the R.

As a rule stem joints and crowns are found separately. The latter consist almost exclusively only of the 5 fused radial plates. Very rarely the ridge-shaped lowest arm joints still lie wedged between the lateral projections of the upper articular surfaces. Complete calyces with the first and second Brachialia (R2 and R3 auct.) and attached arms have up to now never been found.

**[p. 386]**

The genus is frequent in Upper Jurassic deposits, especially in South Germany and Switzerland; rarer in the Dogger and in the Lower Cretaceous.

***Tetracrinus*** Münst. K. composed of 4 low radial joints, in the main built like *Eugeniacrinus*. St. consisting of unequal, spherical, disc-shaped to elliptical joints. Rare in the Upper Jurassic.

***Phyllocrinus*** d'Orb. K. small, spherical, elliptical or five-edged cup-shaped, composed of 5 very thick, somewhat vaulted, closely united radial pieces. Base deeply excavated. B absent or at least not visible. Body cavity narrow, with 5 narrow ambulacral furrows leading to the articular surface of the R, which form a five-rayed star. The articular surfaces of the R lie in a very deep and narrow incision at the outer and upper end of the ambulacral passages and are bounded laterally by high, prong-shaped processes. Through the union of the processes of each 2 neighbouring R, 5 three-sided pyramids, truncated above, arise between the articular surfaces, whose edge is directed inward. The articular surfaces are provided with a transverse ridge, in the middle of which the central canal of the R opens; within the ridge there are 2, outside it 1 pit for the reception of muscles. Course of the inner canals in the calyx as in *Eugeniacrinus*. A. unknown; St. round, probably short and provided with a thickened root. Upper Jurassic and Lower Cretaceous, distributed chiefly in the Alpine region.

The genera *Hemicrinus* and *Leiocrinus* d'Orb. from the Cretaceous are to be suppressed as insufficiently founded.

### 3. Family. Holopidae. F. Roem. (emend. Zitt.).

*K. unstalked, attached by a broad base. B (frequently also the R) fused into an undivided cup- or bowl-shaped calyx. A. thick, alternating, simple.*

***Cotylederma*** Quenst. (emend. Deslongch.) (Fig. 274). Unstalked, attached by a broad base. K. thin-walled, cup- or bowl-shaped, closed below by a thin, horizontal plate provided with 5 weak radial edges (? Centrodorsale). On this rises the cylindrical calyx, somewhat widened above, whose upper margin is notched by 5 crescent-shaped, smooth surfaces (perhaps 5 fused B); on the latter rest 5 low plates (R), with straight, broad articular surfaces above, in the middle of which the openings of central canals are to be seen. A. unknown. Rare in the Lias of Normandy and South Germany.

Fig. 274. *Cotylederma docens* E. Deslongch. From the Upper Lias of May. Calvados. a calyx from above, b from below (nat. size). c, d *C. lineati* Quenst. Lias δ. Asselfingen. Baden. c centrodorsal plate. d lower fused ring of small plates (Basalia). Natural size.

**[p. 387]**

***Cyathidium*** Steenstrup. Unstalked, attached by a broad base. K. undivided cup-shaped, round or five-edged, widened above, thin-walled. Upper margin with 5 crescent-shaped articular surfaces, in the middle of which lies the opening of a fine central canal. All parts of the calyx are most intimately fused; no sutures are visible, but one may nevertheless regard the upper half as composed of 5 R, the lower of 5 fused B. A. unknown. Rare in the uppermost Cretaceous of Faxoe (*C. holopus* Steenstr.) and in the Eocene (*Cyath. Spileccense* Schlüt.) at Monte Spilecco near Verona.

***Holopus*** d'Orb. (emend. Pourtalès). Most probably identical with *Cyathidium*. K. thin-walled, cup-shaped, undivided, attached by a broad base. On the 5 articular surfaces of the upper margin rest 5 axillary first arm joints, which bear 10 short, thick, simple, coiled-up alternating arms. Pinn. strong. Mouth surrounded by 5 large triangular oral plates. Living in deep water near Barbados.

? ***Cothocrinus*** Philippi. K. cup-shaped, undivided, unstalked. A. widely separated. In Chile. Formation and precise locality unknown.

### 4. Family. Plicatocrinidae. Zitt.

*K. consisting of thin small plates. B absent. R 5, 6, 7 and 8 × 2. Calyx cover plated, with 5 large oral plates. Arms long, forked, uniserial. St. round.*

***Plicatocrinus*** Münst. (Fig. 275). K. cup-shaped to funnel-shaped with wide and deep body cavity, consisting of 2 rows of high and thin radial plates, which are distinguished by a fold-like median rib. The lower ring of small plates forms a solid basin composed of 5 to 8 firmly fused pieces; on the slightly notched upper syzygial surfaces of these stands a second ring of trapezoidal radial plates of the second order with crescent-shaped upper articular surfaces for the insertion of the arms. All the plates are traversed by a fine radial central canal. In the somewhat excavated base of the calyx lie, hidden beneath the uppermost stem joint, 5–8 quite rudimentary, ridge-shaped Basalia, which do not touch one another laterally. Calyx cover, arms and St. unknown. Rare in the Lias and White Jurassic.

Most probably the living genus ***Hyocrinus*** W. Th. (Fig. 276), which was brought up in the Atlantic Ocean from a depth of 1850 fathoms, is identical with *Plicatocrinus*. Wyville Thomson, it is true, regards the lower, fused ring of small plates as a basal ring and believes it to be composed of only 2–3 pieces, "but this is difficult to decide with certainty, since the pieces are more or less fused and the connecting sutures in the full-grown animal are somewhat unclear" (1). *Hyocrinus* possesses a very long stem and 5 long arms, forked into numerous lateral branches and covered with strong **[p. 388]** side plates. The calyx cover is paved in the peripheral part with small calcareous plates and provided in the middle with 5 large triangular, somewhat raised oral plates, between which the ambulacral vessels run to the central mouth. The anus lies interradially near the outer margin on a short plated tube.

---
(1) Journal Linnean Society. London. Zoology 1876. vol. XIII p. 47.

Fig. 275. *Plicatocrinus hexagonus* Münst. Upper Jurassic. Streitberg. Franconia. a complete calyx with both rows of radial plates. b lower ring of small plates, seen from the base; c the same from the side (slightly enlarged). d a second Radiale from within, e from outside, f from below.

Fig. 276. *Hyocrinus Bethellianus* Wyv. Thomson. From the Atlantic Ocean. a specimen at twice natural size. b calyx cover, strongly enlarged. *am* ambulacral furrows of the arms, *c* canal of the arm joints, *an* anus, *pst* mouth, *o* oral plates. (After Wyville Thomson.)

### 5. Family. Apiocrinidae. d'Orb.

*K. regular, formed of very thick plates joined by articulation, passing gradually into the mostly thickened upper part of the stem. B 5, R 5 × 1–3. Deviations from the number five frequent. Now and then IR present. Arms strong, uniserial, moderately forked. St. long.*

***Apiocrinus*** Miller (*Guettardocrinus* d'Orb.) (Fig. 277–279). K. pear-shaped, borne by a very long stem, whose low joints become ever broader upward and pass gradually into the calyx. On the uppermost stem joint rests a broad centrodorsal plate with 5 raised radial edges, from which the lateral surfaces fall away roof-like (Fünfrippenglied [five-ribbed joint], Quenstedt). This appears, as follows from the indication of sutures, **[p. 389]** to have arisen from 5 originally separate pieces and probably corresponds to the 5 small infrabasal plates in *Encrinus*.

Fig. 277. *Apiocrinus Parkinsoni* Schloth. From the Great Oolite of Ranville. Calvados. a calyx with the uppermost stem joints from the side, b from above. c articular surface of a stem joint (natural size).

Fig. 278. *Apiocrinus Parkinsoni* Schloth. Ranville. a analysis of the calyx and course of the canals (the canals marked with dotted lines run in the interior of the plates, the unbroken lines represent the fork canals visible on the inner side of the Basalia). b upper part of the stem with centrodorsal plate, cut through in the middle in order to show the interspaces between the joints. *b* basal plate from above and within, *b\** the same from below. *r¹* first Radiale from outside, *r¹\** the same from within. *r²* second Radiale from outside, *r²\** the same from within (the canals of the radial plates are visible only on ground or strongly worn plates, like those figured). *br* arm joints.

Fig. 279. *Apiocrinus Roissyanus* d'Orb. From the Coral Rag of Tonnerre (Yonne). Restored after d'Orbigny.

**[p. 390]**

Above follow alternately 5 interradial, wedge-shaped, thick, axillary B, between which rest 5 low R1, notched crescent-wise above. On these stands a ring of low R2 and upon that the axillary R3. All calyx plates are joined by radially striated syzygial surfaces. In individual species several IR are intercalated between R2 and R3, upon which follow upward some smaller IBr. On R3 follow the lowest simple Br, of which Br1 and 2 are joined by syzygial sutures; the axillary Br (mostly Br2) bears once or twice forked, uniserial arms beset on both sides with strong Pinn. Between Br1 and 2 there are sometimes 2 or more IBr.

The stem is cylindrical, smooth, without cirri, composed of thick joints, which become ever lower upward. The articular surfaces are provided with more or less fine striae radiating from the central alimentary canal to the margin. The root is thickened into a knob and frequently strengthened by lateral runners.

The interior of the calyx consists of two goblet-like spaces, situated one above the other, of very unequal size. The smaller lower one is enclosed by the basal plates and passes directly into the wide alimentary canal of the upper stem joints; the upper, wider one is bounded by the radial plates. Both the B and the R are obliquely truncated at their inner ends and somewhat thickened, on the upper side mostly also set off from the oblique inner surface by a raised transverse ridge.

In the median line of the oblique inner surface of the B runs, proceeding from the alimentary canal of the stem, a superficial canal, which forks into 2 diverging branches; of the latter, 2 each enter the lower margin of an R1, pierce these plates and unite in the middle of the upper margin, whence they continue close beside one another into R2 and pierce these also; in R3 they diverge again and appear on the upper articular surface as 2 more or less distant openings on the inner surface of the transverse ridges. From there they pass as simple canals through the arm joints. These radial canals are connected in R1 by a ring canal, whose course is evident from Fig. 278a.

A very characteristic peculiarity of the genus *Apiocrinus* consists in this, that the upper thickened stem joints and calyx plates, more rarely also all the stem joints, lie closely upon one another only at the periphery, but toward the interior leave interspaces of considerable width free between themselves, which in the living state were probably filled with intervertebral substance (Fig. 278b).

The genus *Apiocrinus* is restricted to the Jurassic and Lower Cretaceous formation. Crowns are found most frequently in the Dogger (Bath, England, Normandy) and Malm. The stem joints sometimes form, in the red Alpine Lias, crinoid marble of considerable thickness.

As *Guettardocrinus* d'Orbigny designated such Apiocriniten in which the 2 first Br are firmly fused laterally with one another and take part in the formation of the calyx.

**[p. 391]**

***Millericrinus*** d'Orb. (*Ceriocrinus*, *Pomatocrinus*, *Symphytocrinus* Koenig, *Pegmacrinus* Jourdan). K. pear-shaped or spherical, consisting of a larger or smaller, sometimes five-parted centrodorsal plate (IB?) provided with 5 radial ribs, 5 large B and 5 R with straight upper articular surfaces. IR absent. On R follow 2 simple Br, of which the upper is axillary. A. 10 uniserial, usually forked only once toward the top. Pinn. long. St. long, cylindrical, with strongly thickened root. Upper stem joints either becoming quite gradually broader and passing into the calyx, or sharply separated in form and size from the centrodorsal plate. Stem joints round or roundish five-edged, smooth or spiny outside; articular surfaces radiately striated, without cirri.

The canals of the calyx and arm joints have in general the same course as in *Apiocrinus*. They arise from the stem canal, fork on the inner surface of the B, then run through the R1, where the branches from two neighbouring B meet the ring canal at the upper articular surface. The former then traverse the arm joints. In individual species (*M. Milleri*) the canals in the calyx interior run on raised ridges. Lias. Jurassic. Lower Cretaceous.

***Acrochordocrinus*** Trautschold (*Cyclocrinus* d'Orb. non Eichw., *Mespilocrinus* d'Orb.). K. similar to *Millericrinus*, incompletely known. Stem joints disc- or barrel-shaped, round, granulated on the articular surfaces, sometimes striated at the outer margin. Jurassic. Cretaceous.

? ***Torynocrinus*** Seeley (*Koninckocrinus* Seeley). Cretaceous.

***Bourgueticrinus*** d'Orb. (? *Thiolliericrinus* Etallon) (Fig. 280). K. small, pear-shaped, composed of a very large, fairly high centrodorsal plate with 5 raised radial ribs, 5 B and 5 or 5 × 3 R. The uppermost stem joints thicken and pass gradually into the calyx. Body cavity exceedingly shallow and narrow. A. probably 10, simple, uniserial. Course of the inner canals as in *Millericrinus*. St. long, consisting of numerous joints, of which the upper show a cylindrical or barrel-shaped form, while the lower are frequently depressed in the middle and possess elliptical articular surfaces. Central canal fine. Articular surfaces of the stem joints not radiately striated, provided with a raised transverse ridge, whereby a certain flexibility of the stem is made possible. The transverse ridges of the upper and lower articular surfaces run mostly at right angles or at any rate at a greater or smaller angle to one another. From the lower part of the stem branch off jointed, root-like cirri, with which the Seelilien attached themselves to foreign bodies. Upper Jurassic. Cretaceous. Tertiary.

Fig. 280. *Bourgueticrinus ellipticus* Mill. White Chalk. Wiltshire. a calyx with centrodorsal plate and the 2 uppermost stem joints (nat. size). b upper side of the calyx, enlarged. c stem joints. d articular surface of a stem joint. e lateral cirrus.

***Conocrinus*** d'Orb. (*Rhizocrinus* Sars, *Bourgueticrinus* p. p. d'Orb.) (Fig. 281). K. small, pear-shaped, with exceedingly shallow body cavity, composed of 5 (often also 4, 6, very rarely 7) small R and 5 very high B, mostly completely **[p. 392]** fused into an undivided knob. The R also are closely united laterally and their sutures frequently scarcely perceptible. The body cavity is not prolonged directly into the stem, but is closed off by a calcareous rosette, which arises from the thickened and fused inner ends of the R. The 2 lowest arm joints (R2 and 3 according to Sars, Ludwig) are somewhat broader and stronger than those following above and are joined to one another by syzygial sutures. Br2 (R3 auct.) not axillary.

Arms 5 (frequently also 4 or 6) short, simple, uniserial, with very strong, jointed Pinn. The depressed tentacle furrows of the calyx cover, arms and Pinn. are covered with large, oval side plates standing in 2 alternating rows.

Calyx cover with 5 open ambulacral furrows, central mouth and lateral anus. The mouth angles filled with calcareous oral plates. Course of the inner canals as in *Apiocrinus*.

St. long, thin, with five-lobed, sometimes also two-parted central canal, not thickened below but beset with root-like, forked lateral cirri, which attach themselves to foreign bodies. The stem joints are elongated, smooth, the upper ones subcylindrical and smaller than the rest, which are depressed in the middle and swollen at both ends. The longer axes of the two articular surfaces provided with a transverse ridge always form an oblique angle with one another. Eocene, Miocene, Pliocene and living.

To the genus *Rhizocrinus*, living at a depth of 100–200 fathoms in the Atlantic Ocean, excellently described by Sars and Ludwig, distinguished by the fluctuating number of the R and arms, belong undoubtedly a series of fossil species of various form and size hitherto designated as *Bourgueticrinus* and *Conocrinus*. In *Eugeniacrinus pyriformis* Münst., *Conocrinus Thorenti* d'Arch. sp., *Conocrinus Seguenzai* Menegh. and others the calyx is pear-shaped or almost subcylindrical, in *C. cornutus* Schafh. sp. low bowl-shaped. According to the rules of priority, priority is due to the name *Conocrinus* d'Orb., even though d'Orbigny's generic diagnosis is incomplete and partly incorrect.

Sars regards the rosette in the centre of the calyx mentioned above as the homologue of the B, while H. Ludwig designates as B the skeletal pieces lying between the central radial furrows of the R (which moreover consist of 2 pieces each and certainly belong to the R). On a specimen of *Conocrinus pyriformis* (Fig. 281) I have, by grinding, clearly ascertained the sutures of the B in the solid basal knob reckoned by Sars and Ludwig to the stem; these **[p. 393]** are already externally visible in *Conocrinus Thorenti* d'Arch., and are just as distinctly recognisable in the living *Rhizocrinus Rawsoni* Pourtalès. The central canal of the stem penetrates the Basalia, widens somewhat at their upper end, but is covered by the rosette and stands in connection with the body cavity only through a fine tube. From the widened upper end (heart, dorsal organ) 5 (4 or 6) interradial canals (fibre bundles) run into the B, fork very quickly and enter the R 2 by 2. These meet already at a short distance from the rosette and are connected by a ring canal with the remaining radial canals. After each 2 canals lying close beside one another have traversed the R, they enter the arm joints. The schematic representation by H. Ludwig (Zur Anatomie des *Rhizocr. Lofotensis* Fig. 18) is thus, according to my observations, not perfectly correct. The course of the inner canals speaks decidedly against Sars' and Ludwig's interpretation of the basal knob. For in all Crinoiden the fibre bundles pass from the dorsal organ directly into the B (never into the uppermost stem joint), run mostly superficially on the inner margin of these and fork before they reach the R.

Fig. 281. *Conocrinus* (*Rhizocrinus*) *pyriformis* Goldf. sp. Eocene. Neighbourhood of Verona. a calyx from the side (nat. size); b the same enlarged; c from above with 3 attached first arm joints. d second specimen, cut through in the middle (nat. size), e the same slightly ground at the surface, in order to show the sutures of the Radialia and Basalia. f five-rayed calyx, from above (enlarged). g–h stem joints (nat. size).

***Bathycrinus*** Wyv. Thoms. Living in the Atlantic Ocean at ca. 1850 fathoms depth.

### 6. Family. Pentacrinidae.

*K. small, bowl-shaped, consisting of 5 B and 5 R, above them 2–3 simple Br. Arms uncommonly strongly developed, many times forked, widely spread. St. long, five-edged, mostly beset with side cirri. Articular surfaces of the stem joints with five-leaved pattern.*

***Pentacrinus*** Miller. K. small, sometimes entirely hidden beneath cirri; arms uncommonly developed, very long and many times forked; St. five-edged, very long, frequently beset with whorled cirri. The body cavity is enclosed by the calyx proper and the two lowest arm joints. The former consists of 5 B of very varied development, which sometimes lie as small separate plates on the uppermost stem joint and do not come into view laterally, but sometimes meet and form the lateral and lower part of the calyx. In individual species there occur moreover 5 IB, which correspond to the centrodorsal plate in *Apiocrinus* and *Millericrinus*. The 5 R are triangular, with straight articular surface above, in individual species ornamented with a downward-directed spur-shaped prolongation, which conceals the B and the uppermost part of the stem. The two first Br do not touch one another laterally and are frequently joined by a syzygial suture, sometimes also by an articular suture. Br2 axillary. In the perisome of the calyx cover lie numerous small calcareous plates, which however only exceptionally (*P. Briareus* Mill.) unite into a solid cover capable of preservation. From the central mouth (without oral plates) radiate 5 open ambulacral passages, which fork toward the periphery and run into the 10 arms. The anus lies interradially on a cone-shaped tube.

**[p. 394]**

Arms uniserial, rarely simple, mostly with numerous (sometimes more than 20) side arms (fingers) beset with Pinn. Between each 2 axillary arm joints there always lies an even number of simple Br. The long jointed Pinn. stand alternately on the right and left side, so that always one Pinn. stands at the distal end of a Br. on the right, the next at the distal end of the next Br. on the left.

Fig. 282. *Pentacrinus Briaroides* Quenst. From the Upper Lias (after Goldfuss). a stem joints of *P. subangularis* Mill. from the Upper, b of *P. basaltiformis* Mill. from the Middle Lias.

The five-edged, more rarely cylindrical stem reaches in many species a length of several feet and is always beset at greater or smaller intervals with whorled lateral cirri, which draw closer together toward the top. A thickened root was never observed; probably the animals stick in the mud or attach themselves with the lower end of the stem or **[p. 395]** with the cirri to foreign bodies. In the Munich palaeontological museum there is a slab of Lias shale from Boll with a piece of carbonised wood, from which numerous *Pentacrinus* stems proceed.

On the articular surfaces of the low stem joints there appears a star-shaped figure of 5 leaves, which meet in the middle, where the central canal passes through. These 5 smooth leaves are bounded on both sides by a row of short transverse ridgelets, which are separated by small pits. The projecting ridgelets of one joint fit into the corresponding pits of the following joint, so that thereby the connection of the stem pieces becomes a very solid one. The lower articular surface of a joint beset with cirri differs from the others by an almost smooth syzygial suture. The lateral cirri (auxiliary arms, cirri) are of various length, round, composed of cylindrical joints and traversed by a central canal.

Of *Pentacrinus* 4 living species, mostly occurring at great depth, and numerous fossil species have been described up to now. The latter are distributed over the Trias, Jurassic, Cretaceous and Tertiary formation and are most frequent in the Lias, Dogger and Malm. Crowns with arms occur frequently, chiefly in the Lias of Württemberg and England; mostly the stem joints are found isolated, and indeed sometimes in such masses that they form crinoid limestones.

Attempts have been made to divide the genus *Pentacrinus* into several subgenera, which however have not found general recognition. *Cladocrinus* (non *Cladocrinus* Austin) Agassiz called the forms with widely spaced whorls of cirri on the stem. In *Balanocrinus* d'Orb. the stem is not five-edged but roundish. In *Isocrinus* Meyer (non Phill.) the B are said to be absent, which according to Quenstedt rests on faulty observation. *Extracrinus* Austin contains Liassic forms with strongly forked arms, in which the first R. are provided with a downward-directed spur-shaped process; of these Fraas designated *P. subangularis* and others as *Heterocrinus*, because the side arms of these proceed only from one side of the main arm. In *Cainocrinus* Forbes (*Picteticrinus* Loriol) the B. are large, pentagonal and form a closed zone.

### 7. Family. Comatulidae.

*Stalked only in youth, later unstalked, free. K. consisting of a centrodorsal plate beset with cirri, 5 more or less atrophied B and 5 R, above them 2 Br. A. well developed, 5–20 and more, simple, not forked, alternating.*

***Antedon*** Fréminv. (1) (*Alecto* Leach, *Comatula* Lam., *Astrocoma* and *Phytocrinus* Blainv., *Comaturella* Münst., *Pterocoma* Ag., *Decacnemos* Bronn, *Decameros* Linck, *Hertha* Hagenow, *Allionia* Michelotti.)

In youth stalked and attached, in the mature state unstalked. K. in the stalked state composed of 5 B and 5 R; in the mature state the B **[p. 396]** atrophy or fuse into a small round plate (rosette), which closes off the centre of the body cavity. The stem is reduced to a massive, undivided, hemispherical and five-edged knob (centrodorsal piece) beset with numerous cirri and contains in its upper part the five-chambered dorsal organ (heart), beside which frequently 5 radial pits also lie. From the heart canals go directly into the cirri, whose openings are visible on the depressed attachment surfaces of the knob. On the latter rest directly the 5 R with straight upper articular surfaces; these are followed by the 2 lower free arm joints (R2 and 3 auct.), of which the upper is axillary. Arms 10, 20 or more, simple (rarely branched), uniserial or alternating, with long alternating Pinn. Calyx cover membranous or covered with thin small plates, mouth central, in youth with, in the mature state without oral plates. Course of the inner canals in the calyx similar to that in *Pentacrinus*. Living and fossil from the Jurassic formation onward.

---
(1) Cf. Schlüter, Ueber einige astyloide Crinoideen. Zeitschr. d. deutschen geolog. Ges. 1878 Bd. 30 S. 28.

Subgenera: a) ***Solanocrinus*** Goldf. (*Comaster* Goldf., *Comatulina* (male) d'Orb., *Glenotremites* Goldf.) (Fig. 283). Like *Antedon*, but the B ridge-shaped and externally visible between the centrodorsal knob and the R1. Dorsal **[p. 397]** organ (heart) round, without radial pits. Knob five-edged, bluntly conical to semicircular or disc-shaped. Jurassic, Cretaceous, Tertiary and living, everywhere fairly rare.

Fig. 283. a *Solanocrinus costatus* Goldf. Upper Jurassic (Diceras limestone) of Kelheim, Bavaria. Specimen with all the arms from the dorsal side. The basal knob and the pinnules are missing; somewhat reduced. b. c. d *S. scrobiculatus* Goldf., from the White Jurassic of Streitberg in Franconia. Calyx in natural size from above, below and the side. e an arm joint.

Further the recent forms:

b) ***Actinometra*** Müll., c) ***Phanogenia*** Lovén, d) ***Ophiocrinus*** Semp., d) ***Promachocrinus*** H. Carp.

## 3. Suborder. Costata. Joh. Müll.

**Unstalked. K. consisting of 5 thin, intimately united R and a minute B, externally ornamented with 10 radial ribs. Arms 5 × 2 thin, widely spaced, with fine lateral branches, coiled up at the ends; arm joints long, alternately provided with unjointed, spine-shaped processes. The whole calcareous skeleton is not dense, but of lattice-like, fairly wide-meshed constitution.**

This remarkable suborder contains only a single fossil genus (*Saccocoma*), which in its whole organisation, especially in the structure of the arms, departs widely from all other Eucrinoideen and was therefore placed by various authors in the neighbourhood of the genus *Euryale*. Nevertheless the whole structure shows after all greater analogy with the Crinoideen than with the Ophiuroideen. Of particular interest is the structure of the skeleton, which agrees with that of young Comatulen and other Echinodermen. One might accordingly take *Saccocoma* for a developmental stage of some other genus; but the Comatuliden, which alone occur in greater quantity together with *Saccocoma*, show, both in the mature and in their Pentacrinoiden stage, such totally different characters that any comparison is excluded.

***Saccocoma*** Ag. (*Stella* Parkinson, *Comatula* p. p. Goldf.) (Fig. 284). K. unstalked, small, pouch-shaped, somewhat constricted at the upper margin, consisting of 5 very thin, three-sided, vaulted radial plates fused with one another, which enclose a minute basal plate between them. In the middle of each R runs, in the direction of the arms, a raised rib; a further, somewhat weaker radial edge arises where two R are fused with one another. The 5 thin arms originate on a peg-shaped projection of the R and consist of elongated, many-edged joints somewhat depressed in the middle and thickened at the articulations; the second arm joint is axillary, so that the arms fork directly above the calyx into 10 equal, thin branches, mostly coiled up at the ends. In the middle of the ventral margin of the arm joints stands (probably alternately on the right or left side) an unjointed, spine-shaped process somewhat excavated in the middle, whose thickened lateral margins appear like isolated bristles when the connecting thin calcareous lamella is destroyed. Somewhat above half the length **[p. 398]** of the 10 main arms originate extremely fine, alternating, thread-like lateral branches with very long and thin joints. The calyx cover is formed by 5 large, triangular interradial plates, which leave free in the middle a small star-shaped mouth opening.

Fig. 284. *Saccocoma pectinata* Goldf., from the lithographic slate of Eichstädt, Franconia. a specimen in natural size. b calyx from the side, enlarged. c calyx from below, enlarged. d two lower arm joints, enlarged. e two middle arm joints with lateral branch, enlarged. f an arm with lateral branches, not coiled up, slightly enlarged. g lower arm joints of *Saccocoma tenella* Goldf., enlarged.

The whole calcareous skeleton does not consist of solid plates, but shows a fairly wide-meshed lattice structure, otherwise occurring among the Crinoideen only in *Comatula* larvae. The arm joints are the most solid, yet even these show, already at moderate magnification, a net-like structure.

This genus is found solely and exclusively, but exceedingly frequently, in the lithographic slate of the Upper Jurassic in Bavaria. The two forms figured by Goldfuss (Petrefacta Germaniae Taf. 62 Fig. 1–3), incorrectly in several respects (*Comatula pectinata* and *filiformis* Goldf.), belong to one and the same species.

Particularly noteworthy in *Comatula tenella* Goldf. are the processes of the arms, expanded near the calyx into broad leaves, which otherwise possess a spine-shaped form. Since all other characters of the skeleton agree completely with *Saccoma pectinata*, *Com. tenella* Goldf. possibly designates sexually mature specimens of the former species.

**[p. 399]**

## Spatial and temporal distribution of the Eucrinoideen.

Although the living Crinoideen represent only a vanishingly small remnant of this formerly uncommonly widespread and form-rich class of Echinodermen, yet a single family, the Comatulidae, seems still today to stand in the full vigour of its development of forms. The Challenger Expedition collected of these alone 111 species (1), which are distributed over the seas of the whole surface of the earth and live preferably at depths down to 200 fathoms, but in part were also brought up from 1000–2900 fathoms.

All other recent genera (*Pentacrinus*, *Conocrinus* [*Rhizocrinus*], *Bathycrinus*, *Hyocrinus* and *Holopus*) are deep-sea dwellers, of which the first two possess a wide geographical distribution in the Atlantic Ocean, while the rest have up to now occurred only sporadically. *Conocrinus* (*Rhizocrinus*) is found in Norway as far as the Lofoten, *Pentacrinus* on the other hand seems to prefer more southerly latitudes and has recently been discovered in great numbers on the coast of Cuba.

The Eucrinoideen belong to the oldest organisms known at all. They appear already in the oldest Silurian beds (Cambrian formation), and in the palaeolithic and mesolithic ages their remains, especially stem joints, become at times so massive that as crinoid limestones they play a not inconsiderable role in the building up of the strata of the earth. The occurrence of the Eucrinoideen is bound to no particular character of rock, and even though they appear most frequently in limestone, they are nevertheless not entirely absent from shaly, sandy and clayey rocks. They may, like their present-day relatives, have lived under the most varied external conditions of existence; particularly favourable to their thriving, however, seem to have been coral reefs.

In contrast to most other fossils of the oldest formations, the Crinoideen almost without exception possess narrowly limited spatial distribution. Just as today the great number of localised *Comatula* species surprises us, so already in the Silurian formation the species, frequently even the genera, show themselves restricted to particular localities or districts. A striking example in this direction is furnished by the Upper Silurian crinoid fauna of the island of Gotland, excellently illustrated by Angelin. Of the 176 species occurring there, which are distributed among 43 genera, only 10 have been found elsewhere, namely 9 in England and Wales, 1 in North America.

---
(1) Herb. Carpenter, Proceed. Royal Society 1879 p. 388.

**[p. 400]**

Of the 43 genera, 23 belong to the island of Gotland alone, 20 are also distributed elsewhere, namely 15 in Great Britain, the rest in North America. Similar relationships result from the comparison of the Devonian Eucrinoideen from the Eifel with the deposits of the same age in North America, or of the Carboniferous forms of the Old and New Worlds. Only very rarely does one and the same species belong to two parts of the world, or even merely to two very remote regions of one continent, and as a rule the genera too keep within restricted geographical areas. These phenomena may most simply be explained by the mode of life of the Seelilien, which makes free locomotion, and in any case wide migrations, impossible for these animals, by the isolation thereby entailed, and by adaptation to particular conditions of existence.

From the Primordial stage (Cambrian formation), stem joints are indeed known, but no reliably determinable remains of Eucrinoideen; on the other hand, from the Lower Silurian Llandeilo and Caradoc beds of Wales stem joints of various genera (*Cyathocrinus*, *Glyptocrinus*, ? *Rhodocrinus*) are mentioned; the so-called Vaginaten limestones of the region of St. Petersburg yield, in small quantity, calyces of *Hybocrinus* and various stem joints not generically determinable in closer detail. Much richer than Europe in Lower Silurian Eucrinoideen is North America, and specifically the limestones and shales of the Chazy, Trenton and Hudson River groups in Canada (City of Ottawa, Montreal), the island of Anticosti, the region of the Hudson River and Trenton in New York, as well as of Cincinnati in Ohio, form the principal centres of distribution for the genera *Hybocrinus*, *Anomalocrinus*, *Pachyocrinus*, *Carabocrinus*, *Palaeocrinus*, *Heterocrinus*, *Dendrocrinus*, *Homocrinus*, *Schizocrinus*, *Cupulocrinus*, *Lecanocrinus*, *Glyptocrinus*, *Cleiocrinus* and *Thysanocrinus*.

If the number of Eucrinoideen in the Lower Silurian is still limited, the division of the Tesselaten in the Upper Silurian already stands, at least with respect to genera, at the peak of its development of forms. The Upper Silurian coral limestones of the island of Gotland, with 43 genera and 176 species, have so far remained unequalled in respect of richness in Crinoideen; beside them the beds of the same age at Wenlock (Wales) and Dudley (England), as well as the Baltic provinces of Russia, deserve mention. Bohemia, on the other hand, so rich in other Silurian fossils, has so far yielded only a single genus (*Scyphocrinus*). In North America, Lockport and Schoharhie Co. in the State of New York (Niagara Group), Decatur Co. in Tennessee, Racine and Waukesha in Wisconsin, Leclaire Falls (Iowa), Waldron (Indiana), Chicago **[p. 401]** (Illinois), Guelph (Canada) are to be named as rich localities, to which isolated occurrences in Kentucky, Maryland and Pennsylvania would still have to be added. In all, some 450 Upper Silurian species are distributed over about 62 genera. While the species fall in roughly equal quantity to Europe and North America, Europe shows, through the Gotland wealth of forms, a much greater diversity of genera.

The Upper Silurian fauna of Eucrinoideen receives its characteristic stamp from the strong development of the Cyathocrinidae, Taxocrinidae, Ichthyocrinidae, Crotalocrinidae, Habrocrinidae, Briarocrinidae, Dimerocrinidae, Stelidiocrinidae, Melocrinidae and Calyptocrinidae.

Of Lower Silurian genera, *Dendrocrinus*, *Homocrinus*, *Glyptocrinus* and *Lecanocrinus* are carried over, and of these the first three are in the process of dying out, the last only just in the course of development. Striking is the weak representation of the Poteriocrinidae and Actinocrinidae.

Devonian Eucrinoideen are found in Europe most numerously and in the most favourable preservation in the Middle Devonian limestone of the Eifel (Gerolstein, Kerpen, Prüm, etc.), somewhat more sparingly in the Spiriferen sandstone of Rhenish Prussia, Nassau, Westphalia and in the Harz. Isolated forms are known from the Fichtelgebirge, Thuringia, the Belgian Ardennes, from the Dep. Mayenne in France, from the province of Asturias in Spain and from Russia. In North America the beds of the Upper Helderberg, Hamilton and Oriskany groups in the State of New York (Ontario Co., Erie Co., Falls of the Ohio, Utica etc.)(1), Indiana, Kentucky, Maryland and Illinois have yielded a limited number of forms. In all, some 40 genera with about 230 species have so far been demonstrated in the Devonian formation. As typically Devonian forms the Haplocrinidae, Cupressocrinidae and Gasterocomidae deserve to be named in the first place. The Cyathocrinidae, Taxocrinidae, Cheirocrinidae and Calyptocrinidae have ascended from the Silurian formation, but have already lost in wealth of forms; on the other hand the Poteriocrinidae, Actinocrinidae and Platycrinidae increase, and the Rhodocrinidae and Melocrinidae reach the peak of their development. Among the genera, the following stand out as particularly characteristic, temporally narrowly restricted index forms for the Devonian formation: *Haplocrinus*, *Cupressocrinus*, *Gasterocoma*, *Myrtillocrinus*, *Nanocrinus*, *Hexacrinus*, *Cacabocrinus*, *Lampterocrinus*, *Rhipidocrinus*, *Acanthocrinus* and *Ctenocrinus*. Greatly reduced or even entirely extinct are the Upper Silurian families of the Ichthyocrinidae, Crotalocrinidae, Habrocrinidae, Briarocrinidae, Dimerocrinidae and Polypeltidae.

---
(1) J. Hall, 15th Report of the New York State Cabinet 1862.

**[p. 402]**

In the Coal Measures the Tesselaten unfold once more an astonishing wealth of species and individuals, so that, particularly in North America, in certain beds of the Carboniferous limestone they belong among the more frequent index fossils, whereas in Europe only stem joints occur in masses and calyces count, after all, among the sparser finds. In favourable preservation the specimens from the white, clay-rich Carboniferous limestone of Miatschkowa near Moscow, mostly provided with stem and arms, surpass almost all other Crinoideen originating from Europe. In Germany the Carboniferous limestone of Regnitzlosau in Bavaria, of Ratingen near Elberfeld, Iberg and Grund in the Harz yields isolated, mostly imperfect remains; the preservation is similar in Upper Silesia and in the neighbouring Austrian territories; on the other hand at Tournay and Visé in Belgium, at Bolland, Richmond, Wexford in Yorkshire and at various localities in Scotland and Ireland, well-preserved calyces, sometimes provided with arms, occur alongside stem joints. In North America the Carboniferous Eucrinoideen are distributed over the 5 subdivisions of the Carboniferous limestone, which is extraordinarily developed in the Mississippi and Missouri region (Kinderhook, Burlington, Keokuk, St. Louis and Chester Group, which lie one upon another from bottom to top in the order named). No region offers more favourable conditions for the study of the succession and temporal change of Crinoideen than the surroundings of the famous locality Burlington (Iowa), where all the horizons named are developed in undisturbed bedding and are distinguished by a rare wealth of excellently preserved Crinoideen. Beside the silicified specimens from the "Burlington beds", the arm-bearing and stalked calyces from Crawfordsville (Montgomery Co. Indiana), embedded in the blue marl of the Keokuk group, have attained fame and have recently been exploited on a large scale. Numerous other localities lie in the States of Illinois, Missouri, Kentucky, Ohio and Tennessee.

In all, the number of genera fluctuates between 45 and 50, whereas no fewer than 800 Carboniferous species are enumerated, of which at least ⅔ fall to North America. The extraordinary development of the Actinocrinidae, Platycrinidae and Poteriocrinidae gives the Carboniferous limestone a very definite stamp, particularly since individual genera are distinguished by an enormous wealth of species. Thus, for example, from *Actinocrinus* (incl. *Batocrinus*, *Eretmocrinus*, *Strotocrinus*, *Steganocrinus*, *Amphoracrinus*, *Dorycrinus*, *Agaricocrinus*, *Coelocrinus*) about 250, from *Poteriocrinus* (incl. *Scaphiocrinus* and *Zeacrinus*) **[p. 403]** about 165, from *Platycrinus* about 100 species have been described from the Carboniferous limestone. Besides the genera named, *Cyathocrinus*, *Barycrinus*, *Onychocrinus*, *Graphiocrinus*, *Hydreionocrinus*, *Woodocrinus*, *Agassizocrinus*, *Dichocrinus* and *Ollacrinus* are to be named as characteristic types of the Carboniferous limestone.

From the Dyas only a single genus (*Cyathocrinus*) is known so far.

In the Mesolithic era the Tesselaten are represented only by two unstalked genera (*Marsupites* and *Uintacrinus*) of the Upper Cretaceous. These stand in a relationship to the Poteriocriniden and Rhodocriniden similar to that of the Comatuliden of the present day to the fossil Pentacriniden. All other Crinoideen of the Mesolithic era belong to the Articulaten and Costaten.

The former begin in the Trias with the dicyclic genus *Encrinus* (incl. *Chelocrinus*, *Dadocrinus*). In addition, stem joints of *Pentacrinus* and ? *Porocrinus* Dittm. are found in this formation. All these forms are restricted to the European and Asiatic Trias; crowns of *Encrinus* occur chiefly in the Muschelkalk near Brunswick, Berlin, Upper Silesia, Württemberg and Baden, as well as in the Southern Alps.

The Jurassic formation contains the genera *Eugeniacrinus*, *Triacrinus*, *Phyllocrinus*, *Plicatocrinus*, *Cotylederma*, *Apiocrinus*, *Millericrinus*, *Acrochordocrinus*, *Pentacrinus*, *Antedon*, *Solanocrinus* and *Saccocoma*. *Cotylederma*, *Pentacrinus*, *Millericrinus* and *Acrochordocrinus* are found already in the Lias, and indeed *Pentacrinus* in the Lower Lias near Lyme Regis in Dorset and in the Upper Lias of Boll, Ohmden and other places in Württemberg and Banz in Franconia in the most perfect preservation. Stem joints of *Pentacrinus* belong among the common occurrences in all divisions of the Jurassic formation throughout Europe, whereas *Apiocrinus* is restricted to a smaller number of localities of the Dogger and Malm. A very narrow spatial distribution is possessed by the Upper Jurassic genera *Eugeniacrinus*, *Triacrinus*, *Phyllocrinus*, *Plicatocrinus* and *Solanocrinus*, which occur only in southern Germany, Switzerland, southern France, the Alps and Carpathians. *Saccocoma* belongs exclusively to the lithographic slate of Bavaria.

With the exception of *Saccocoma*, *Plicatocrinus*, *Triacrinus* and *Cotylederma*, all the Jurassic Eucrinoideen also occur in the Cretaceous formation. In the Upper Cretaceous, *Cyathidium*, *Bourguetocrinus* and the two youngest unstalked Tesselaten genera *Marsupites* and *Uintacrinus* are added.

**[p. 404]**

The Tertiary formation is exceedingly poor in Crinoideen. *Pentacrinus*, *Conocrinus*, *Cyathidium* and *Comatula* are the few forms which, in a moderate number of species, connect the Mesolithic Articulaten with their living descendants.

If one compares the Recent Crinoideen with the fossil forms, it emerges that the former are almost without exception carried over from earlier periods of the earth and, with their ancestors, reach up into the Jurassic and Triassic formations. The three most important and form-richest families of the Articulaten (Pentacrinidae, Apiocrinidae, Comatulidae) moreover show themselves so closely bound together by transitions that, despite the diverse appearance of the end members, a common descent appears more than probable. A more isolated position is taken by the families of the Encrinidae, Plicatocrinidae, Eugeniacrinidae and Holopidae.

Whether and in what manner the Articulaten have arisen from the Palaeolithic Tesselaten can now hardly be demonstrated; yet attention may be drawn to the not inconsiderable similarity of certain species of *Heterocrinus* to *Pentacrinus*, to the manifold agreement of *Stemmatocrinus* with *Encrinus*, and to the relationship of *Belemnocrinus* to *Apiocrinus* emphasised by Wachsmuth and Pourtalès. A puzzlingly isolated position is taken by the Jurassic genus *Saccoma*.

It has already been pointed out from many sides that the fossil Eucrinoideen resemble in many respects juvenile states of the living forms. Thus the agreement of the young *Comatula* with *Pentacrinus* cannot in fact be denied; but whether from this single fact the conclusion is justified that all unstalked forms are to be regarded merely as advanced and therefore more highly organised developmental forms of their nearest relatives must, despite all probability, be left open, since unstalked forms appear very early (*Edriocrinus*, *Agassizocrinus*, *Belemnocrinus*) and in other respects display no noteworthy peculiarities.

More important for the assessment of the rank of Tesselaten and Articulaten may be the circumstance that the former bear embryonic characters in several respects. Even the thin constitution of their calyx plates, the strong development of the base and the great extent of the body cavity agree far better with the skeleton of the *Comatula* larva than with that of the mature animal; but still more important may prove to be the structure of the calyx roof. The genera *Coccocrinus* and *Haplocrinus* repeat almost exactly the constitution of the calyx **[p. 405]** roof of the young *Comatula*, and numerous other Palaeolithic genera bear, through strong development of the oral plates, a juvenile stamp. Following Götte, one could designate as persistent embryonic types all the Tesselaten with a subtegminal mouth in general, for in an early juvenile stage the mouth of the *Comatula* larva too lies beneath a closed, though membranous and not plated, roof. For all the reasons adduced, one may designate the Tesselaten as the lower-standing, but also the more developmentally capable, division of the Eucrinoideen.

## 2nd Order. Cystoidea. Beutelstrahler [pouch-rayed animals].(1)

**Spherical or egg-shaped calyces composed of more or less numerous small plates, seldom quite regularly arranged, short-stalked or sessile, very rarely free. Arms weakly developed or absent, emerging in the vicinity of the mouth, which is situated at the summit. Besides the mouth, which is usually surrounded by ambulacral grooves, a second plated opening (? anus) and frequently also a third smaller opening (? ovarial opening). Frequently all or a part of the small plates are provided with pores or hydrospires.**

---
(1) Literature.
Buch, Leop. v. Ueber Cystideen. Abhandlg. Berl. Ak. für 1844. Berlin 1845. 4.
Volborth, Alex. v. Ueber die russischen Sphaeroniten, eingeleitet durch einige Betrachtungen über die Arme der Cystideen. Verhandlg. mineralog. Ges. St. Petersburg 1845—46.
Forbes, Edw. On the British Cystideae. Memoirs of the geological survey of Great Britain. vol. II part 2. 1848.
Müller, Joh. Ueber den Bau der Echinodermen. Abhandlg. Berl. Ak. 1853. S. 178—186.
Hall, J. Palaeontology of New York. vol. II. 1852; vol. III. 1859.
Hall, J. 20th and 24th annual report on the New York State Museum of natural history 1868 u. 1878.
Billings, E. On the Cystidea of the lower Silurian Rocks of Canada. Geological survey of Canada. Figures and Descriptions of Canadian organic remains. Decade III. 1858.
Billings, E. Notes on the structure of the *Crinoidea*, *Cystidea* and *Blastoidea*. Silliman's American Journ. of Sciences 1869. vol. 48 p. 83 und 1870. vol. 49 p. 51 (reprinted in Canadian Naturalist. 2 Ser. vol. V, and in Ann. and Mag. nat. hist. 4 ser. vol. V p. 251 u. 409 und vol. VII p 142).
Schmidt, Fr. Miscellanea Silurica. II. Ueber *Glyptocystites* und untersilurische Cystideen. Mémoires de l'Acad. impér. de St. Petersbourg. vol. XXI No. 11. 1874.
