In addition, papers or descriptions of individual genera and species in the collective works of Gyllenhahl, Wahlenberg, Hisinger, Angelin, Pander, Eichwald, Goldfuss, Bronn, Quenstedt and others.

**[p. 406]**

Of this extinct group of Echinodermen, restricted to the oldest formations,(1) individual species of *Echinosphaerites* and *Sphaeronites* were already described in the last century by Linné and Gyllenhahl under the names Krystalläpple, *Aetites* (Lin.) and *Echinus* (Gyll.), their zoological position however being misinterpreted; it was Leopold v. Buch who first, in the year 1844, in a masterly treatise, furnished an exact account of the structure and the affinities of these fossil bodies, for which he proposed the name Cystideen. The later works of Volborth, Forbes, Joh. Müller, J. Hall, Billings and others rest altogether upon the foundation created by Leop. v. Buch and modify it only in individual points.

In external appearance the Cystoideen recall most of all certain Seelilien with weakly developed arms (*Hypocrinus*, *Poteriocrinus*), yet some (*Sphaeronites*) also resemble the Echiniden, and indeed a small group (*Agelacrinus*) shows characters which recall the Seesterne.

The Cystoideen are spherical, egg-shaped, elliptical, more rarely cylindrical or disc-shaped bodies of moderate size (10—60 mm in greatest diameter), whose dermal skeleton consists of a number, exceedingly variable according to the genus, of polygonal small plates of slight thickness closely joined by sutures. The smallest observed number of calyx plates amounts to 13 (*Hypocrinus*, *Cryptocrinus*); in other forms (*Sphaeronites*, *Glyptosphaerites*, *Agelacrinus*, etc.) it can rise above a hundred. A characteristic feature of most Cystoideen is the lack of a regular radial arrangement of the calyx plates. Only at the summit does a more or less distinct five-rayed structure sometimes come to light through the development of ambulacral grooves.

The base of the calyx is always easily recognisable, whether by an insertion pit for the stem or by an attachment site in the sessile, unstalked forms. A single genus (*Protocrinus*) is unstalked and free.

At the summit, and as a rule in the middle of it, is situated an opening, covered over by small plates in well-preserved specimens, from which 2—5 simple or forked, sometimes quite short ambulacral grooves proceed. Leop. v. Buch, and after him Volborth, Joh. Müller, Forbes, Hall, Ferd. Roemer and others, designate this opening as the mouth; Billings, however, will recognise in it merely the point of entry of the ambulacral vessels and transfers the mouth to one of the lateral openings.

---
(1) The supposed Recent Cystideen genus *Hyponome* Lovén is, according to Wyv. Thomson, to be referred to mutilated specimens of *Antedon*.

**[p. 407]**

Billings has found little approval with this view, and since the course of the ambulacra beneath the plated calyx roof to a central, but completely plated-over mouth has been demonstrated in numerous fossil Armlilien, and since it is known that in all living Eucrinoideen the mouth opening is enclosed by the central ambulacral ring, the interpretation of the summit opening first advocated by Buch can hardly be subject to any doubt, despite Billings's objections.

Besides the mouth, one observes in all Cystoideen a further eccentric opening, in many genera even two, whose significance, in the absence of living representatives of this order, can hardly be established with absolute certainty. If besides the mouth only one opening is present, it lies mostly laterally in the vicinity of the summit; sometimes it also moves further down and is now and then found even in the vicinity of the base. In well-preserved specimens it is as a rule closed by a valvular apparatus of triangular small plates, which form a usually five- or six-sided pyramid. (In *Pleurocystites* and *Glyptocystites* the lateral opening possesses a considerable size and is covered by a pavement of small polygonal plates.) Leopold v. Buch, who believed the triangular valves of this opening in *Echinosphaerites* to be provided with pores, designated it as the ovarial opening. Only a few authors, such as Volborth, Forbes, J. Hall, have unconditionally adhered to this view. Already Joh. Müller raises doubts against "a conjecture which could not be securely proved". Salter, Wyv. Thomson, Meek, Worthen and Angelin hold the second opening, in those forms which possess only two at all, to be merely the anus; Ferd. Roemer holds it to be anus and genital opening at the same time.

Billings is of a divergent opinion and designates the opening provided with the valvular pyramid as the common mouth and anal opening. In a treatise on the living Echiniden genus *Leskia* (translated by Kunth, Zeitschr. der deutschen geolog. Gesellschaft 1868. XX. S. 639), Lovén, on the ground of the mouth of *Leskia* being provided with a pyramid of small plates, adheres to the view already represented by Wahlenberg, Pander, Hisinger and de Koninck and declares the "pyramid" of the Cystoideen to be the mouth. In the part of the posthumous work of Angelin edited by Lovén (Iconographia Crinoideorum in Stratis Sueciae siluricis. 1878), however, the ambulacral opening at the summit is always designated as the mouth, the pyramid as the anus. In favour of this latter interpretation speak all the analogies among the **[p. 408]** living Crinoideen. A third, smaller opening, sometimes pore-like and always situated in the vicinity of the mouth, is found only in a limited number of genera, and chiefly in those with weakly developed arms. Leop. v. Buch held it to be the anus. Those authors, on the other hand, who recognise the anus in the valvular pyramid designate the small opening beside the summit as the genital pore.

The arms are as a rule weakly developed, sometimes even so atrophied that Leop. v. Buch emphasised the lack of arms as a prominent character of the Cystoideen. Yet already Volborth showed that the supposedly armless Echinosphaeriten and Echinoencriniten are provided with jointed appendages to which the designation of arms cannot be denied. Their points of attachment are situated mostly in the vicinity of the mouth, and thus stand far nearer to the middle of the summit than the arms of the Eucrinoideen. The number of arms varies between 2 and 5; they are never branched and, when they stand freely around the mouth, are only exceptionally beset with pinnules (*Comarocystites*); usually they are composed of uniserially arranged joints, whose hollowed ventral side is covered with marginal platelets (*Pleurocystites*, Fig. 285).

Fig. 285. Arms of *Pleurocystites*.

In various genera (*Glyptosphaerites*, *Protocrinus*, *Echinosphaerites* etc., Fig. 286) simple or branched ambulacral grooves proceed from the mouth, which end at small articular facets, and on these rise minute, very rarely preserved arms or pinnules. Other genera (*Lepocrinites*, *Callocystites*, *Agelacrinus*) are distinguished by ambulacral furrows which are bordered on both sides by rows of small plates bearing jointed, mostly biserial pinnules. Since a furrow leads from the base of the pinnules to the central groove, these ambulacral furrows correspond exactly to the ventral side of a Crinoideen arm; they are therefore also designated outright by Billings and others as recurved arms grown fast to the calyx by their dorsal side. In some forms the groove lies somewhat to the side and only one row **[p. 409]** of pinnules comes to development — the recumbent arms are thus laterally attached. In several genera, finally, the evidently minute and easily destroyed arms are still unknown, but their presence is indicated by small tubercles or facets in the vicinity of the mouth.

Fig. 286. *Glyptosphaerites Leuchtenbergi* Volb. Calyx with ambulacral grooves, plated mouth opening, laterally situated large anal opening and small genital opening between mouth and anus.

A characteristic peculiarity of most Cystoideen is the porous constitution of some, sometimes even of all, of the calyx plates. In a number of genera (*Glyptosphaerites*, *Sphaerites*, *Protocrinites* etc.) two pores are always joined in pairs and are situated either on a wart-like elevation or small facet or in a slight depression of the plate surface (Fig. 287). These double pores mostly cover the whole calyx in great number (Fig. 286). Still more noteworthy are the striated rhombs and pore rhombs occurring in numerous forms, i.e. rows of pores or slits which form a rhombic figure and always belong to two neighbouring small plates, the sutures of the plates usually falling in the short, sometimes also in the longer diagonal of the rhombs. The pores pass through the whole thickness of the plates and open either freely on the outer side or into superficial, somewhat raised transverse tubes (Fig. 288 b), or they are closed by a thin membrane (Fig. 288 a). Each two opposite pores of a rhomb are always joined by tubes or channels (rhomb slits), which lie either on the outer (*Caryocystites*, *Codonaster*) or inner side (*Echinosphaerites*, *Caryocrinus*) of the plates. In the former case the tubes sometimes project as raised little ridges (Fig. 288 b). In some genera 2, more rarely 3 tubes or slits can join two opposite pores of a rhomb; sometimes the marginal pores are entirely absent and the rhombs consist only of parallel slits which arise through a folding of the exceedingly thin shell (*Lepocrinites*, *Codonaster*, *Glyptocystites*). The rhombs are sometimes found on all **[p. 410]** the plates; in some genera, however, they are restricted to particular places on the surface and are present in small number (*Echinoencrinus*, *Lepocrinites*). The isolated striated rhombs (pectinated rhombs) too always belong by halves to two neighbouring plates; sometimes the two halves develop independently and are separated from one another by a smooth interspace (*Callocystites*, Fig. 290), and indeed one of the two halves can under some circumstances be entirely absent, so that triangular half-rhombs arise. Not seldom the two halves possess unequal shape: the one is triangular, the other kidney-, egg- or crescent-shaped, or both assume a crescent shape and are then mostly surrounded by a somewhat raised rim which encloses a transversely furrowed surface.

Fig. 287. Double pores on calyx plates of *Glyptosphaerites* (enlarged).

Fig. 288. Pore rhombs a of *Echinosphaerites* and b of *Caryocrinus* (enlarged). In Fig. a the thin surface layer is abraded on the left, so that the connecting tubes lie open.

Fig. 289. Calyx plates of *Caryocrinus* with 4 half pore rhombs, a from inside and b from outside.

Fig. 290. *Callocystis Jewetti* Hall. a Calyx in natural size with ambulacral furrows and 2 striated rhomb halves above the base. b Summit enlarged (o mouth, an anus, g genital opening, rh striated rhombs).

On the significance of these remarkable pore rhombs the living Crinoideen afford only insufficient information. The only homology might perhaps be found in the calyx pores of *Pentacrinus*, *Antedon* and *Rhizocrinus*, which, according to the observations of Hub. Ludwig, conduct water to the central ambulacral ring. Forbes compares them with the ciliated bands of the Ophiuriden and Echiniden larvae, and Joh. Müller with the respiratory pores of the Asteriden; this view is shared by most authors, such as Huxley, Eichwald, Dana, Rofe, Billings and others. The last-named designates the rhombs outright as hydrospires and their openings, according to their form, as pores, slits or spiracula. A conjecture expressed several times, that the hydrospires and pores of the Cystoideen indicated the points of exit of ambulacral feet or tentacles, is already refuted by the fact that the pores of the rhombs open not directly into the body cavity but into the connecting tubes, and likewise the slits arising through folding communicate only indirectly, by means of their porous walls, with the interior. A homology **[p. 411]** with the ambulacral pores of the Echiniden is, moreover, not to be thought of, also because of the interambulacral position of the Cystoideen pores.

With the exception of the long-stalked *Caryocrinus*, the stem in the Cystoideen shows a weak development; often it is entirely absent and the calyces are fastened to the bottom either with the whole underside (*Agelacrinus*) or by means of a knobby projection (*Echinosphaerites*). The stem joints are round, always without lateral cirri, ring-shaped, provided with a very wide alimentary canal, and frequently pushed into one another like the draw-tubes of a telescope. Even in the forms with a developed stem, the latter does not seem to have served as an organ of attachment, for as a rule it tapers downwards and shows at the end no thickening or root-like offshoots. Stems of *Echinoencrinus* and other Cystoideen have several times been described under the name *Cornulites*.

Concerning the assignment of the Cystoideen to the Crinoideen no doubt can any longer prevail at present. By the position of the mouth at the summit, the position and constitution of the ambulacral furrows, by the presence of arms, by the possession of a jointed stem and by the plated constitution of the calyx, which consists of a dorsal and a ventral side, the Cystoideen attach themselves closely to the true Seelilien, and specifically to those of the older formations. As characters which separate the Cystoideen from the Eucrinoideen and make the establishment of a special order necessary, there are to be emphasised the pores and pore rhombs interpreted as respiratory organs, the irregular arrangement of the calyx plates, the incomplete radial structure of the body, as well as the atrophy and peculiar constitution of the arms. In the Armlilien the calyx pores are restricted to the ventral roof; in the Cystoideen they are developed on the whole surface and particularly on the dorsal side. Not to be underestimated is also the circumstance that in the Cystoideen a third opening sometimes lies between mouth and anus, which can hardly be interpreted otherwise than as an ovarial opening. If, however, the genital organs are really situated in the body cavity and not, as in the Eucrinoideen, in the arms, this points to an essential difference in the whole organisation. If some genera (*Pleurocystites*, *Caryocrinus*, *Porocrinus*, *Comarocystites*) still possess arms which resemble those of the Armlilien in all essential characters, then on the other hand the peculiar ambulacral furrows beset with rows of pinnules in *Lepocrinites*, *Callocystites*, *Cystoblastus*, *Asteroblastus*, *Codonaster* etc. depart widely from the normal form of a Crinoideen arm. They have been designated as recurved arms grown fast by their dorsal side, and they are certainly structures homologous with the **[p. 412]** true arms. According to their external appearance as well as their structure, they moreover agree still better with the so-called pseudambulacral fields of the Blastoideen.

Some Cystoideen genera (*Caryocrinus*, *Porocrinus*, *Hemicosmites*, *Cryptocrinus* and others) place themselves, as Leop. v. Buch has already excellently shown, immediately beside the true Eucrinoideen and point to a common descent with them. Opposed to these types, however, stand the forms just named, provided with ambulacral fields, as transitional types to the Blastoideen; indeed one may say that the boundary line between Cystoideen and Blastoideen is drawn much less sharply still than that between Cystoideen and Eucrinoideen. Since Billings recognised in *Codonaster* a Cystoideen form with slit rhombs whose calyx is built exactly as in the Blastoideen, there remains for the distinction of the two orders, as the only character, merely the circumstance that in the Cystoideen the hydrospires never communicate with the ambulacral fields through pores, whereas in the Blastoideen this is always the case.

The Cystoideen thus take up an intermediate position between Eucrinoideen and Blastoideen. They are true Crinoideen, and if the affinities of, for example, *Agelacrinus* with the Asteriden and Ophiuriden, or of *Echinosphaerites*, *Sphaeronites* and others with certain Echinoideen have been emphasised by some authors, the supposed homologies mostly do not stand the test on careful examination and in any case rank behind those with the Eucrinoideen.

Nearly 40 Cystoideen genera are known at present, of which some, to be sure, are available only in insufficient preservation. As a systematic principle for the Cystoideen, Volborth and Forbes utilised in the first place the more or less regular structure of the calyx, consisting either of a fixed, small number of plates or of a very considerable quantity of irregularly arranged small plates, and in the second place the development and constitution of the arms or ambulacral passages. Joh. Müller laid the chief weight on the pores and rhombs (hydrospires) and accordingly divided the Cystoideen into 3 groups: a) forms without pores (Aporitidae), b) forms with double pores (Diploporitidae), c) forms with pore rhombs or striated rhombs (Rhombiferi). Müller's classification has also been retained in the present book, although it presents various defects and in particular the chief character of the first group, which consists of rather heterogeneous elements, does not seem to be an absolutely reliable one. In the genus *Cryptocrinus*, for example, I believe I can observe scattered simple pores, and in *Hypocrinus* too Beyrich mentions a **[p. 413]** porous constitution of the calyx plates. It may also be remarked that the genus *Mesites* is united by F. Schmidt with *Agelacrinus*, but differs from the poreless *Agelacrinus* by the possession of double pores and thus, according to the above principle of classification, must be assigned to another group.

### 1st Group. Aporitidae. J. Müll.

*Calyx plates without double pores or striated rhombs.*

***Cryptocrinus* Pander.** Calyx spherical, consisting of 3 smooth B and 2 zones of large unequal lateral plates. In addition some small plates at the summit, where the mouth, covered with small platelets, lies. If the covering plates have fallen off, one sees 4—5 quite short forked furrows proceeding from the mouth opening, which end at small articular surfaces. In the corner of junction of two lateral plates of the first and one of the second zone lies a larger anal opening closed by valves (ovarial opening v. Buch). Arms and stem unknown. On the base a round and very small insertion site for the stem. Lower Silurian (Vaginaten limestone) of Russia.

***Hypocrinus* Beyrich.** Calyx spherical, consisting of 3 B, 5 large lateral and 5 somewhat smaller summit plates. Mouth central, five-sided. On the summit plates small attachment facets for arms. Anal opening in the corner where one lateral and two summit plates meet. Attachment site of the stem small, round. Arms unknown. All calyx plates smooth, in part of porous texture. Carboniferous limestone of Timor.

***Echinocystites* Hall** (non Wyville Thomson). Spherical, consisting of 4 zones of plates. B probably 4, above them 2 zones with 5 lateral plates and one row of summit plates. Mouth central; anus lateral. Upper Silurian (Niagara Group). Wisconsin.

***Ateleocystites* Billings** (*Placocystites* de Kon., *Anomalocystites* Hall). Body four-sided, rounded at the summit, convex on one side, flat on the other, composed of a moderately large number of plates, which are not arranged in regular rows and are provided with scaly transverse striae. On the convex side there are 2 rather deeply situated openings one above the other. From the summit proceed jointed, free, thin arms. Lower and Upper Silurian. Devonian. England, North America.

**? *Heterocystites* Hall.** Upper Silurian (Niagara Group).

***Amygdalocystites* Billings.** Egg-shaped, consisting of 3 B and 8 or more irregular rows of plates. At the summit arise 2 strong arms lying upon the calyx, whose ambulacral furrows lie laterally and bear only a single row of pinnules. Mouth at the summit, below it a second anal opening closable by valves. Stem round, rather long. Lower Silurian (Trenton limestone). Canada.

***Malocystites* Billings.** Egg-shaped or spherical, composed of about 40—50 plates, which show no regular arrangement. From the laterally situated mouth proceed several (often numerous) narrow and long raised **[p. 414]** ambulacral furrows (recumbent arms according to Billings). At the summit there is a roundish opening (? anus). Stem unknown. Plates not porous. Lower Silurian. Canada.

***Agelacrinus* Vanuxem** (*Lepidodiscus*, *Streptaster* Hall, *Haplocystites* F. Roem.) (Fig. 291). Circular, hemispherical or disc-shaped, unstalked, grown fast with the whole underside upon foreign bodies; upper side arched. Calyx composed of numerous, irregular small plates, which at the periphery are small and overlap in scaly fashion. From the central mouth, closed by 4 triangular oral plates, 5 curved ambulacral furrows radiate out towards the periphery, which are bordered on both sides by one, sometimes also 2 rows of somewhat differently shaped small plates. In the middle of the largest interambulacral field lies an opening (? anus) closable by a valvular pyramid. Silurian. Devonian. Carboniferous limestone. Principal distribution in the Silurian.

Fig. 291. *Agelacrinus Cincinnatiensis* F. Roem. Lower Silurian. Cincinnati, Ohio. Specimen in natural size grown upon *Strophomena alternata*.

The subgenus *Hemicystites* Hall is distinguished by broader, shorter and straight ambulacral furrows. As *Cytaster* Hall designates a sack-shaped, attached, sometimes also free *Hemicystites* form. Lower Silurian.

***Edrioaster* Billings** (*Cyclaster* Billings). Like *Agelacrinus*, only the ambulacral furrows accompanied on each side by 2 rows of double pores, which emerge in the sutures of the ambulacral plates. Lower Silurian. Canada.

***Cyathocystis* F. Schmidt.** Calyx cup-shaped, grown fast with a broad base, consisting of a single piece, transversely wrinkled on the sides. Summit closed by a pentagonal or roundish lid, which displays a star of 5 ambulacral rays covered by 2 alternating rows of small plates, and between these in each case a large triangular interpalmar plate. In the centre 5 small pentagonal oral plates close the mouth. The lid is surrounded all round by a row of marginal platelets. In one of the interpalmar fields lies the anal opening provided with a valvular pyramid. The lid just described is seldom preserved; mostly one finds only the calyces open at the top, which are sometimes laterally fused into colonies. Lower Silurian. Reval.

### 2nd Group. Diploporitidae. Joh. Müll.

*Calyx with double pores, of which several always belong to one and the same small plate.*

***Mesites* Hoffmann** (emend. F. Schmidt and Nikitin(1)). Spherical, with somewhat flattened base, probably stalked, consisting of numerous polygonal small plates which are provided with double pores.

---
(1) Nikitin. Ueber *Mesites Pusirefskii*. Bull. Soc. des Naturalistes de Moscou 1877. vol. 52 p. 301.

**[p. 415]**

From the depressed summit centre, covered by small plates, radiate 5 narrow, straight ambulacral furrows, which become somewhat higher towards the lower margin and continue on the underside as simple porous ribs. On the upper side the ambulacral furrows are covered over by 2 rows of roof-like upraised small plates, and on the latter one also notices small insertion surfaces and pores for pinnules. In one of the interambulacral fields lies an anal opening closable by a valvular pyramid. A large insertion site located in the middle of the base points to the presence of a stem. Lower Silurian. Russia.

***Asteroblastus* Eichw.** (emend. Fr. Schmidt(1)). Bud-shaped, five-sided, stalked. Stem round. Upper side of the calyx forming a flat five-sided scalenohedral pyramid; underside hemispherical, composed of many round, radially ribbed small plates beset with double pores. Basalia 4. Summit formed of 5 summit plates forked out downwards, between which the ambulacral passages and the summit opening open. With the summit plates alternate 5 flat, rhombic or egg-shaped pseudambulacral fields reaching down about to the middle of the calyx, which attach with their upper ends to the lower limbs of the summit plates. These consist of 2 alternating rows of 6—10 elongated transverse pieces, which in the middle leave free an ambulacral groove running up to the summit plate. Into the latter open laterally smaller grooves, corresponding in number to the radial platelets. On the two lateral margins one recognises the depressed attachment sites of the pinnules, which, when preserved, completely cover the pseudambulacral fields. On the latter there are no pores; also only a single summit opening is present. Lower Silurian. Region of St. Petersburg.

***Gomphocystites* Hall.** Elongate club-shaped, narrowed downwards, unstalked, consisting of numerous small plates provided with double pores. Summit arched, with central mouth, from which 5 spiral, simple ambulacral furrows proceed. Anus close beside the mouth. Upper Silurian (Niagara Group), Lockport (New York), Racine (Wisconsin) etc.

---
(1) Possibly the genus *Astrocrinites* Austin from the Carboniferous limestone of Scotland can be attached to *Mesites* and *Asteroblastus*, although it deviates in essential characters from all Cystoideen and, according to the careful account of Etheridge (Quart. journ. geological Soc. London 1876. vol. 32 p. 103), stands in several respects nearer to the Blastoideen. The small calyx of *Astrocrinites* (*Zygocrinus* Bronn) is unstalked, the arched upper side four-edged, with 4 depressed fields in which narrow leaf-shaped pseudambulacral fields lie. At the summit there is a mouth opening covered by 4 spearhead-like plates and a second, likewise plated opening in an interambulacral field built differently from the 3 others and composed of several small plates. The 4 lateral pieces of the calyx are forked out upwards and covered with striated warts. The base consists of 3 unequal plates and perhaps also a centrodorsal plate. The warts of the basalia seem to be provided with pores (?). To the family of the Astrocrinidae Austin also reckons a second, not more closely known genus *Aporocrinites* Austin.

**[p. 416]**

***Protocrinites* Eichwald.** Spherical or almost hemispherical, unstalked, free, with flattened base, consisting of numerous, thick, arched small plates beset with double pores. From the triangular mouth situated at the summit proceed 5 long, branched ambulacral grooves. Anus eccentric, closed by a valvular pyramid. Between mouth and anus a third pore-shaped (? genital) opening. Lower Silurian. Russia.

***Glyptosphaerites* Joh. Müll.** (Fig. 292). Body large, spherical, stalked, consisting of numerous polygonal small plates beset with double pores.

Fig. 292. *Glyptosphaerites Leuchtenbergi* Volb. Lower Silurian. St. Petersburg. a Specimen from above in natural size, b from below with stem, somewhat reduced (after Volborth). c some calyx plates with double pores, enlarged.

The pores lie in great number either on warts or in small rimmed depressions. Mouth pentagonal, at the summit, covered by 5 unequal valves. From the corners of the mouth arise 5 long, fine ambulacral slits, which send out shorter lateral canals, all of which end at small wart-like articular facets. Rather distant from the mouth there is a rather large, eccentric anal opening, and between the two a third smaller ? genital pore. Stem roundish-pentagonal, composed of low, ring-shaped joints with a very wide, pentagonal central canal. Lower Silurian. Pulkowa near Petersburg and Sweden.

***Eucystis* Angelin.** Egg-shaped, sessile, similar to *Glyptosphaerites*. Anus approached to the mouth. From the corners of the mouth proceed 5 short, branched furrows ending in wart-shaped facets. Lower Silurian. Sweden.

***Holocystites* Hall** (*Caryocystites* Hall non Buch, *Megacystites* Hall, Angelin). Body elongate, subcylindrical or oval, consisting of numerous, rather large, six-sided or polygonal plates, short-stalked or sessile. Mouth at the summit, surrounded by short arms; in addition a second eccentric opening closable by valves. Between the two sometimes also a third smaller opening. Double pores on all calyx plates. Stem round, short, at the end with root-like offshoots. Upper Silurian. (North America and Gotland.)

**? *Crinocystites* Hall.** Upper Silurian. Wisconsin.

**[p. 417]**

***Sphaeronites* Hisinger** (*Sphaeronis* Ang., *Echinosphaerites* Volb. p. p.). Body spherical or egg-shaped, unstalked, grown fast with the base upon foreign bodies, consisting of numerous polygonal, warty plates. All the warts are pierced by double pores, which are connected in the interior by means of tubes. Summit with a pentagonal ambulacral field, which is surrounded by a depression and in whose corners the facets for 5 pairs of small arms are situated. Mouth slit-shaped, beside it at the summit a further larger opening (? anus) and between the two a third smaller ? genital pore. From the mouth 5 fine tentacle furrows lead to the base of the arms. Lower Silurian (Vaginaten limestone) of Sweden and Russia. *Sphaer. pomum* Gyllh.

### 3rd Group. Rhombiferi. Joh. Müll.

*Calyx plates with pore rhombs or striated rhombs.*

#### a) Pore rhombs in very great number, on almost all the calyx plates.

***Echinosphaerites* Wahlenbg.** (*Leucophthalmus* König, *Sphaeronites* p. p. auct., *Echinosphaera* Ang.) (Fig. 293). Body spherical, unstalked, grown fast with a short, drawn-out base, consisting of numerous, irregularly placed, smooth, thin, mostly six-sided small plates. Mouth at the summit, in the middle of a very short, simple or three-limbed, finely plated ambulacral slit surrounded by raised margins, at whose ends stand short and weak arms. Beside the mouth a small opening, seldom closed by valves (genital opening Angelin, anus v. Buch); somewhat more distant an anal opening closed by usually 5 (sometimes 4—8) triangular valves forming a pyramid **[p. 418]** (? ovarial opening v. Buch, ? mouth Billings). All the calyx plates are provided with rhombic hydrospires, whose opposite pores are connected on the inner side of the plates by one or two slits. The number of striated rhombs, which always belong to two neighbouring plates, depends on the number of sides of the plates, since the striae always stand perpendicular to the suture and frequently connect the midpoint of one plate with the centre of the neighbouring ones. The striation becomes distinctly visible on weathered specimens by grinding, or even merely by wetting. Very frequent in the Lower Silurian limestone of Russia and Scandinavia.

Fig. 293. *Echinosphaerites aurantium* His. sp. Lower Silurian (Vaginaten limestone) Pulkowa near St. Petersburg. a from above, b from the side. c Mouth with plated ambulacral furrows and arms (enlarged). d Calyx plates enlarged, with pore rhombs; the latter are somewhat abraded on the left side, so that the canals reach the surface.

***Caryocystites* v. Buch** (*Echinosphaerites* p. p. auct., *Sphaeronites* p. p. auct., *Helicoceras* Eichw.) (Fig. 294). Spherical, egg-shaped or elongate, unstalked. Mouth, anus and ambulacral furrows as in the preceding genus. The calyx plates, however, larger, less numerous and not smooth, but ribbed or striated, since the tubes of the hydrospires project ridge-like at the surface. Lower Silurian. Sweden, Norway and Russia.

Fig. 294. *Caryocystites granatum* Wahlenbg. sp. Lower Silurian. Oeland. A portion of the surface with hydrospires in nat. size.

***Palaeocystites* Billings** (*Actinocrinus* p. p. Hall). Oval or pear-shaped, composed of numerous polygonal, porous plates. The pores form rhombs whose apices meet in the centre of the plates and whose tubes on the inner side of the plates connect each 2 opposite pores. On the outer side one notices the pore slits only over the sutures of the plates as a short transverse striation. Stem, mouth, anus and arms unknown. Lower Silurian (Chazy limestone) Canada.

***Achradocystites* Eichw.** Lower Silurian. Esthonia.

***Comarocystites* Billings.** Egg-shaped, composed of 3 B and 8—11 irregular zones of mostly six-sided plates. A lateral anal opening, closable by 5 valves, in the vicinity of the summit. Mouth at the summit, slit-shaped, beside it 2 (or more?) long, uniserial arms beset with thin pinnules. Stem round, smooth. Pore rhombs incompletely developed, but numerous. Lower Silurian. Canada.

#### b) Pore rhombs only on the lateral plates of the calyx, absent on the roof.

***Caryocrinus* Say** (Fig. 295). Calyx acorn- to egg-shaped, very long-stalked, with moderately developed simple arms. On the cylindrical stem (sometimes 18 cm long), consisting of low round joints, rest 4 paired basal plates, of which the 2 smaller ones lying beside one another are four-sided and pointed upwards, while the 2 larger five-sided ones turn their broad side upwards. There then follows a circlet of 6 large and high plates, of which 3 are five-sided, 1 six-sided and 2 seven-sided. The 3rd, uppermost zone consists of 8 somewhat smaller plates, of which 6, of fairly equal shape, are six-sided and direct their apex downwards, while on the 2 large **[p. 419]** seven-sided plates of the middle zone rest 2 smaller plates truncated below. The flat, three-sided calyx roof is plated by a six-sided central plate around which 5 smaller ones lie. At the margin arise 6, 9 or 13 uniserial, whip-shaped, simple arms, and between these is situated the single, almost marginal opening of the calyx roof, provided with 6 triangular valves (anus). The tentacle furrows of the arms form fine pores at their base and probably continue beneath the calyx roof. With the exception of the calyx roof, all the calyx plates bear pore rhombs (hydrospires), and indeed 2 neighbouring plates always take part in each rhomb. The pores, projecting wart-like on the outside, penetrate the whole thickness of the plates and are connected on their inner side by means of simple tubes (Fig. 295 c). The rows of pores on the two lateral zones of plates always begin in the midpoint of the plates, on the basalia from the common centre, and run from there to the corners, where they meet the corresponding rows of the neighbouring plates. Very frequent in Upper Silurian deposits (Niagara Group) at Lockport and other places on the Erie Canal in New York, in Tennessee etc. in North America.

Fig. 295. *Caryocrinus ornatus* Say. Upper Silurian. Lockport, New York. a Calyx with arms from the side. b Summit (nat. size). c Calyx plates of the 2nd row with hydrospires from outside and inside.

***Hemicosmites* v. Buch.** Calyx egg-shaped. B 4, developed in pairs, as in *Caryocrinus*. First zone of lateral plates consisting of 3 seven-sided, one six-sided and 2 five-sided, large and high, symmetrically arranged plates; the 3 standing on and between the pentagons of the base end in a point above, the 3 of the other half of the calyx are truncated above. In the upper corner between a five-sided pointed plate and the narrower six-sided plate is situated an opening closed by 5 valves (anus). The second zone already takes part in the arched upper side and contains 3 narrow five-sided and 6 six-sided plates, broader below and becoming narrower upwards. The summit is closed by 6 unequal small plates; in their middle is situated a three-limbed slit (mouth), which in very well-preserved specimens is covered with small platelets. Each slit continues into a shallow ambulacral furrow, at whose ends lie raised facets for the insertion of arms. Stem round. The basalia have only scattered pores; on the lower lateral plates a double row of pores runs from the centre to the middle upper corner, a single row to each upper lateral angle; on the lower half of the plates the rows are incompletely developed. In the **[p. 420]** upper zone of lateral plates only the lower half shows itself provided with rows of pores, and these form rhombs (hydrospires) with those of the plates lying beneath. The pores are perforating and connected on the inner side by means of tubes. Lower Silurian (Vaginaten limestone) of St. Petersburg, Bala and Llandeilo beds in England and Wales. Upper Silurian. Wisconsin (North America).

**Forms of doubtful position:**

***Porocrinus* Billings** (Fig. 296). Calyx conical, small, very regular, built like *Poteriocrinus*. Base dicyclic. IB 5, large, five-sided. PB 5, high, six-sided. R 5, large, with narrow, horseshoe-shaped articular surface, between them 2 IRA. Arms whip-shaped, simple, uniserial. In the sutural corners of the calyx formed by the meeting of the sutures there are situated three small, finely striated pore rhombs in each case, which belong half to a calyx plate and for the other half to one of the small supplementary plates inserted in the corners. Stem round, becoming thinner downwards, joints low. Lower Silurian. Canada. Russian Baltic provinces.

This genus forms a connecting link of the Cystoideen with the Eucrinoideen. The calyx is constituted exactly as in *Poteriocrinus* and the arms too are similarly formed. The presence of pore rhombs, however, speaks for an assignment to the Cystoideen.

Fig. 296. a *Porocrinus conicus* Billings. Lower Silurian. City of Ottawa, Canada (nat. size, after Billings). b *P. radiatus* Beyr. Lower Silurian. St. Petersburg. 3 R with attached arms, 2 PB and 3 IB, strongly enlarged (after Beyrich).

***Macrocystella* Callaway.** Calyx subcylindrical, twice as long as broad, thickest in the middle, consisting of about 4 rows of six-sided small plates, which are covered with raised ribs radiating from the centre. At the upper margin stands a circlet of fine, jointed, short pinnules? (arms?). Column very long, thick above, tapering downwards. Lowest Silurian (Cambrian) of Shropshire.

***Tiaracrinus* Schultze.** Cup-shaped or subconical, with truncated, somewhat sunken summit; the sides formed of 3 very large, equal, leaf-shaped triangular basal plates, a 4th somewhat shorter basale and a four-sided low small plate which rests upon the latter. Summit circular, probably covered with 2 circlets of small plates, in the centre with a short five-rayed star. Outer margin of the summit with a few flat, rounded notches (? arm articulations). A marginal anal opening seems to be present. Insertion surface of the stem four-sided. On the sides there are 4 large triangular hydrospires, which consist of 4—6 horizontal, raised tubes, whose perforating end pores are arranged on 2 neighbouring plates in rows running parallel to the sutures. The connecting tubes of two pores thus always cross the longitudinal sutures and belong half to the one, half to the other plate. Between the hydrospires lie 4 smooth longitudinal furrows. Devonian. Eifel.

**[p. 421]**

The only species (*T. quadrifrons*) was described by L. Schultze and, as a wholly aberrant form, attached to the Blastoideen, to which however the genus possesses no closer relations. In my opinion Schultze has placed the body upside down and designated the 4 basal pieces provided with hydrospires as summit plates.

#### c) Striated rhombs present in small number, mostly separated by considerable interspaces.

***Lepadocrinus* Hall** (*Lepocrinites* [male!] Conrad, *Pseudocrinites* Pearce, *Apiocystites* Forbes). Calyx egg-shaped, two- to four-sided or rhombic, with rounded edges, long-stalked, consisting of 4 B and 3 successive zones of 5 lateral plates each. Mouth situated at the summit; from it radiate 2 or 4 straight ambulacral passages, running down to below the middle of the calyx and bounded by raised margins. The margins of these consist of small plates, between which short grooves lead from a marginal pore to the median furrow; in very well-preserved specimens biserial, long pinnules stand above these pores. An anal opening closable by 6 triangular valves lies laterally in the vicinity of the summit. Pore rhombs 3, formed of kidney-shaped or triangular halves; of these the lower one half on a basale, the other half on one of the first lateral plates; the 2 other rhombs in the vicinity of the summit. Stem thick, comparatively long, tapering downwards, consisting of low, ring-shaped joints. Middle and Upper Silurian in England, North America, allegedly also in the Carboniferous limestone of North America.

***Callocystites* Hall** (Fig. 297). Egg-shaped, short-stalked, composed of 4 principal rows of plates, of which the upper one at the summit is little distinct. B 4, the 2 lower lateral zones consisting of 8 plates each. From the summit radiate 5 long ambulacral furrows, which sometimes fork into 2 branches and are laterally beset with 2 alternating rows of strong pinnules. Laterally below the central mouth situated at the summit lies an anal opening closable by valves. Close beside the mouth, on the plated margin of the ambulacral furrows, there are 2 fine pores (? genital openings). Pore rhombs 4, consisting of 2 kidney-shaped halves surrounded by raised rims; of these one above the base, the three others in 3 interambulacral fields in the vicinity of the summit. Stem round, tapering downwards. Upper Silurian (Niagara Group).

Fig. 297. *Callocystites Jewetti* Hall. Lower Silurian (Niagara Group) Lockport, New York. a Specimen in nat. size. b Summit and ambulacral furrows enlarged (copy after Hall).

***Sphaerocystites* Hall.** Upper Silurian.

**[p. 422]**

***Prunocystites* Forbes.** Incompletely known. Calyx small, egg-shaped, consisting of 3 principal rows of large polygonal plates. Summit provided with long, biserial, simple arms. Below these a pore rhomb composed of 2 halves. Stem thick, round, tapering downwards. Upper Silurian. England.

***Pleurocystites* Billings** (Fig. 298 and 285). Calyx flatly egg-shaped, short-stalked, unequal-sided. Anal side occupied almost entirely by a large oval space (? anus) closed by numerous small plates; the opposite side (dorsal side) with large polygonal plates, which are arranged as in *Echinoencrinus*. B 4; the 3 isolated striated rhombs are situated on the dorsal side, namely one above the base, the two others high up on the 2nd row of lateral plates. At the summit arise two simple, rather strong arms, beset on the ventral side with marginal platelets (Fig. 285). Stem short, tapering downwards, consisting of round, unequally thick, ring-shaped joints. Lower Silurian. Canada.

Fig. 298. *Pleurocystites squamosus* Billings. Lower Silurian. Ottawa, Canada. a from the dorsal side, b from the anal side.

***Echinoencrinus* H. v. Meyer** (*Sycocystites* Buch, *Gonocrinites* p. p. Leuchtenbg.). Calyx irregularly egg-shaped, short-stalked, formed of 4 successive zones of polygonal plates. B 4, of which 3 pointed pentagonal, the 4th truncated hexagonal. The 3 following zones consist of 5 plates each, of which the uppermost, smaller ones form the summit. All the calyx plates are ornamented with rod-shaped ribs, which, proceeding from the centre, stand at right angles to the sutures and, by fusing with the corresponding ribs of the neighbouring plates, form raised triangles and rhombs on the surface, which almost entirely conceal the sutures. Low down, where 4 plates of the first and second rows of lateral plates unite, lies a large opening (? anus), sometimes closed by valves. At the summit there is a groove whose raised margins are beset with articular surfaces for short, uniserial, simple arms. The short, curved stem, tapering downwards, sinks into a quadrangular depression of the base and consists of ring-shaped, hollow joints, which are pushed into one another like the draw-tubes of a telescope.

Each calyx possesses 3 pore rhombs (hydrospires); of these 2 lie beside one another opposite the anus on the B and the first lateral plates, the 3rd on the opposite side between mouth and anus. The pores are connected by tubes which run in the middle of the plates. Lower Silurian. *E. angulosus* Pand. sp. Pulkowa.

To *Echinoencrinus* Forbes also reckons 2 species occurring in Upper Silurian deposits of England (*E. baccatus* and *armatus* Forbes); these differ, however, from the typical forms in several characters, which may justify a generic separation.

**[p. 423]**

The calyx plates lack the characteristic rib ornamentation; the large lateral opening (? anus, according to Forbes ovarial opening) lies high up between the 2nd and 3rd rows of side plates; on the raised rim next to the mouth opening in the summit there is a curved small slit ending on either side in a pore (ovarial opening, according to Forbes anus); the stem joints are not telescoped into one another, and the pore rhombs, which in the typical *Echinoencrinus* are flat and almost hidden by the sculpture, stand out on the British forms with a thickened rim and are differently shaped. They consist of 2 unequal, kidney-shaped halves which rest on 2 neighbouring small plates. Moreover, above the base there is always only one pore rhomb, while near the summit 1–2 of them are present.

**Glyptocystites** Billings (*Cheirocrinus* Eichw.). Calyx cylindrical or egg-shaped, consisting of 4 zones of striated plates. B [basals] 4 (of which 3 pentagonal, the 4th hexagonal). The 2 following rows formed of 5 plates each, the uppermost of 5–6. ? Anal opening situated fairly low on the side, large, partly (or ? wholly) closed by numerous small plates. From the summit proceed 5 somewhat irregularly formed ambulacral furrows (recumbent arms according to Billings), set with alternating platelets, which are furnished with biserial pinn[ules]. Pore rhombs numerous (10–13), flat; the majority of them stand around the summit, 2–3 above the base. On the outer side of the plates the slits which form the rhomb usually leave a smooth space free in the middle; on the inner side, on the other hand, continuous slits are always present. Stem short, tapering downward, consisting of ring-shaped joints telescoped into one another. Lower Silurian. Canada, Russia.

**Cystoblastus** Volborth. Egg-shaped to spherical, composed of 4 zones of plates (4 + 5 + 5 + 5). Those of the second lateral zone are fork-shaped notched above and embrace 5 broad ambulacral fields proceeding from the summit, which form a five-rayed star. In the middle of these a furrow runs to the mouth; besides this there are pores between the side plates (probably at the base of the pinn[ules]). An (? anal) opening at about the middle height of the calyx. Only 2 complete pore rhombs are found above the base; on the other hand the 5 forked side plates show at the edge of the ambulacral fields a transverse striation produced by pore slits, and likewise the trapezoidal plates of the uppermost zone are halved by a ridge, beside which pore stripes appear on either side. Besides the central mouth and the larger lateral opening there is also a small pore in the corner where 2 ambulacral fields meet. Lower Silurian (Vaginatenkalk) Russia. *C. Leuchtenbergi* Volb.

**Blastoidocrinus** Billings. Incompletely known. Bud-shaped, stalked, formed of 5 fork-shaped notched basals and a few summit plates. From the central mouth proceed 5 narrow, straight ambulacral fields consisting of two rows of small plates, with median grooves. On the outer **[p. 424]** margins of these are attachment points for pinnulae. The solid basals are accompanied at their notched edge by a row of transverse pore stripes. Lower Silurian.

**? Rhombifera** Barr. Body elongate-pyramidal, three-edged, short-stalked, incompletely known. B. 3. On the sides, one above the other, are 2 very large pore rhombs each, whose long diagonals run parallel to the principal axis of the calyx. These appear to belong to 2 plates, whose sutures are indicated on the internal casts by a line in the long diagonal. Each 2 opposite pores are connected by horizontal slits. Lower Silurian (Étage D Barr.) Bohemia.

**Codonaster** F. Roem. (*Codaster* M'Coy). Calyx ovoid-conical, blunt-edged, truncated at the summit, consisting of 3 unequal basals, 5 large side plates and 5 summit plates. Summit five-sided, with 5 pseudoambulacral fields proceeding from the central mouth, which form a star. In 4 interambulacral fields one sees wedge-shaped, three-sidedly pointed, smooth, raised or depressed pyramids, whose base begins in the mouth corners; in the 5th, smooth, field lies the anal opening. The lateral margins of the ambulacral fields are each bordered by a row of small platelets (between which there are no pores). Each 2 of the interambulacral wedges described above bound a hydrospire, whose slits run parallel to the pseudoambulacral furrows. The two hydrospires lying nearest the anus are only half developed. Devonian and Carboniferous limestone.

This genus agrees completely in the composition of the calyx with the Blastoideen and was also always reckoned there, until Billings, on account of the well-developed hydrospires in the summit, placed it with the Cystoideen.

Fig. 299. *Codonaster acutus* M'Coy. Carboniferous limestone. Derbyshire. a calyx from the side, b from below (nat. size), c from above, enlarged.

As insufficiently known genera of Cystoideen there would still be to be cited: *Lichenoides* and *Trochocystites* Barr., *Protocystites* Salt., *Strobilocystites* White.

Fossils of wholly doubtful position, which are as a rule attached to the Cystoideen, are:

**Lichenocrinus** Hall. Disc-shaped, grown parasitically by its underside on foreign bodies, without arms or pinnulae; upper side flatly arched, somewhat depressed in the middle, with a proboscis-shaped process in the centre. Body composed of numerous small, irregularly arranged small plates. Proboscis perforated and consisting of 5 alternating rows of small plates. Underside with radial striae proceeding from the centre, which correspond to vertical radial lamellae in the interior. Lower Silurian. Cincinnati.

**[p. 425]**

**Cyclocystoides** Salter and Billings. Lower Silurian. *Cyclocrinus* Eichw., *Dictyocrinus* Hall., *Pasceolus* Billings (cf. p. 84).

### Temporal and spatial distribution of the Cystoideen.

The Cystoideen form a totally extinct order of the Crinoideen, which reached the high point of its development in the Middle and Upper Silurian deposits and already disappears in the Coal Measures. They are found only in marine deposits, most frequently in calcareous or shaly rocks. Their state of preservation usually leaves much to be desired; most often calyces, fragments of calyces or single small plates occur, whereas the uncommonly delicate and weak arms and pinnulae are almost always lacking, and the stems too are as a rule separated from the calyces.

In general the Cystoideen do not belong among the very widely distributed fossils. A limited number of localities in Europe and North America has furnished almost all the material known up to now. The Thesaurus Siluricus of Bigsby enumerates 63 European and 56 North American Silurian species of Cystoideen, to which however 5 *Agelacrinus* and 7 *Caryocrinus* species are still to be added. From Devonian and Carboniferous beds about 12 species in all may be known.

The oldest forms come from the lowest fossiliferous beds: from the Cambrian formation (Lingula flags) of Wales and Shropshire (*Protocystites*, *Macrocystella*), further from the so-called Primordial stage of Bohemia and the Fichtelgebirge (*Lichenoides* and *Trochocystites* Barr., *Cystidea Bavarica* Barr. and 3 undetermined forms), as well as from the beds of the same age in Canada (*Palaeocystites* Bill.). All are imperfectly preserved and in part not yet more closely described.

The principal repository for Cystoideen is formed by the lower division of the Silurian formation. In Europe the Vaginatenkalk (Pleta) of the Russian Baltic provinces, especially in the immediate surroundings of St. Petersburg, furnishes the most numerous and best preserved specimens. The genera *Echinosphaerites*, *Sphaeronites*, *Caryocystites*, *Glyptosphaerites*, *Cryptocrinus*, *Hemicosmites*, *Echinoencrinus*, *Glyptocystites*, *Cystoblastus*, *Asteroblastus*, *Blastoidocrinus* and *Mesites* are distributed here. By far the commonest form among them is *Echinosphaerites*, which in Sweden too (Oeland, Ostgotland, Dalecarlia) completely fills individual beds of the Middle Silurian Orthoceras limestone, and which during the Diluvial period was carried in great numbers to the North German plain. Besides *Echinosphaerites*, Angelin mentions in Sweden also *Caryocystites*, *Holocystites*, *Sphaeronites*, *Eucystis*, *Glyptosphaerites*, *Gomphocystites*, *Glyptocystites* **[p. 426]** and *Lepadocrinus*. Several of these genera are also found in the Bala and Caradoc beds of Great Britain and Spain.

In Bohemia the genera *Echinosphaerites*, *Trochocystites* and *Rhombifera* are known from Étage D.

The Lower Silurian beds (Chazy and Trenton groups) yield near Montreal and Ottawa in Canada a rich harvest of Cystoideen from the genera *Pleurocystites*, *Glyptocystites*, *Comarocystites*, *Amydalocystites*, *Malocystites*, *Ateleocystites*, *Blastoidocrinus*, *Agelacrinus* and *Edrioaster*. In the same horizon forms from the same genera also occur in the states of New York, Ohio, Indiana and Tennessee.

In the Upper Silurian the Cystoideen become somewhat scarcer. From the Wenlock and Dudley beds of Great Britain 10 species of the genera *Lepadocrinus* (*Apiocystites*, *Pseudocrinus*), *Prunocystites*, *Echinoencrinus* and *Ateleocystites* are known; alongside these stand at least as many from the Niagara beds of Lockport and some other places in the State of New York, as well as from Racine in Wisconsin (Hall, 20th Report), from the genera *Caryocrinus*, *Lepadocrinus*, *Callocystites*, *Holocystites*, *Gomphocystites*.

Upper Silurian Cystoideen are moreover known from the states of Indiana, Illinois and Maryland.

In the Devonian formation only the genera *Tiaracrinus*, *Agelacrinus*, *Echinocystites*, *Ateleocystites* and *Codonaster*, in the Carboniferous limestone only *Hypocrinus*, *Agelacrinus*, *Lepadocrinus* and *Codonaster* are distributed, in a very limited number of species.

On the stem of the Crinoideen the Cystoideen represent the oldest and earliest withered side branch; they are however by no means the primitive forms from which all the other Crinoideen have developed, for they appear simultaneously with the Eucrinoideen, and the oldest more exactly known forms already bear all the characteristic marks of the order; indeed one may even say that precisely the most extreme types, which depart furthest from the Eucrinoideen, such as *Echinosphaerites*, *Glyptosphaerites*, *Caryocystites*, *Agelacrinus* etc., are among the earliest to appear. If therefore the Cystoideen, as already remarked, did indeed most rapidly attain a rich development of forms, they are nevertheless not absolutely older than the Eucrinoideen, for the latter likewise show a considerable number of representatives in the lower division of the Silurian formation.

Very closely linked with the Cystoideen in morphological respect are the Blastoideen, and since these also attach themselves to the former in their temporal development, the thought of a **[p. 427]** direct descent lies fairly near at hand. But since the Blastoideen depart furthest from the crinoid type that is most decidedly expressed in the Eucrinoideen, and betray manifold reminiscences of the Asteroideen and Echinoideen, the rank of the Cystoideen between the Eucrinoideen and Blastoideen is thereby also determined.

## 3rd Order. Blastoidea. Fleming. (1)

### Knospenstrahler.

**Bud- or egg-shaped, armless, short-stalked bodies of five-rayed structure. Calyx consisting of 13 regularly arranged principal pieces. Instead of arms, pseudoambulacral fields furnished with pinnules are present, beneath which on either side lie bundles of tubes (hydrospires), whose openings surround the central mouth in the summit.**

The first figure of a representative of this wholly extinct order was published by Parkinson under the designation "Kentucky asterial fossil"; this was followed in the year 1820 by a description of the most widespread genus (*Pentremites*) by the American zoologist Thomas Say. Fleming in the year 1828 first designated the fossils made known by Say as Blastoidea, without however furnishing an essential contribution to the knowledge of them. By far the most important

---

Shumard, B. F. and D. Owen. On 15 new species of Crinoidea. Journ. Acad. nat. scienc. Philadelphia 1850 new ser. vol. II.

Shumard, B. F. New species of Blastoidea. Transactions Acad. of Sciences of St. Louis vol. I p. 238.
