**[p. 428]**

and comprehensive work is owed to Ferd. Roemer, whose monograph of the Blastoidea treats in exhaustive fashion the morphological structure, the systematic position, the relationships of affinity, as well as the temporal and geographical distribution of all forms known up to the year 1850.

The most important part of the blastoid body, the calyx (calyx), most frequently possesses a bud-, pear- or egg-shaped form and is composed in the main of 13 intimately fused, solid calcareous plates, to which are added a variable number of small plates and calcareous fragments which are more loosely connected with the principal pieces and fall off more or less easily. A roomy body cavity, enclosed by the calyx wall, serves for the reception of the soft parts. The dorsal base (Fig. 300), resting directly on the stem, consists of three basal pieces, whose sutures meet in one point and here, in the middle of the insertion pit of the stem, leave free a fine opening for the entry of the central canal into the calyx. Two of these basals are alike, the third is differently shaped. As Lyon first demonstrated, between the 3 basal pieces and the uppermost stem joint there frequently lie 3 tiny infrabasals, which however do not alternate with the basals, but as it were merely represent sections of them. On the three-part base rest 5 equally large radials (fork pieces), deeply fork-notched at the upper margin, which as a rule form the greater part of the side wall of the calyx; the upper, third circlet of 5 mostly deltoid- or trapezium-shaped, interradially situated plates (deltoid or interradial pieces) is interrupted by the pseudoambulacral fields running between them; it takes part in the plating of the summit and in some forms (*Nucleocrinus*) reaches down beyond the middle of the sides. A considerable part of these interradial plates remains as a rule hidden beneath the neighbouring plates.

If the accessory pieces of the calyx have fallen out and the principal pieces alone are present, one sees 5 depressed, elongate-triangular fields, now broadly leaf-shaped, now narrower, sometimes almost linear (pseudoambulacral fields), proceeding from the summit in a radial direction, which end in the lower corner of the fork-shaped notch of the radial pieces (Fig. 301 aα). The obliquely sloping sides of these fields are

Fig. 300. Analysis of the calyx of *Pentremites florealis*.

**[p. 429]**

formed by the margins of the fork notch of the radials, as well as by the oblique lateral faces of the interradials, and are mostly transversely striated by numerous shallow grooves directed perpendicular to the long axis of the fields (Fig. 301a α, β). In the bottom of the depressed fields lie two bundles of tubes (Fig. 301a α), which appear compressed toward the outside and above and pressed against the lateral margins, but which

Fig. 301. a *Pentremites florealis* Say. Carboniferous limestone. Illinois. (Enlarged.) α pseudoambulacral field after removal of the lancet piece and the pore plates, with the tubes lying in the bottom of the field. β the same with the lancet piece preserved. γ the same with lancet piece, pore plates and transverse striation preserved. δ as γ, but with the transverse striation obliterated in the middle. ζ pseudoambulacral field covered with pinnules. b *Pentremites sulcatus* Say. Calyx cut through horizontally, in order to show the tubes of the pseudoambulacral fields in cross-section (enlarged).

widen toward the inside and meet in the middle of the field, there forming a depressed groove. These tubes, first demonstrated by F. Roemer, run in a straight line from the lower tip of the pseudoambulacral fields toward the summit; they are strongly compressed laterally and in cross-section look like a deeply folded, fine calcareous membrane (Fig. 301b). As such they are also described by Billings; however, the bundles consist not of open folds but of tubes closed all round, as Roemer had already more correctly represented. The number of tubes of each bundle varies according to the species between 2–10 each. Most frequently 5–6 each seem to occur.

When the calyx is completely preserved, each pseudoambulacrum presents not a depressed but a flat field, apparently covered by a single transversely striated piece. Only through weathering do sutures emerge on the surface, from which a rather complicated composition of the same becomes evident. The middle of the pseudoambulacral fields is occupied in the typical forms (*Pentremites*, *Granatocrinus*) by a mostly narrow, lanceolate, **[p. 430]** sometimes also broadly leaf-shaped, downwardly pointed, undivided piece, which however, through a depressed median groove, appears to consist of two halves, and which reaches from the summit opening to the lower tip of the field. At its upper end this "lancet piece" (Fig. 301a β and 302) is provided with a short process by which it inserts itself between the deltoid pieces and forms the summit ring together with them.

The lancet piece does not occupy the whole pseudoambulacral field, but leaves narrower or broader grooves free on both sides, which are filled by a great number of narrow transverse pieces which are ranged closely against one another like upright-standing bricks. Since the side grooves narrow downward, the vertical side view of the marginal pieces shows an unequally three-sided form. Where these narrow platelets apply themselves to the solid calyx, they narrow so far into a slender stalk that between two neighbouring marginal pieces a slit-shaped pore opening always arises, and thus the margins of the pseudoambulacral fields appear bordered on each side by a row of pores. F. Roemer has therefore called the marginal platelets "pore pieces". The slit pores correspond to the above-mentioned vertical grooves on the bevelled bounding surfaces of the pseudoambulacral fields and lead down to the bottom of these, where on each side they lead to the tube bundles. Behind each slit pore a very small "supplementary pore piece" further inserts itself between the narrowed stalks of the marginal plates and thereby narrows the opening of the pores (Fig. 302). These small platelets easily fall out in weathered specimens, and now and then seem to be wholly absent.

The structure of the pseudoambulacral fields described so far is found in the typical species of the genus *Pentremites*, but can undergo considerable modifications. Thus the lancet pieces in some *Pentremites*, as well as in all *Granatocrinus* and *Orophocrinus* species, become almost linearly narrow and can be entirely covered by the pore pieces, so that they only come to light after removal of the latter. The supplementary pore pieces now and then almost attain the size of the pore pieces.

In the genus *Nucleocrinus* the pore pieces lie not at the margin but in the middle of the pseudoambulacral fields and enclose an extremely narrow lancet piece. Outside the pore pieces there are

Fig. 302. Lancet piece of *Pentremites*, enlarged, with the median furrow, faintly transversely striated, beset on both sides with pore pieces. The supplementary pore pieces have partly fallen out on the left side.

**[p. 431]**

in this genus on either side transversely striated accessory plates, which fill out the broad pseudoambulacral field. These accessory plates, which are lacking in the other Blastoideen, consist according to Billings perhaps of numerous closely connected upright transverse small plates.

Fig. 303. a A pinnule of *Pentremites*, enlarged. b *Granatocrinus Norwoodi* with completely preserved pinnules (after Meek and Worthen).

Some excellently preserved specimens of *Pentremites* and *Granatocrinus* show that the pseudoambulacral fields of the Blastoideen were completely covered with fine, thread-like, jointed pinnules (Fig. 303a, ζ). These stand above the pore pieces and are directed upward, overtopping the calyx roof (Fig. 303b). Tentacle furrows have not been observed on their ventral side.

In the summit of *Pentremites*, *Granatocrinus* and *Eleutherocrinus* one sees, in the usually occurring state of preservation (Fig. 301a), 6 openings: a five-rayed one in the centre and 5 above the interradial deltoid pieces, exactly in the angle where two pseudoambulacra meet. One of these peripheral openings is distinguished by its considerably greater size and somewhat different shape from the 4 others. All appear simple on cursory examination; if however the nearest pore pieces have fallen out through weathering, one sees clearly that each of the 4 smaller openings is divided by one, the fifth larger one by two vertical partitions (Fig. 301a). In the latter one accordingly distinguishes a middle, larger and two lateral openings, in all the others two openings. The paired openings are now nothing other than the outlets of the tube bundles lying beneath the pore pieces of the pseudoambulacra. But since in the summit the upper corners of two neighbouring pseudoambulacra meet, one half of each summit opening belongs to the right-hand, the other to the left-hand tube bundle of the adjoining radial fields. In *Nucleocrinus* all the openings are completely separate, so that one distinguishes clearly one excentric interradial and ten equal paired tube openings. In *Orophocrinus*

Fig. 304. Summit of *Orophocrinus* with slit-shaped openings beside the pseudoambulacral fields (somewhat enlarged).

**[p. 432]**

the tube openings are replaced by 10 long slits, which run downward from the summit on the outer side of the pseudoambulacral fields (Fig. 304).

The central summit opening is in *Nucleocrinus* always closed by small plates, in the other genera almost always open. Only in some magnificently preserved specimens of *Pentremites* from the American Carboniferous limestone (Fig. 305a) did all the summit openings appear covered with small plates, which evidently lay on only loosely and have therefore almost always fallen out. In *Granatocrinus* (Fig. 305b) the central opening is closed by numerous small plates, the larger of the 5 peripheral openings by one large plate; the 4 others stand open.

At the base of the blastoid calyces there is, with the exception of *Eleutherocrinus*, a round insertion place for a stem, but only extremely rarely have calyces been met with together with their stem (Fig. 303b); isolated round, fairly high stem joints pierced by a round central canal, on the other hand, are found abundantly in the beds which harbour calyces of Blastoideen.

Since at the present day no Echinodermen exist which can be compared with the Blastoideen, we are still far removed from a satisfactory understanding of the organisational relations of this order. Almost all parts and organs are subject to different interpretations. According to Billings, e.g., the 3 basal pieces are parabasals and the fork pieces are not radials. The pseudoambulacral fields may best be interpreted, like the ambulacra of the Cystoideen, as arms bent back and fused with the calyx. Their base would then be situated in the summit, their tip in the fork end of the radials; the lancet piece might be interpreted as the homologue of the dorsal plates of the crinoid arm, the pore pieces as marginal platelets. Wholly peculiar structures are the tube bundles of the Blastoideen (Fig. 306), which communicate with the pores of the pseudoambulacra and attach themselves on the one hand to the lower margin of the lancet piece, on the other to that of the fork piece. Billings has drawn attention to their agreement with the pore slits and rhomb tubes of the Cystoideen and has designated the tube bundles of the Blastoideen as hydrospires, their openings

Fig. 305. a *Pentremites sulcatus* with completely preserved summit, somewhat enlarged (after Billings). b Summit of *Granatocrinus*, enlarged (a anal opening closed by a plate, r radials, sp openings of the hydrospires).

**[p. 433]**

in the summit as spiracula. E. Forbes and Ferd. Roemer compare the paired summit openings with the genital slits of the Ophiuriden and Euryaliden and interpret the tube apparatus as receptacles of the ovaries and as oviducts. Hub. Ludwig (1) holds the hydrospires of the Blastoideen to be homologous with the genital bursae of the Ophiuriden, which probably function at the same time as respiratory organs.

The unpaired, always interradially situated opening, which is isolated in *Nucleocrinus* and *Orophocrinus* and in *Pentremites* and *Granatocrinus* is provided with two fused genital openings, is evidently the anus. Billings holds it to be mouth and anus at once. Most authors, on the other hand, recognise the mouth in the central summit opening, which however, as in the Eucrinoidea Tesselata, lay subtegminally beneath a plated roof. Billings has observed in the 5 mouth corners, which lie at the upper end of the median furrows of the lancet pieces, simple pores (Fig. 305a), which probably served as points of entry of the ambulacral vessel and of the alimentary canal beneath the mouth roof.

From the egg-shaped, now and then even almost spherical form of the Blastoideen and from the similarity of the pseudoambulacral fields to the ambulacra of the Echiniden, a conclusion as to the relationship of the two groups of Echinodermen was formerly sought to be drawn. But these supposed relations are of a wholly external nature. The whole construction of the calyx and of the pseudoambulacral fields, as well as the presence of jointed pinnulae, points to the Crinoideen and among these to the Cystoideen. With the Cystoideen the Blastoideen share several important characters. The pseudoambulacral fields evidently correspond to the recumbent arms fused to the calyx, and the tube bundles beneath the pore pieces to the hydrospires of the Cystoideen. Mouth and anus too have a similar position in both orders. The principal difference thus rests solely on the nature of the genital openings and on the circumstance that the hydrospires in the Blastoideen communicate with the pores of the pseudoambulacral fields. Through

Fig. 306. *Pentremites sulcatus* Say, from the Carboniferous limestone of Illinois. Calyx cut through horizontally, at about the lower third of the pseudoambulacral fields, and enlarged 1½ times. hy hydrospires, l lancet pieces, p pore pieces, r radial pieces (fork pieces).

---
(1) Morphologische Studien an Echinodermen Bd. I. Zur Anatomie der Ophiuriden S. 283.

**[p. 434]**

the arrangement of the genital openings and through the total atrophy of the arms the Blastoideen place themselves, among all Crinoideen, nearest alongside the Ophiuriden.

The 5 typical genera known up to now, to which the doubtful *Stephanocrinus* is added, lived in the sea and were, with the exception of *Eleutherocrinus*, stalked. Of their developmental history nothing is known so far.

**Pentremites** Say (*Pentatrematites* F. Roemer, *Troostocrinus* Shumard, *Tricoelocrinus* Meek and Worth.) (Fig. 307). Calyx bud-, pear-, club-, egg-shaped, or truncated five-edged, stalked; consisting of 13 principal plates. B 3, unequal; the 5 fork pieces equally large, strongly developed. Interradial deltoid pieces rather small, forming the summit. Pseudoambulacral fields of varying breadth, rarely reaching down to the basals. In the summit 6 openings. Stem round, short and thin. Upper Silurian. Devonian. Principal distribution in the Carboniferous limestone.

Ferd. Roemer divides the genus *Pentremites*, according to the external form of the calyx and the pseudoambulacra, into the following groups:

1. *Floreales*. Calyx spherical or pear-shaped. Pseudoambulacra of broadly lanceolate form, not reaching down to the base. *P. florealis* Say. *P. sulcatus* Roem. etc.

2. *Elliptici* (= *Granatocrinus* Troost). Calyx ellipsoidal. Pseudoambulacra narrow, linear, reaching to the base. B tiny, sunken. *P. ellipticus* Sow., *P. Norwoodi* M. and W., *P. oblongus* Sow. etc.

3. *Truncati* (= *Pentremitidia* d'Orb.). Calyx top-shaped with truncated summit. Pseudoambulacra short, broad, situated only on the truncated upper side. B very high. *P. Pailleti* Vern., *P. Schultzii* Vern.

4. *Clavati*. Calyx club-shaped, summit pointed by a five-faced pyramid whose edges are occupied by the narrow, linear pseudoambulacra. Only species *P. Reinwardti*.

The genus *Troostocrinus* was established by Shumard for Pentremiten with a three-edged base and very narrow ambulacral fields; for those forms of this group in which the lateral faces of the broad base are deeply hollowed out, Meek and Worthen proposed the name *Tricoelocrinus*.

**Granatocrinus** Troost (Fig. 308). Elliptical, egg-shaped or spherical. Calyx composed as in the preceding genus, but B small, sunken, not visible from the side. Fork pieces relatively small, reaching to about half the height. Deltoid pieces large. Pseudoambulacral fields narrow, linear, running down to the base of the body. Carboniferous limestone. *Gr. ellipticus*, *Norwoodi* etc.

**Orophocrinus** v. Seebach (*Codonites* Meek and Worthen). (Fig. 309.) Bud-shaped. Calyx as in *Pentremites*, but the anal opening isolated, somewhat

Fig. 307. *Pentremites florealis* Say. Carboniferous limestone. Illinois. Nat. size.

**[p. 435]**

removed from the summit in an interambulacral field. The genital openings are long slits, which begin in the summit and run along the outer margin of the pseudoambulacral fields. Carboniferous limestone. *O.* (*Pentremites*) *stelliformis* Owen and Shumard.

Fig. 308. *Granatocrinus Norwoodi* Owen and Shumard sp. Carboniferous limestone. Burlington, Iowa. a specimen with well-preserved pinnules (nat. size), b summit, enlarged. (After Meek and Worthen.)

Fig. 309. *Orophocrinus* (*Codonites*) *stelliformis* Owen and Shum. sp. Carboniferous limestone. Burlington, Iowa. a specimen in nat. size. b summit, enlarged. (After Meek and Worthen.)

**Nucleocrinus** Conrad (*Olivanites* Troost, *Elaeacrinus* Ferd. Roem.). Calyx elliptical or egg-shaped, seated on a thin, round stem. B 3, tiny, covered by the uppermost stem joint. The 5 weakly notched, likewise small fork pieces form the outer base of the calyx, whose sides are formed by 5 very long and narrow interradial deltoid pieces and 5 broad, transversely striated pseudoambulacral fields. One of the deltoid pieces is broader than the rest and bears the anal opening in the summit. The plates of the pseudoambulacral fields are so intimately fused among themselves and with the interradials that scarcely any sutures remain visible. The lancet piece is extremely narrow, almost linear, bordered on both sides by a row of pore pieces. Outside these the field is filled on each side by a fairly broad, transversely striated plate (perhaps consisting of numerous transverse pieces). The hydrospires lie in the middle of the pseudoambulacra and consist on each side of only 2 tubes. Genital openings in the summit 10, distinctly separate, between them the anal opening. Central opening (mouth) plated. Devonian and Carboniferous limestone in North America. *N. Verneuili* Troost sp.

**Eleutherocrinus** Shumard and Yandell. Elliptical, three-edged, wholly unequal-sided, unstalked; summit somewhat truncated. Four linear pseudoambulacra run on one half of the calyx (front side) down to near the base; the fifth, broad and short, lies horizontally in the summit and occupies half of it (hind side). Of the 3 basal plates the one situated on the front side is very small, whereas the two of the hind side reach to half the height of the calyx. The 4 fork pieces of the front side form almost the entire calyx wall and enclose in the summit the small interradial deltoid pieces. The fifth fork piece occupies the whole surface of the dorsal side lying above the two basals, is broad, and only shallowly notched above. Mouth central. The 4 (or 8) genital openings (spiracula) of the front side **[p. 436]** as in *Pentremites*, those of the hind pseudoambulacrum separate, in the upper lateral corners. Devonian. Kentucky and New York. *E. Cassedayi* Shum.

### Appendix.

Genus of doubtful position:

**Stephanocrinus** Conrad (1) (Fig. 310). Calyx angular, cup-shaped, narrowed downward. B 3, high, somewhat unequal. R 5 × 1, large, very deeply notched above, so that the calyx margin appears adorned with 5 thorn-shaped, far-projecting processes, between which lie 5 depressed fields. In the bottom of the notches there are radial furrows, which are covered by two elongate, easily detached, narrow plates. The central subtegminal mouth is closed by 5 large triangular oral plates. Anus small, almost marginal, covered by 5 small valves. Stem round, thin.

On an excellently preserved specimen J. Hall (Geol. New York vol. II p. 351) has observed fine, thread-like pinn[ules], which are attached at the margin, "along a shallow groove in the base of the depression" between the thorn-like processes that adorn the upper margin. "These tentacles or fingers consist of 10 branches, each of which is composed above of a double row of small plates, but which below unite into a row of fused platelets which possess a different arrangement." The pinnulae completely cover the 5 radial fields. Upper Silurian (Niagara group). Lockport, New York.

This genus was placed by F. Roemer with the Cystoideen on account of the supposed lack of arms and the presence of an ovarial pyramid. The latter, however, is evidently an anal opening, and through the discovery of the pinnula-like arms the first reason has also fallen away. Nevertheless the position of *Stephanocrinus* remains very doubtful. Against the Cystoideen speak the lack of hydrospires, the regular structure of the calyx and the peculiar development of the pinnulae. The greatest agreement, both in the construction of the calyx and in the nature of the pinnulae, is shown by the Blastoideen, among which the genus *Stephanocrinus*, despite its lack of genital tubes, may provisionally be lodged.

Fig. 310. *Stephanocrinus angulatus* Conrad. Upper Silurian. Lockport, New York. a calyx in nat. size. b calyx roof enlarged, with broken-off tips.

### Temporal and spatial distribution.

Of the three orders of Crinoideen the small group of the Blastoideen appears latest. The oldest form (*Pentr. Reinwardti*

---
(1) Roemer, Ferd. Ueber *Stephanocrinus*. Troschel's Archiv für Naturgeschichte 1850 S. 365.

**[p. 437]**

Troost) comes from Upper Silurian deposits of the State of Tennessee and is up to now (besides *Stephanocrinus*) the only certain representative of our order in the Silurian formation. The Blastoideen become more frequent in Devonian deposits. The region of Gerolstein and Prüm in the Eifel, Nassau and Sabrero in Asturias have furnished 5 mostly top-shaped Pentremiten with truncated summit (group of the *Truncati*). From North America about 8 Devonian *Pentremites* (and *Granatocrinus*), 5 *Nucleocrinus* and 2 *Eleutherocrinus* species are known, which come from the western parts of the State of New York, from Kentucky, Ohio and Indiana. The principal development of the Blastoideen falls in the Coal Measures, where they at the same time also become extinct. In Europe (Belgium, Yorkshire, Derbyshire, Ireland, Ratingen) they remain, however, isolated rare occurrences and are without any influence on the total character of the fauna; on the other hand the genera *Pentremites* and *Granatocrinus* play in North America, and indeed chiefly in the drainage basin of the Mississippi (in the states of Missouri, Kentucky, Indiana, Iowa, Illinois, Tennessee and Alabama), through their abundance and favourable preservation so important a role that there in the Upper Carboniferous limestone they belong among the most characteristic index fossils. Bigsby enumerates in the Thesaurus devonico-carbonicus no fewer than 36 Carboniferous *Pentremites* and 12 *Granatocrinus* species from North America, whereas the two genera in Europe are represented by only 16, mostly rare, species. Of *Orophocrinus* and *Elaeocrinus* only a few forms are known from North America.

## II. Class. Asteroidea. Seesterne (1).

To the Asteroideen one reckons, besides the star-shaped or pentagonal disc-shaped true Seesterne, also the so-called Schlangensterne [brittle stars] (Ophiuridae) and the

---

A. Works of more general content.

**[p. 438]**

Euryaleen or Medusenhäupter [Medusa's heads] provided with many-branched arms. They are at once distinguished from the Crinoideen by the lack of a stem, by the possession of unjointed, spine- or wart-shaped skin appendages, and above all by certain internal skeletal formations standing in connection with the ambulacral system, as well as by the restriction of the ambulacral system to the lower side of the body. In the Crinoideen mouth and anus always lie in the upwardly directed calyx roof; in the Asteroideen the mouth is invariably found in the centre of the lower side (ventral side), while the anus, if it is present at all, finds its place on the upper (antiambulacral) dorsal side. In every Asteroiden body one can distinguish a central disc and 5 or more radially elongated processes (arms, rays). In the disc the viscera (central water vessel, nerve ring, genital organs, intestine etc.) are chiefly lodged, yet these organs in part send processes out into the rays. Thus, for example, in the central ring of the water-vascular system there unite
