Quenstedt, H. B. Petrefactenkunde Deutschlands Bd. IV. Asteriden und Encriniden 1874–76.

**[p. 439]**

always 5 or more principal vessels, which run in a radial direction on the lower side of the rays or arms as far as their outermost tips, and these are in turn accompanied by corresponding radial cords of the nervous system. The radial vessels named lie one above the other in the bottom of a ventral ambulacral groove (which in the Stelleriden is open, in the Ophiuriden is covered by a row of skin shields) and are surrounded or roofed over on their dorsal side by two rows of calcareous ambulacral pieces connected in the middle. Apart from this internal skeleton the Asteroidea also possess a more or less developed, very variously constituted dermal skeleton, whose individual pieces are seldom immovably connected by sutures and therefore easily fall apart after the death of the animals. For this reason well-preserved, complete fossil remains of Asteroideen belong among the rarer occurrences. The conditions of preservation are still most favourable in the Ophiuriden, where the small plates of the dermal skeleton lie close beside one another and form a closed envelope. In the true Seesterne, on the other hand, one has mostly to do with isolated plates and only exceptionally with complete specimens.

In few divisions of the animal kingdom is palaeontology so dependent on the knowledge of the living forms as in the Asteroideen. Their fossil representatives offer in general no striking differences from their present-day descendants; indeed they even belong for the most part to the same genera, and only the genera of the Palaeolithic age form, as regards the structure of their ambulacral furrows, a somewhat foreign group.

The first complete fossil Seestern, from the Quadersandstein of Pirna, was described in 1760 by Schultze. There followed figures of scattered Jurassic, Triassic and Cretaceous species by Guettard, Walch, Saussure, Parkinson, Münster, Goldfuss, Hagenow and others. More comprehensive works on fossil Asteroideen are owed chiefly to J. Hall, E. Billings, Edw. Forbes and Thom. Wright.

## 1st Order. Ophiuridae. Schlangensterne (1).

The Ophiuriden are anus-less Seesterne with long, thin, round or flattened arms, which are clothed all round with shields and are sharply set off from a central disc containing the viscera.

---
(1) For living Ophiuridae compare, besides the literature named on p. 437:
Lütken. Addidamenta ad historiam Ophiuridarum. K. danske Vitensk. selskabs skrifter. Bd. V. 1859–61. I. u. II. Afd. Bd. VIII. 1869. III. Afd.

**[p. 440]**

The ventral side of the arms lacks an ambulacral furrow; on the other hand, between the so-called lateral and ventral shields two rows of ambulacral feet (tentacles) emerge from pores. Slit-shaped genital openings are found beside the arms on the ventral side of the disc, and likewise the madreporite, if it is present at all, lies on the underside. The five-cleft mouth occupies the middle of the disc; intestine and genital organs, as well as the central water-vascular ring, are restricted to the disc.

Whereas in the true Seesterne the arms represent only lateral prolongations of the disc and, like it, contain viscera, in the Ophiuriden they are almost entirely filled by a series of vertebra-like, upright discs (Fig. 311), at whose base merely the water vessel and a nerve cord run in a groove. Each vertebra consists of two immovably connected, equal halves (ambulacral plates, vertebral halves) and represents in general a disc thickened in the middle and sharpened toward the edges. Both the

Fig. 311. a three vertebrae of an Ophiuren arm seen from the side and enlarged (x exit opening of the water-vessel branch, below it the point of re-entry; y pit for the intervertebral muscle). b a vertebra from the adoral side, surrounded by the dermal skeleton (w position of the water-vessel trunk, v ventral shield, l lateral shields, d back or dorsal shield). c internal mouth skeleton of an Ophiure together with two arm pieces still lying within the disc, from the underside (enlarged). Beside the arms are the genital slits bounded by two ridges; the darkly shaded pentagonal line indicates the groove for the central nerve ring.

---
Lyman, Th. Ophiuridae and Astrophytidae. Illustrated catalogue of the Museum of comp. Zoology No. I. 1865, nebst Supplem. 1871. II. 1875.
Lyman, Th. Ophiuridae and Astrophytidae new and old. Bull. of the Mus. of comp. Zoology vol. III. 1874.
Lyman, Th. Ophiuridae and Astrophytidae of the Challenger Expedition part I. Ib. 1878.

**[p. 441]**

"adoral" faces of the vertebrae, turned toward the mouth, and the "aboral" faces, turned toward the tip of the arm, are provided in their thickened middle part with joint-like knobs and depressions, and by means of these are movably fitted to one another. The interspace between the vertebrae is filled by muscular substance. The ventral water vessel sends out in each vertebra, on the aboral side, two fine lateral tubes, which pierce the vertebral halves symmetrically, then turn downward and on the underside, in lateral notches, thicken into ambulacral feet or tentacles, which reach the surface through pores between the skin shields. Toward the tips of the arms the vertebrae assume, through elongation, a cylindrical form; into the disc, on the other hand, they enter unchanged (Fig. 311c), and only the first two vertebrae assume, through the expansion and alteration of their divergent and no longer connected halves, a different constitution and, together with some other calcareous pieces, form the internal mouth skeleton (Fig. 311c). Here the two halves of the first vertebrae of two neighbouring arms abut laterally on one another and form the mouth corners. The halves of the first vertebra Joh. Müller designates as the "peristomial", those of the second as the "ambulacral" pieces of the mouth corners. On the underside of these two pieces the central water vessel runs in a groove. In the formation of the mouth, however, several other pieces also take part, which belong partly to the internal, partly to the external skeleton. Thus in each mouth corner there lies either a single jaw plate beset with teeth (maxilla, torus angularis), or a row of smaller platelets which take its place. To the external skeleton belong the interradial mouth shields (scuta buccalia) (Fig. 312a), important in systematic respect, situated between the projecting mouth corners; further, small, narrow pieces situated between the mouth slits and the mouth shield, the side mouth shields (scutella adoralia, or second adambulacral pieces, Fig. 312b), as well as a small shield, not always present, situated beside the mouth corner (scutellum orale, first adambulacral piece, Fig. 312c). Both the mouth corners (jaws) and the mouth slits are serrated by tooth-like processes or armed with short movable spines or warts (tooth papillae and mouth papillae).

Fig. 312. Part of the underside of the central disc of *Ophioglypha*. a mouth shield (scutum buccale). b side mouth shield (scutellum adorale). c mouth-corner piece (scutellum orale). The two scut. oralia are beset with mouth papillae. g genital slit. h lateral shields (scutella lateralia). i pores for the exit of the ambulacral feet, beset with small scales. k spines.

**[p. 442]**

The typical Ophiuren possess as a rule 5 simple long arms; in some Euryaliden, on the other hand, these branch into numerous side branches rolled in toward the mouth, all of which show the same structure as the main arms. A leathery outer skin envelops the vertebral series of the arms of the Euryaliden. In the typical Ophiuren, on the other hand, each arm is surrounded by 4 rows of skin shields, which lie close against one another and form a regularly and neatly plated surface. The dorsal shields (scutella dorsalia) form a single unpaired row; against them on each side lies a row of lateral shields (Lateralschilder, adambulacral shields, scutella lateralia), which everywhere bear a comb of shorter or longer spines and in part also take part in the formation of the underside. The ventral shields (superambulacral shields Joh. Müll., Ventralschilder, subambulacral shields Ludwig) cover the water vessel and are bordered by the lateral shields. Beside each ventral shield there is on the ventral side, on either side, a hole for the passage of a thread-like tentacle, which is frequently partly closed by one or more scale-shaped valves (squamae tentaculares).

Within the disc the underside of the arms remains, at least as regards the ventral shields and part of the lateral shields, almost unchanged as far as the mouth. The interspaces (interbrachial fields) between two arms are either covered with scales or granulated, sometimes even spiny. Immediately beside the arms there are in each interbrachial space 2 (rarely 4) genital slits, which are bounded on either side by calcareous ridges. In fossil specimens these openings, important in systematic respect, can only be found under especially favourable preservation. One of the mouth shields is frequently provided with a knob and functions as madreporite.

On the upper side of the disc (Fig. 313) the scaly or granulated skin conceals the arms lying beneath it; the plating of the latter thereby also breaks off abruptly at the disc. Sometimes on the dorsal side 5 pairs of shields on both sides of the beginning of the arms (scuta radialia, radial shields) are distinguished by their size or the special nature of their surface. Anus and pedicellariae are lacking in the Ophiuriden.

From ontogeny only the fact has practical interest for the palaeontologist that in the

Fig. 313. Upper side of a part of the disc of *Ophioglypha*. a radial shield (scutum radiale). b dorsal shield. c lateral shield.

**[p. 443]**

Euryaliden the branched arms develop out of originally simple, undivided ones.

The genera of the Ophiuriden are distinguished chiefly according to the number of genital slits and the nature of the disc, the mouth and the arms. Unfortunately, however, the fossil forms only rarely give information on all the more important systematic characters; frequently only the upper or the lower side of the body is known, or the preserved parts are indistinct, so that they do not permit an exact comparison with living forms. In many cases our knowledge is even limited to the crude impression of the body. For these reasons there is a whole series of fossil forms which must be lumped together under the collective name *Ophiura* until a lucky find makes their more exact determination possible.

One distinguishes, as already remarked, two suborders (Euryaleae and Ophiureae), which are already represented in the Silurian period by fossil remains.

### 1st Suborder. Euryaleae.

**Arms simple or more often branched, rolled in toward the mouth, suited for grasping, surrounded not by shields but by a granulated and very finely scaly skin. Genital slits often resolved into rows of pores. One or also several madreporites present on the underside.**

Here belong the living genera *Astrophyton* Linck (*Euryale* Lam.), *Trichaster* Ag., *Astronyx* M. & Tr. etc., and further, most probably, the following fossil forms.

**Eucladia** Woodward (Geol. Mag. 1869 p. 241). Body consisting of a granulated disc and 5 round arms, each of which is provided with 5 pairs of strong round side branches. The first pair of side arms springs off close behind the large leaf-shaped oral plates. Arms and side branches taper gradually, are covered with small rough scales and probably bore spines on the sides. Simple madreporite on the underside large. Upper Silurian. England.

**Onychaster** Meek and Worthen (Fig. 314). The body consists of a small, round disc and 5 simple, movable, thin, terete arms, which are as a rule rolled in toward the mouth side and intertwined with one another at their ends. Both the arched dorsal side of the disc and that of the arms is covered with a skin beset with small warts, which under the lens proves to be composed of tiny, scale-like small plates. The inwardly directed ventral side of the arms is beset on each side with a row of spines. If the scaly epidermis is removed, it is seen that the **[p. 444]** arms contain rows of vertebrae, which consist of two fused halves, articulate with one another and are pierced below the middle by a canal. Mouth side of the disc known only from within. Around the round central opening stands a threefold circlet of small plates: innermost 5 interpalmar oral platelets (?), upon these follow in the direction of the arms 5 pairs of smaller, and upon these 5 pairs of large, thick, prominent plates pierced by 2 pores, to which the arm vertebrae then attach. Ventral side of the arms unknown.

The only species of this remarkable genus (*O. flexilis* M. and W.) is found in the Carboniferous limestone (Keokuk group) of Illinois and Indiana.

The genera *Onychaster* and *Eucladia* are the only fossil forms which can be placed with some certainty among the Euryaliden. Possibly there also belong here crude impressions with forked arms from the Angulaten-Sandstein of Nürtingen in Würtemberg, which Quenstedt describes as *Euryale liasica* (Petrefaktenkunde Deutschl. IV. S. 86 Taf. 93 Fig. 6).

Fig. 314. *Onychaster flexilis* Meek and Worthen, from the Carboniferous limestone (Keokuk group). Crawfordsville, Indiana. (After Meek and Worthen.) a specimen in nat. size with folded-together arms; the roof of the disc is removed, so that one sees the internal mouth skeleton from above; likewise the arms lack the granulated skin near the disc. b mouth skeleton from within (enlarged). c an arm vertebra (enlarged).

### 2nd Suborder. Ophiureae.

**Arms simple, unbranched, suited for creeping, covered all round with 4 rows of shields. In the angles of the projecting mouth corners lie 5 mouth shields.**

a) Palaeozoic genera of doubtful position, mostly incompletely known. On the underside the ambulacral pieces appear not to be covered by ventral shields; they are similar to the ambulacral plates of the Stelleriden and their halves are not completely fused in the middle.

**Protaster** Forbes. Disc circular, with small scaly platelets. Arms thin, long; upper side with 2 small plates, which are ornamented with transverse and longitudinal furrows and are beset laterally with spines. Underside with 2 rows of elongate ambulacral plates, which are bounded on either side by a row of spine-bearing plates. Mouth star-shaped. Madreporite present. Lower and Upper Silurian of Great Britain, Russia and North America. Also in the Carboniferous limestone of North America. *P. Miltoni* Forbes.

**Eugaster** Hall. Star-shaped, with central disc and 5 long, thin, curved arms. Ventral side of the disc covered with small warty and radiately striated **[p. 445]** platelets. Arms with a double row of alternating, four-sided ambulacral plates and on either side a row of spiny adambulacral plates, which at the same time form the margin. Oral plates 10. The pores are situated at the connecting corners of the ambulacral plates and adambulacral plates. Upper side unknown. Devonian. New York.

**Taeniaster** Billings. Five-rayed; disc tiny; arms long, thin, flexible, beset above with small spines. Ambulacral furrows with 2 rows of pores. Ambulacral plates narrowed in the middle, widened on both sides; adambulacral plates elongate, obliquely inclined outward and somewhat overlapping one another. Lower Silurian. Canada.

**Ptilonaster** Hall. Upper Silurian. North America.

b) Typical Ophiuren.

1. With four genital slits in each interbrachial space.

**Ophioderma** Müll. and Trosch. (*Palaeocoma* p. p. d'Orb., *Ophiurella* p. p. Ag.). Upper side of the disc finely granulated. Arms long, smooth, round; lateral shields beset with short papillae or spines. Scuta buccalia undivided. In each interbrachial space 4 genital slits, which lie in pairwise correspondence close beside the arm prolongations of the disc. Mouth slits beset with papillae. Living and fossil from the Lias onward, perhaps already in the Muschelkalk (*O. Hauchecornei* Eck.), frequent and excellently preserved in the Lias of England (*O. Egertoni* Brod. sp.). Several of the fossil forms are distinguished from the living ones by 10 large radial shields on the back of the disc and perhaps form a genus of their own. According to Lütken most of the fossil species described as *Ophioderma* belong to *Ophioglypha*.

2. With two genital slits in each interbrachial space.

**Aspidura** Ag. emend. Pohlig (1) (*Acroura* and *Aspidura* Ag., *Aplocoma* d'Orb.) (Fig. 315). Upper side of the disc plated with 16 large, five-sided, smooth, firmly connected platelets, of which a central one is surrounded by a double circlet of 5 platelets each. The 5 smaller small plates of the inner circlet lie interradially, the 10 larger outer ones in pairs in the direction of the 5 rather short and broad arms. The corners between the inner and outer circlet of small plates are usually filled by a small scale. The mouth shields of the underside are divided by a median furrow into two equal halves. The side mouth shields do not touch one another within; teeth pointed. Mouth and ambulacral papillae present. On the sides 3–6 spines each. Interbrachial space of the underside plated. Trias. Especially in the Muschelkalk of Thuringia, Franconia, Würtemberg, Upper Silesia, Alsace-Lorraine, Luxemburg.

Subgenera: a) *Hemiglypha* Pohlig. Mouth shields large, split by a deep furrow. Arms broad, depressed at the base. Interbrachial space narrow; ventral platelets of the disc rather large. *Ophiura loricata* Goldf. (*Aspidura scutellata* Bronn.)

---
(1) Zeitschr. für wissenschaftl. Zoologie XXXI. S. 235.

**[p. 446]**

Fig. 315. *Aspidura (Hemiglypha) loricata* Goldf. sp. a Slab with numerous specimens from the Muschelkalk of Waschbach (Württemberg), nat. size. (After Quenstedt.) b Underside, c upper side of the disc, enlarged. (After Pohlig.)

Fig. 316. a *Geocoma carinata* Goldf. Specimen in nat. size from the lithographic slate of Zandt near Solenhofen. b granulated upper side of the disc, depressed and sunken in the middle (enlarged). c Underside of an arm (enlarged). d *G. planata* Quenst. sp. from the lithographic slate of Kelheim (Bavaria). Disc from below (enlarged), after a splendidly preserved specimen in the Munich Museum. e Arm from above (enlarged).

**[p. 447]**

b) *Amphiglypha* Pohlig. Oral shields moderately large, divided by a shallow furrow. Arms slender, as high as they are broad. Interbrachial space large, tessellated with small shields. Genital slits distinct. *Ophiura prisca* Goldf.

*Ophiurella* Ag. Disc small, finely granulated above and below. Arms very long, depressed and flattened, lateral shields on the sides and below beset with long, strong spines. Oral shields small, triangular. Tentacle scales present. In the lithographic slate of Bavaria (*O. speciosa* Goldf.). According to Lütken the typical species named belongs to *Ophiocoma*; however, the finely granulated disc lacks the radial shields, and the presence of the numerous dental papillae characteristic of *Ophiocoma* also cannot be demonstrated.

*Geocoma* d'Orb. emend. Zitt. (Fig. 316). Disc small, round, finely granulated above and below; 2 genital slits in each of the interbrachial spaces, which are usually somewhat depressed. Arms thin and slender, long, running out into quite fine points, angular. Oral shields large, heart-shaped. Mouth slits with 4—5 mouth papillae. Dorsal shields of the arms very large, weakly keeled in the middle; lateral shields small, beset with quite short spines, for the most part crowded onto the underside. Ventral shields elongate or almost as broad as long, not separated by the lateral shields but touching one another. Tentacle scales absent. Jurassic, in places very common.

*Ophiolepis* Müll. u. Trosch. Upper side of the disc covered with numerous naked shields and bordered with circles of scales. Mouth slits with a single row of papillae. Jaws with simple dental processes. Lateral shields of the arms beset with 2—7 papillae or spines. One or two scales at each tentacle pore. Oral plates simple, heart-shaped. Living; perhaps also Tertiary.

*Ophioglypha* Lyman (Fig. 312. 313). Upper side and underside of the disc covered with numerous smooth shields. Radial shields naked, large. Oral shields very large. Mouth papillae 3—6. Arm spines mostly 3 (or 1—3). Tentacle scales numerous. To this genus, very rich in species at the present day, Lütken assigns a series of fossil forms from Jurassic, Cretaceous and Tertiary deposits, such as *Ophiurella Griesbachi* Wright (Jurassic); *Acroura Cornueliana* d'Orb. and *serrata* Roem. (Cretaceous), *Palaeocoma Milleri* Phill., *Ophioderma Escheri* Heer, *Ophioderma carinata* Wright (Lias), *Ophiura Wettherelli* Forbes (Eocene) etc., which had hitherto been described under various generic names.

*Amphiura* Forbes. Living and ? Tertiary.

The genera *Ophicoma* d'Orb. (non *Ophiocoma* M. u. T.), *Aplocoma* d'Orb. and *Palaeocoma* d'Orb. are to be suppressed as wholly insufficiently or erroneously characterized.

## 2. Order. Stelleridae. Seesterne.

Among the true Seesterne or Stelleriden are reckoned those Asteroideen in which the arms represent outward bulgings of the disc and, like the latter, contain appendages of the intestine and extensions of the genital organs. On the underside of the arms there is a broad, open furrow, in the bottom of which runs a water-vessel strand springing from the central ring at the mouth, **[p. 448]** which gives off numerous lateral branches that serve to swell the ambulacral feet, standing in rows, and their ampullae. The margin of the ambulacral furrows is beset with spines or so-called papillae (furrow papillae). The madreporite plate, the small genital pores and the minute anal opening lie on the dorsal side in the vicinity of the centre.

Most commonly the Seesterne possess 5 broad, flattened arms, which project to a greater or lesser length from the disc; but there are also genera with 8, 10, 12, 20 and more arms.

The firm, calcareous dermal skeleton consists either wholly or in part of plates abutting one another, or of a network of bars whose interstices are joined by skin. These plates or bars are flat, tuberculate, or else beset with granules, small knobs, spines and bristles. Frequently there rise, upon the naked skin connecting the skeletal plates, stalks whose summits are crowned with bristles (paxillae). The individual plates (bones, ossicles) and bars of the skeleton are distinguished by great constancy within the genera.

The external form of the Seesterne depends chiefly on the relative extent of the disc and arms. In many genera the former attains a considerable size and the arms project only as short points from the pentagonal body; sometimes, however, the disc disappears almost entirely and the Seestern appears to be composed merely of 5 arms fused at the centre.

On the dorsal side there is in most genera a fine central or subcentral anal pore, which is usually difficult to find on fossil specimens; sometimes, however, any anal opening is lacking (*Astropecten*, *Luidia*). In one of the interbrachial spaces one observes one, two, or even several labyrinthically furrowed, porous, spongy plates (madreporite plates), through which water passes into the so-called stone canal and from this is conducted to the water-vessel ring running around the mouth. When several madreporite plates occur, they sometimes lie in different interbrachial spaces. In all Seesterne there are furthermore, in the skin covering of the back, more rarely also on the sides and beside the ventral furrow, fine pores for the passage of respiratory tentacles.

In the centre of the underside is the mouth, which is usually rendered five-cleft by 5 pairs of large "oral plates" situated in the projecting angles. The re-entrant slits are beset with papillae, but not with teeth. From the mouth, broad furrows run out on the underside toward each arm; these gradually narrow somewhat and are closed at the tip of the arms by a simple plate (ocular plate), **[p. 449]** notched on its underside (Fig. 317). Each of these ambulacral furrows contains 2 rows of narrow, obliquely placed, bone-like plates of elongate form, which are connected with one another in the middle by muscles and form a roof sloping outward and downward, beneath whose ridge a water vessel and a nerve strand run from the mouth to the tip of the arm. These two rows of plates, which roof over the ambulacral vessels and separate them from the outpouching of the body cavity lying above, are called ambulacral plates (Fig. 318). Their form varies in the different genera, but precisely these skeletal pieces, on account of their characteristic form, offer excellent characters for generic discrimination. In all living forms the two opposing rows of ambulacral plates correspond; in the oldest fossil forms, on the other hand, they alternate in the middle and appear to have only a slight roof-like inclination. The radial ambulacral vessel sends thin lateral branches to right and left between every 2 ambulacral plates, from which then spring, downward, the swellable ambulacral feet and, upward and inward, the bladder-shaped ampullae. The latter enter the interior through slit-like or pore-like openings between two ambulacral plates and are the only parts belonging to the water-vessel system of the arms that are situated within the skeleton (Fig. 319. 320). As a rule there stand on each side of the ambulacral furrow one, more rarely 2 rows of feet. From the position of the radial water vessel it follows that the so-called vertebral halves of the Ophiuriden are homologous with the ambulacral plates of the Stelleriden; the principal difference between the two orders consists in this, that the lateral branches of the water vessel in the Ophiuriden pierce the ambulacral plates, whereas in the Asteriden they pass off laterally between them. The ambulacral plates are bounded on each side by **[p. 450]** a row of adambulacral plates belonging to the dermal skeleton, which correspond to the lateral shields in the Ophiuriden and always appear armed with papillae or spines (Fig. 319. 320). Their number usually corresponds to that of the ambulacral plates. The oral plates in the mouth angles, already mentioned above, belong to the adambulacral plates.

Fig. 317. Ocular plate of *Goniaster*, from the white Jurassic limestone of Streitberg (2/1). a from within, b from without.

Fig. 318. An isolated ambulacral plate of *Goniaster*, from the white Jurassic of Streitberg (nat. size).

Fig. 319. *Astropecten aurantiacus* Phil., from the Mediterranean. Cross-section of an arm (enlarged). w radial water vessel, p ambulacral feet, amp ampullae, a ambulacral plates, ad adambulacral plates, mv lower marginal plates, md upper marginal plates, i intermediate plates.

The calcareous bodies lying in the skin, which begin beside the adambulacral plates, lie in part closely side by side or are joined into a network by transverse bars and processes, but frequently are also separate and held together merely by the leathery skin. In many genera the lower or the upper lateral margin of the arms is bordered by a row of large, uniformly shaped so-called marginal plates, of which the dorsal (upper) and ventral (lower) ones (Fig. 319) usually lie directly upon one another with their harmonic surfaces, but are sometimes also separated by one or more rows of smaller intercalary plates. Intermediate plates (filling plates) is the name given to the calcareous bodies situated between the lower marginal plates and the adambulacral plates, dorsal plates to those on the dorsal side. In genera with a very developed disc the intermediate and dorsal plates may occur in very great numbers (Fig. 321).

Among the dermal structures of the Seesterne are also reckoned the so-called pedicellariae, forceps-shaped or two-valved, stalked or sessile minute appendages, which, both on account of their small size and on account of their thin calcareous skeleton, can scarcely occur as fossils.

Fig. 320. *Uraster (Asteracanthion) rubens* Lin. sp. North Sea. Cross-section of an arm (enlarged). a ambulacral plates, ad adambulacral plates. The water vessel together with ampullae and ambulacral feet is indicated by dotted lines.

Fig. 321. *Goniaster (Goniodiscus) Parkinsoni* Forbes, from the white Chalk of Sussex. Underside in nat. size. o oral plates, ad adambulacral plates, mv ventral marginal plates, i intermediate filling plates.

**[p. 451]**

In general the Stelleriden, like the Ophiuriden, belong to the rarer fossils, and in particular completely preserved skeletons, or even merely impressions of whole bodies, are prized by collectors as special rarities. Isolated marginal plates are found most frequently. In comparison with the extraordinarily great abundance of living Seesterne, which in places occur so massively that they are used as manure, the fossil forms make up a vanishingly small fraction. Remarkably, however, they appear already in the oldest Silurian beds.

Joh. Müller and Troschel divide the Seesterne into 3 families: 1. forms with 4 rows of tentacles in the ambulacral furrows and with anus (*Asteracanthion*); 2. forms with 2 rows of ambulacral feet and with anus; 3. the same without anus. To these true Stelleriden (Asteriae verae) Bronn opposed the Palaeolithic Encrinasteriae, i.e. forms with alternating, not corresponding ambulacral plates. (1)

### 1. Suborder. Encrinasteriae. Bronn.

Ambulacral plates alternating in the middle of the ambulacral furrows.

*Aspidosoma* Goldf. (*Encrinaster* Haeck. p. p.) (Fig. 322). Pentagonal, flat, with 5 narrow arms projecting beyond the large disc. Underside bordered by a single row of smooth marginal plates, which on the part of the arms extended beyond the disc directly border the ambulacral furrow and thus take the place of the adambulacral plates; the latter begin only where the marginal plates follow the interbrachial margin of the disc, and run as a row of smaller plates up to the mouth angles. The large triangular interbrachial spaces appear to have been covered with a membranous perisome.

Fig. 322. *Aspidosoma petaloides* Simonowitsch, from the Lower Devonian sandstone of the Hohenreiner Hütte near Niederlahnstein. (After Simonowitsch.) a Specimen in nat. size from the underside, b arm from the upper side, c arm from the underside (enlarged).

---
(1) Since almost all palaeontological works hitherto have been based on the work of Müller and Troschel, I have retained their classification and nomenclature in essentials, although the systematics of the living Stelleriden has been substantially improved and remodelled by the more recent works of Lütken and especially of Perrier and Viguier.

**[p. 452]**

In the broad, shallow, sometimes broadly leaf-shaped ambulacral groove one observes two rows of oblong ambulacral plates, of which, however, two corresponding ones do not (as in all living Asteriden) unite in the middle into a so-called vertebra, but rather they alternate with one another like the biserial arm joints of the Crinoideen. Toward the lower end they are narrowed and leave free a round opening for the exit of the sucker feet. The mouth angles are surrounded by 5 or 10 thickened oral plates; beside the mouth, in one interbrachial field, there is a madreporite plate (Quenstedt). The dorsal side shows either two or four rows of polygonal plates, which in the first case are arranged alternately and occupy almost the entire breadth of the arm. At half the length of the arm the two rows separate toward the middle and leave free an interspace gradually increasing in breadth. With four dorsal rows, either all or only two rows continue up to the apex, where they leave free a central pentagonal star. Since the plates of the upper and lower sides are of fairly equal size and also uniformly arranged, the distinction of lower from upper presents difficulties. Devonian.

Of this remarkable genus two species (*A. Arnoldi* and *petaloides*) are found in the Rhenish Grauwacke (Niederlahnstein), a third, very large species in the roofing slate of Bundenbach near Birkenfeld.

*Palaeaster* Hall (*Uraster* p. p. Forbes, *Petraster* Billings) (Fig. 323). Five-rayed. Arms thick, convex, short or moderately long, consisting on the upper side of several rows of small spiny plates. Ambulacral furrows deep, beside the alternating ambulacral plates a row of adambulacral plates. Intermediate plates between the arms absent or only rarely present. Madreporite plate small and simple. In Cambrian beds of Bala in Wales. In the Lower Silurian of North America. Upper Silurian. Westmoreland. In the Devonian of Devonshire and North America. In the Carboniferous limestone of Ireland and Russia.

Fig. 323. *Palaeaster Eucharis* Hall. Devonian. Hamilton. New York (After J. Hall.) a Specimen in nat. size from below, b arm from above, c arm from below (schematic).

*Archasterias* Joh. Müll. Probably identical with *Palaeaster*. Flat, disc-shaped, five-rayed. Arms long, seven-rowed in all. Ventral plates **[p. 453]** in three rows on each side, those of the ambulacral rows alternating. Marginal rows less numerous. Intermediate plates present in small number. On the dorsal side three rows of plates with smaller plates between. Lower Devonian. *A. Rhenana* Müll.

*Urasterella* M'Coy (*Stenaster* Billings). Five-rayed, without disc. Rays linear, lanceolate or leaf-shaped. Ambulacral furrow broad, bounded on each side by a single row of solid, oblong or square adambulacral plates, which are at the same time marginal plates. Oral plates triangular. Upper side with small warty plates. Lower Silurian. Canada, Cincinnati, allegedly also in the Carboniferous limestone of Russia.

*Palasterina* M'Coy. Pentagonal, depressed, arms projecting little from the disc, above with 3 or 5 principal rows of warty plates. The angles between the arms filled with intermediate plates. Ambulacral furrows shallow, ambulacral plates subquadrate to rhombic, accompanied by a row of large quadrilateral adambulacral plates, of which the first 10 (oral plates) have a triangular shape and bear spines. Lower Silurian. Canada. Upper Silurian. Westmoreland, Gotland. *P. primaeva* Forbes.

*Schoenaster* Meek u. Worthen. Like *Palasterina*, but the filling plates beside the adambulacral plates and on the underside of the disc overlapping one another like scales. Carboniferous limestone. North America.

*Palaeodiscus* Salter. Disc-shaped, pentagonal, flattened. Arms not projecting beyond the disc. Ambulacral plates narrow, closely crowded; mouth surrounded by 5 pairs of large triangular oral plates, between them in the interradial angles a triangular wedge-shaped piece. Intermediate plates polygonal, beset with spines. Upper Silurian. England.

*Palaeocoma* Salter (non d'Orb.). Pentagonal. Arms projecting little, flat. Middle of the disc on the upper side frequently with scattered, star-shaped calcareous corpuscles. Interbrachial fields filled in a similar manner. Arms with several rows of quadrangular plates, of which the outer ones are fringed with spines. Ambulacral furrows narrow and shallow, ambulacral plates quadrilateral or elongate. Beside the quadrangular adambulacral plates a row of oblique plates beset with long spines. Interspaces of the arms filled by a net-like membrane. Upper Silurian. Shropshire.

Subgenera: a) *Bdellacoma* Salt. Like the preceding, but spines short, on the upper side intermixed with single larger ones of club-shaped form. Upper Silurian.

b) *Rhopalocoma* Salt. The fine spines wholly absent and only club-shaped ones present. Upper Silurian.

Palaeolithic genera of doubtful position:

*Helianthaster* F. Roem. Incompletely known. Many-rayed with central disc. Arms numerous (16), narrow, long, lanceolate. The apex of the acute angle between two neighbouring arms is occupied on the underside by an elongate tubercle. In the Lower Devonian roofing slate of Bundenbach near Birkenfeld and in the Upper Devonian of Harbertonford in Devonshire.

? *Trichotaster* Wright. Ten-rayed, only the outline known. Upper Silurian. Dudley.

**[p. 454]**

*Lepidaster* Forbes. Incompletely known, many-rayed. Disc fairly large, 13 arms projecting beyond it at equal intervals. Underside with elongate, scaly, overlapping plates, which stand in 2 pairs of rows beside the ambulacral furrows. Of these 4 rows of plates the adambulacral plates are provided with small pits (for spines?). Upper Silurian. Dudley. England.

? *Calliaster* Trautschold. Carboniferous limestone. Miatschkowo. Only ambulacral plates known.

### 2. Suborder. Asteriae verae. Bronn.

#### a) with two rows of ambulacral feet.

*Xenaster* Simonowitsch (*Asterias* p. p. Joh. Müll., *Coelaster* Sandbg. non Ag.). Five-rayed, small. Arms moderately long, gradually tapering. Dorsal side strongly convex, ventral side flatly concave. Margin formed by a double row of ventral and dorsal marginal plates. Ambulacral furrow fairly narrow. Adambulacral plates quadrangular; between them and the marginal plates usually 1—3 rows of intermediate plates. Interbrachial space of the underside filled by 4—5 (sometimes also only 1) prominent larger plates (interbrachial plates). Mouth pentagonal. Dorsal side provided with 3 principal rows of parallel, bead-string-like plates, between which lie rows of smaller plates. In the Devonian Spiriferen sandstone of Niederlahnstein, Ems and Rhenish Prussia. *X. margaritatus* Sim., *X. (Asterias) Rhenana* Müll., always preserved only as an impression.

? *Cribrellites* G. Tate. Carboniferous limestone. Alnwick. England.

*Trichasteropsis* Eck (Zeitschr. d. deutschen geol. Ges. 1879 S. 43 u. 254) (*Asterias* p. p. auct., *Trichaster* Quenst. non Ag.). Five-rayed, with large disc. Arms moderately long, blunt. Underside completely covered with short, bristle-like spines. Ambulacral furrow broad, shallow, bounded on each side by a row of narrow, elongate-quadrilateral adambulacral plates, which on the part of the arms projecting beyond the disc serve at the same time as lower marginal plates. The disc itself is bordered with a row of small ventral marginal plates, and the space in the interpalmar angles is filled by intermediate rows of plates. On the upper side the ends of the arms are beset with large marginal plates, which fade out or become weaker toward the disc. The back of the disc, as well as the parts of the arms situated between the marginal plates, appear originally to have been covered with a perisome in which small calcareous corpuscles, arranged in a median row and numerous transverse rows, form a **[p. 455]** wide-meshed net. Between the adambulacral plates, or ventral marginal plates, and the upper marginal plates runs a row of small lateral plates. Widespread in the Muschelkalk, especially in the Tauber region (*Asterias cilicia* Quenst.).

Fig. 324. *Astropecten aurantiacus* Phil. Recent. Mediterranean. Vertical section of an arm (enlarged).

*Pleuraster* Ag. emend. Eck. Trias. *Pl. Chopi* Eck.

*Astropecten* Linck (*Asterias* p. p. auct., *Crenaster* Lhwyd, *Pleuraster* p. p. Ag., *Stellaria* Nardo) (Fig. 324). Five-rayed, flattened on both sides, with long arms. Margin formed by two rows of large marginal plates. Lower row with thorn-shaped scales, which toward the outside grow into long spines. Dorsal marginal plates granulated, sometimes beset with spines. Flat upper side of the disc and arms beset with stalks that are crowned with bristle-like processes. Anus absent. Adambulacral plates quadrilateral. Living and fossil from the Lias onward; isolated marginal plates common.

? *Coelaster* Ag. Cretaceous. *C. Couloni* Ag.

*Goniaster* Ag. (*Pentagonaster* Linck, *Scutasterias* and *Platasterias* Blainv., *Comptonia*, *Tosia*, *Calliderma* Gray) (Fig. 325. 326). Pentagonal. Arms short, not projecting far beyond the disc. Margin formed on all sides by a dorsal and a ventral row of large marginal plates, sometimes beset with spines. Upper side within the marginal plates covered with small quadrilateral or polygonal plates. Anus subcentral. Underside tessellated in a manner similar to the upper. Ambulacral fields bounded by quadrangular adambulacral plates. Recent and fossil from the Lias onward. The most complete fossil specimens in the white Chalk of Sussex, North Germany and northern France. Marginal plates common in the Upper Jurassic.

Joh. Müller and Troschel distinguish the following 3 subgenera:

a) *Astrogonium* M. T. (Fig. 325). Marginal plates smooth toward **[p. 456]** the middle, at the margin with a wreath of granules. Cretaceous. Tertiary. Living.

Fig. 325. *Goniaster (Goniodiscus) Parkinsoni* Forbes. Lower Chalk. Sussex. a from the underside, b from the side. (After Forbes.)

Fig. 326. *Goniaster impressae* Quenst., from the white Jurassic and from Reichenbach im Thaele. a upper, b lower marginal plates in nat. size. c marginal plate with ? pedicellariae. (After Quenstedt.)

b) *Goniodiscus* M. T. Marginal plates granulated over the whole surface. Jurassic. Cretaceous. Tertiary. Living.

c) *Stellaster* M. T. Marginal plates granulated, those of the lower row adorned with a hanging spine. Cretaceous. Recent.

*Coulonia* Loriol. Like the preceding; marginal plates completely granulated, those of the lower row beset on the outside with a bundle of spines. Dorsal side with paxillae. Neocomian.

*Arthraster* Forbes. Incompletely known. Arms (6 or 7) cylindrical, long, disc small. The arms, apart from the unknown ambulacral plates, are surrounded by 7 parallel rows of oblong plates. The only species (*A. Dixoni*) comes from the Upper Cretaceous of Sussex.

*Luidia* Forbes. (*Hemicnemis* M. u. T.) Many-rayed. Arms long, beset with a ventral row of marginal plates which bear spines. Upper side with bristles. Living and perhaps fossil in the Lias.

*Solaster* Forbes (*Crossaster* Müller u. Trosch.). Many-rayed with large disc. Arms numerous, short. Upper side of the disc and arms with bundles of spines; between them naked perisome. Ambulacral furrows narrow, with two rows of tentacles. The only fossil species (*Sol. Moretonis* Forbes) of this predominantly recent genus comes from the Great Oolite of Gloucestershire.

*Plumaster* Wright. Arms numerous, long, feather-like, narrow at their base, broad in the middle, narrowing toward the blunt end. Intermediate plates **[p. 457]** transversely elongated, beset on the underside with a row of warts which bear hair-like spines. Ambulacral plates thick and prominent, furrows narrow and low. Oral plates form a projecting wreath. Lias. Yorkshire. (*Pl. ophiuroides* Wright.)

Fig. 327. a *Pentaceros jurassicus* Zitt. from the lithographic slate (Upper Jurassic) of Bemfeld near Ingolstadt (1/2 nat. size). b *P. thoracifer* Gein. Marginal plate from the Pläner of Plauen (Saxony). c *P. primaevus* Zitt. Dorsal plate from the Upper Jurassic of Streitberg.

*Tropidaster* Forbes. Five-rayed. Arms arched above, beset with simple spines and keeled; the keel formed of a double row of scaly plates. Ambulacral furrows bounded by transverse adambulacral plates, whose anterior margin bears spines. Ambulacral plates broad, bent knee-like, inner articular surface comb-like striated in the median plane. Middle Lias. England.

*Rhopia* Gray. Five-rayed. Arms very long, thin, pointed. Skin with a network formed of elongate small plates, on which stand short spines. Two rows of spines beside the ambulacral furrows. Anal opening present. Neocomian and living.

*Pentaceros* Linck (*Oreaster* Müll. u. Trosch.) (Fig. 327). Pentagonal. Disc large, arms blunt, moderately long, underside smooth, upper side highly arched. Margin with two rows of plates, of which the upper, larger, tuberculate ones alone form the margin, while the lower marginal plates already lie entirely on the underside. Between the upper marginal plates there are on the upper side large tuberculate or granulated plates, either arranged in rows or irregularly distributed, which are connected with one another by lateral processes and frequently bear strong spines. Living and fossil in the Upper Jurassic, Cretaceous and Tertiary; usually only isolated plates are found.

*Sphaeraster* Quenst. (*Sphaerites* Quenst., *Asterias* p. p. Goldf.) (Fig. 328. 329). By the above names Quenstedt and Goldfuss designate Echinodermen plates of mostly six-sided, but sometimes also four-, five-, seven-, eight-sided or wholly irregular form, which occur fairly commonly in the Upper (white) Jurassic of the Swabian-Franconian Jura mountains. They evidently belong to different species. In the largest and thickest (*Asterias scutata* Goldf.) there is on the upper side of the plates, which are provided with a jagged margin, a round articular pit for the reception of a conical spine (Fig. 328). These appear to be dorsal plates of a flat Seestern whose underside, according to Quenstedt (Petrefk. Deutschlands Pl. 94 Fig. 12. 13), was covered with smaller rhombic or irregularly six-sided plates which bordered the ambulacral furrows. For the present the mostly hexagonal plates from the Upper Jurassic designated as *Sphaerites punctatus* (Fig. 329b), *Asterias tabulata* (Fig. 329a) and *annulosa* and *Sphaeraster juvenis* remain puzzling, of which **[p. 458]** on a single occasion 21 were found united into an arched, Crinoideen-like pavement (Quenst. ib. Pl. 94 Fig. 55). In the angle of three plates abutting one another there is on this piece a triangular, porous small plate, which is interpreted by Quenstedt as a madreporite plate. *Asterias stellifera* Goldf. and *digitata* Quenst. most probably belong to *Pentaceros*.

Fig. 328. *Sphaerites scutatus* Goldf. Upper Jurassic. Sontheim (Württemberg).

Fig. 329. a *Asterias tabulata* Goldf. b *Sphaerites punctatus* Goldf. Upper Jurassic. Streitberg (Franconia).

#### b) with four rows of ambulacral feet.

*Asterias* Lin. (*Uraster* Ag., *Asteracanthion* Müll., *Stellonia* Nardo) (Fig. 330). Five-, ten-, twelve- or more-rayed. Arms long, thick. Skeleton consisting of small, irregularly shaped pieces connected in net-like fashion. Ambulacral furrow broad, with closely crowded, laterally compressed, fairly long ambulacral plates, between which there are on each side 2 rows of tentacle pores. Dorsal side beset with blunt or pointed thorns, which are arranged either in rows or in groups. Anus subcentral, small. Madreporite plate simple. Living chiefly in the Arctic and Atlantic region. Fossil in the Lias and Upper Jurassic. *A. Peslongchampsi* Morière (Bull. Soc. Lin. Norm. 3. Ser. vol. II).

Fig. 330. *Asterias rubens* Lin. Vertical section of an arm (enlarged). Recent.

### Phylogeny, distribution in time and space of the fossil Asteroideen.

Among the Echinodermen the Asteroideen unquestionably represent the most uniform and most enduring group. It is positively astonishing when one finds among the fossils of the oldest Silurian beds Stelleriden and Ophiuriden which in their total appearance deviate so little from the forms now living that a zoological distinction becomes possible only through an investigation going into details. Already in the Silurian formation one sees the three principal types — Seesterne, Ophiuren and Euryaleen — appear almost simultaneously, if with less sharp differentiation, and it is therefore difficult to say which of the three groups harbours the original stem form.

But the oldest Asteroideen also differ from the remaining classes of the Echinodermen scarcely less sharply than their present-day descendants. The only point of attachment to the Crinoideen is afforded by certain Cystoideen, such as *Agelacrinus* and *Edrioaster*. But even these genera, fixed with a broad base, differ from the true Asteroideen so essentially by their large ventral anal opening, by the lack of ambulacral plates **[p. 459]** and of rows of pores, as well as by the ambulacral furrows being wholly restricted to the disc, that the supposition of a close genetic relationship stands on weak feet. Noteworthy, to be sure, is the circumstance that most Palaeolithic Asteroideen possess alternating and not corresponding ambulacral plates, and in this respect are somewhat reminiscent of the arm structure of the Crinoideen. Whether great weight may be attached to this circumstance, however, must be left open, since in the Crinoideen skeleton there are no elements homologous with the ambulacral plates of the Asteroideen.

If, then, the origin of the Asteroideen from the Cystoideen becomes very doubtful already on purely morphological grounds, equally weighty objections stand in the way of the derivation from the Blastoideen advocated by E. Forbes. Hub. Ludwig has, it is true, recently demonstrated in the structure of the genital bursae of the Asteroideen an unexpected agreement with the hydrospires of the Blastoideen, but if one compares the entire morphological structure of the two Echinodermen groups, one must nevertheless admit between the two a wide and as yet unfilled gulf.

From the geological standpoint it may further be objected that the Asteroideen decidedly appear earlier than the Blastoideen and that they are also not inferior in age to the Cystoideen.

In view of these difficulties it is easily understandable that E. Haeckel (Generelle Morphologie) declares the Seesterne outright to be the stem forms of all Echinodermen and attempts to derive from them the Crinoideen, Echinoideen and Holothurioideen. While for the three orders of the Crinoideen a common descent does indeed appear probable, since on the one hand the Eucrinoideen are most closely connected with the Cystoideen through *Porocrinus*, *Caryocrinus*, *Cryptocrinus* and others, and on the other hand scarcely any boundary line can be drawn between Cystoideen and Blastoideen, there is nevertheless as yet a lack of mediating intermediate forms to the Asteroideen and Echinoideen.

An appraisal of Haeckel's second hypothesis, according to which the Seesterne would be regarded as colonies of 5 or more fused worms, is possible solely from the zoological and embryological standpoint, since the palaeontologist lacks all factual observations in this regard. In no case, moreover, may the alleged fossil worms described by Geinitz and Liebe from the so-called Taconic slates of Germany (Acta Leop. Carol. Ac. vol. XXXIII. 1867) be regarded as ancestors of the Asteroideen, for according to the investigations of Gümbel the Wurzbach **[p. 460]** slates belong not to the Silurian but to the Carboniferous formation, and moreover it is highly doubtful whether the genera *Phyllodocites*, *Crossopodia*, *Nereites* etc. really represent remains of worms (cf. Schimper-Zittel, Handb. d. Palaeont. Bd. II S. 49).

The oldest fossil Asteroideen come from the lowest fossiliferous (Cambrian) beds of the Silurian formation (1) of Bala in Wales. They are 3 species of *Palaeaster* and one species of the Ophiuriden genus *Protaster*. Lower Silurian Asteroideen are yielded by Canada and the so-called Trenton group of Ohio and New York (*Palaeaster*, *Urasterella*, *Palasterina*, *Protaster*, *Taeniaster*). The greatest number of Palaeolithic Seesterne and Ophiuren is found in the Upper Silurian deposits of Wales, England and North America. To the genera already named are added *Palaeocoma*, *Bdellacoma*, *Rhopalocoma*, *Palaeodiscus*, *Lepidaster*, ? *Trichotaster* and the oldest Euryaleen form *Eucladia*. Individual species, such as *Protaster Miltoni* Salter, are exceedingly common, but mostly poorly preserved. In the year 1868 Bigsby enumerated a total of 45 Silurian Asteroideen.

Devonian Seesterne of considerable size occur in great quantity, but poorly preserved and in part not generically determinable with certainty, in the roofing slate of Bundenbach near Birkenfeld (*Aspidosoma Tischbeinianum*, *Helianthaster Rhenanus*, *Asterias spinosissima*, *Asterias (Ophiura) asperulata* F. Roem.). Sharp impressions of *Aspidosoma*, *Archasterias* and *Xenaster* are found in the Rhenish Grauwacke, especially near Niederlahnstein, and from Pilton in Devonshire also several species of *Protaster*, *Palaeaster* and *Helianthaster* are known. Finally, J. Hall describes excellently preserved remains of *Palaeaster*, *Eugaster* and *Ptilonaster* from North America.

In the Carboniferous limestone there are known up to now the 3 North American genera *Schoenaster*, *Protaster*, *Onychaster* and the 2 problematic genera *Calliaster* and *Cribellites* from Russia and England. From the Dyas Geinitz describes a problematic *Asterias bituminosa*.

All Palaeolithic forms belong to extinct genera, and indeed they differ (with the exception of *Xenaster*) from all later ones by the alternating position of the ambulacral plates. This alternate position of the pore plates recurs both in the Encrinasterien and in the oldest Ophiuriden, and thus appears to have been a character common to all Asteroideen in the Silurian formation. In general, in the Silurian formation the **[p. 461]** characters of the Stelleriden and Ophiuriden still appear to intermingle in many ways, so that rather strange types difficult to place arise from this (*Protaster*, *Taeniaster*).

---
(1) Woodward H. Geolog. Magazine. 1. Ser. vol. VI und 2. Ser. vol. I.

In the Trias the Ophiuren genus *Aspidura* is widely distributed and is found in the Lower, Middle and Upper Muschelkalk of Germany, Lorraine, Poland and the Southern Alps (Eck, Zeitschr. d. deutsch. geol. Ges. 1879 S. 48). As rarer occurrences the genera ? *Ophioderma*, *Trichasteropsis*, *Pleuraster* and some forms from the Muschelkalk not more closely determinable generically are still to be mentioned. To *Aspidura* or a new closely related genus should probably also belong the dainty *Ophiura Cornaliae* Lepsius (Das westliche Südtirol Pl. III) from the Rhaetic slates of the Val Lorina.

From the Jurassic formation onward a decided approach to the present day makes itself felt. Already in the Lias one meets the recent genera *Asterias*, *Solaster*, *Goniaster*, *Luidia*, *Astropecten*, *Ophioderma*, *Ophioglypha* and *Ophiolepis*, to which are added the extinct *Tropidaster*, *Plumaster* and *Ophiurella*. The principal localities are the Lower and Middle Lias of Gloucestershire, Dorsetshire and Yorkshire in England and the Lower Lias in Aargau. In Germany the yellow Lower Liassic "Angulaten sandstone" of Swabia, Franconia and Coburg (Oppel, württemberg. Jahreshefte 1864. XX. S. 206) forms a rich deposit for Seesterne and Ophiuren. Unfortunately, however, these crude casts, internal moulds or impressions, lumped together under the collective names *Asterias lumbricalis* and *lanceolata* Goldf. (Fig. 331), are so poorly preserved that a strictly zoological determination cannot be thought of. Remarkable, too, are the plaits and ridges occurring with them, which Quenstedt (Petrefk. Deutschl. IV. S. 83) takes for trails of Asteriden. Similar impressions are, incidentally, also known from the (Rhaetic) bone-bed sandstone of Württemberg and Burgundy, as well as from the sandstone of the Brown Jurassic of Swabia.

Fig. 331. *Asterias lumbricalis* Schloth., from the Lower Liassic sandstone of Walzendorf in the Coburg district.

**[p. 462]**

In the Middle and Upper Jurassic the genera *Astropecten*, *Goniaster*, *Ophioglypha*, *Ophiurella* and *Geocoma* have the widest distribution, and in particular isolated plates of the genera provided with marginal plates belong among the more frequent finds. The commonest of all fossil Ophiuriden is probably *Geocoma elegans* Heller, which at La Voulte (Ardèche) covers by the thousands the beds of a ferruginous fine Callovian sandstone (1). Splendidly preserved species of *Ophiurella*, *Geocoma*, *Oreaster* and *Astropecten* are yielded by the Upper Jurassic lithographic slate in Bavaria, as well as the beds of the same age of Sontheim, Oerlingen and Hochstraess near Ulm. In England the Stonesfield slate, Forest marble and the Calcareous grit are distinguished by some excellent Asteriden forms. For the South German and Swiss white Jurassic *Sphaeraster* is a highly characteristic type.

The Cretaceous formation contains chiefly recent Stelleriden genera such as *Astropecten*, *Goniaster*, *Oreaster* and *Rhopia*; among the Ophiuriden *Geocoma*, *Ophioglypha* and the problematic genera *Palaeocoma* and *Ophiocoma* are cited. The most perfect specimens of *Oreaster*, *Goniaster* and *Arthraster* from the white Chalk are figured by Forbes in Dixon's Geology of Sussex.

In Germany numerous marginal plates and isolated complete specimens of Seesterne occur in the Pläner and Quader sandstone of Saxony (Geinitz, Das Elbthalgeb. I u. II) and Westphalia (Schlüter Cl., fossile Echinodermen des nördl. Deutschlands. Verhandlg. des naturhist. Ver. für Rheinland u. Westfalen 3. Folge IX. 1869). The scanty Asteriden of the Tertiary formation (cf. Heller a. a. O.) belong without exception to genera still existing today.

From the limited and in part insufficiently preserved fossil material it nevertheless follows that the Asteroideen have passed from the Silurian period onward in moderate numbers and without considerable changes through all formations up to the present day, and have on the whole left remains of perhaps 150—200 species. Sandy bottom appears from the first to have been most favourable to their flourishing, yet their skeletons are also found in decidedly deep-sea deposits, such as, for example, in the white Chalk and in the Spongiten beds of the Upper Jurassic. Their occurrence is thus not bound to any particular rock facies.

---
(1) C. Heller, über neue fossile Seesterne, Sitzungsbericht d. Wiener Akademie 1858 Bd. XXVIII.

**[p. 463]**

## 3. Class. Echinoidea. Seeigel, Igelstrahler (1).

The Seeigel are spherical, disc-shaped or oval, unstalked Echinodermen whose soft parts are enclosed by a solid, tessellated shell, which is adorned with tubercles, spines and other appendages and is deposited in a thin membranous perisome.
