Wright, Thom. Monograph of the foss. Echinod. of cretaceous form. Pal. Soc. 1864—71.

Laube, Gust. Die Echinodermen des braunen Jura von Balin. Denkschr. der k. k. Akad. Wien 1867.

Laube, Gust. Die Echinodermen des vicentinischen Tertiärgebirges. Ebend. 1868.

**[p. 464]**

On the underside is the mouth, and from this 5 radial water vessels run to the apex of the upper side. In contrast to the Asteroideen, the water vessels do not lie free in superficial furrows but are covered by the tessellated shell; their processes (ambulacral feet) must therefore pierce the latter in order to reach the surface, and thereby there arise on every Seeigel test 5 meridional fields pierced with pores, which are separated from one another by an equal number of poreless fields. By the course of the water vessels, then, the form of the Seeigel test is determined in the first instance, for over each of the 5 principal branches of the water-vessel system there develop in the perisome two meridional rows of small plates (ambulacral fields), bordered on each side by pore bands, which extend from the mouth to the apex. The intervening fields (interambulacral fields) also cover themselves with 2 parallel rows of small plates, so that in all living Seeigel the shell consists of 20 meridional rows of small plates, mostly immovably connected with one another by zigzag sutures.

The water-vessel system is fed by the stone canal, which connects the apex with the central ring vessel near the mouth and receives its water through a finely porous madreporite plate in the apex. The latter takes part in an apical wreath of small plates (apical shield) corresponding to the base of the Crinoideen calyx and to the apex of the Asteroideen. Mouth and apex are thus the two fixed poles between which the Echinoideen shell attains its development in very various forms. The first small plates of the meridional rows arise at the mouth; all those formed later insert themselves continually beneath the apical shield, which rises more and more.

---
Laube, Gust. Die Echinoiden der österr.-ungarischen Obertertiär-Ablag. Abhandlgn. k. k. geolog. Reichsanstalt 1871.

Desor et Loriol, P. de. Echinologie helvetique. I. Echinides de la periode jurassique. Wiesbaden 1878—72.

Quenstedt, F. A. Petrefaktenkunde Deutschlands Bd. III. Echiniden. Leipzig 1872—75.

Dames, W. Echiniden der nordwestdeutschen Jurabildungen. Zeitschr. d. deutschen geol. Ges. Bd. XXIV. 1872.

Loriol, P. de. Echinologie helvetique. II. Echinides de la période crétacée. 1873.

Pavay, Al. v. Die fossilen Seeigel des Ofener Mergels. Jahrb. d. k. ungar. geolog. Reichsanst. Bd. III. 1874.

Loriol, P. de. Echinologie helvetique. III. Echinides de la période tertiaire. Mémoires Soc. paléont. de la Suisse 1875.

Dames, W. Die Echiniden der vicentinischen und veronesischen Tertiärablagerungen. Palaeontographica Bd. XXV. 1877.

**[p. 465]**

With the exception of the intestine, which begins on the underside as the direct continuation of the pharynx and oesophagus and, after some windings in the interior of the shell, in which it is held by ligaments, ends in the anal opening either in the apex or at any point in the median plane of the posterior half of the shell between apex and mouth, all the remaining central organs are radially developed and dependent in their arrangement on the ambulacral system. Thus the 5 nerve strands radiating from the central pharyngeal ring, as well as the principal blood vessels, follow the course of the water vessels. The grape-shaped genital glands too are present in fivefold or fourfold, very rarely only in twofold number, and lie in the interambulacral fields; their egg or sperm canals emerge as a rule as 5, 4 or 2 pores in the so-called genital plates of the apical shield, exceptionally also immediately beside the latter at the upper end of the interambulacra.

Respiration is attended to directly by the water-vessel system. Either certain processes of the ambulacral vessels reaching the surface through pores develop into leaf-shaped or pinnate gill tentacles (ambulacral gills), or bushily branched mouth gills, supplied by the water vessel, project in the mouth angles.

For the palaeontologist it is chiefly the shell and its calcareous appendages that have practical interest. The former consists of the so-called corona, i.e. the meridional, laterally connected rows of small plates, and the apical shield.

### The Corona,

frequently also called simply the shell (perisoma, calyx), is divided into 5 ambulacral and 5 interambulacral fields (1), which correspond to the radial and interradial fields of the Crinoideen. With the exception of the Palechinoideen of the Palaeolithic age and the genus *Tetracidaris*, each of the 10 fields consists of 2 meridional rows of five- or six-sided small plates (plaques coronales, assulae, Asseln), of which those belonging to the same row are joined by parallel transverse sutures, those of two adjacent rows of one and the same field, on the other hand, by zigzag sutures. Usually the union of all the plates is so intimate that an immovable shell results; sometimes, however, the small plates overlap one another with their obliquely truncated margins like roof tiles or scales, or are even separated from one another by weak **[p. 466]** membranous interspaces, so that a certain displaceability and mobility of the calcareous skeleton results. Both phenomena were first observed in the Echinothuriden and Palechinoideen, but, as Lovén has demonstrated, the plates of the Spatangiden too show (though to a very slight degree) a bevelling of the margins, and according to Hub. Ludwig the two rows of the posterior, unpaired IAf. of the Spatangiden and Holasteriden not only gape somewhat, but can also be voluntarily brought closer together or moved apart by muscles.

---
(1) The ambulacral fields are designated in what follows by Af., the interambulacral fields by IAf.

In all living and most fossil Seeigel the corona consists invariably of 20 meridional rows of small plates. The oldest fossil forms, however, show a striking irregularity, especially in the composition of the IAf. While, for example, in the Silurian genus *Bothriocidaris* the IAf. consist of only a single row of small plates, their number rises in *Melonites* to 7—8; in rare cases the meridional rows may also multiply in the Af., so that, for example, the corona of the genus *Melonites* already mentioned appears composed of 75—80 rows of small plates.

The Af. beset with pores and the poreless IAf. develop quite independently of one another, and as a rule the small plates of the latter are larger and less numerous than those of the former; indeed, in certain forms (Cidaridae) the difference becomes so considerable that, for example, an interambulacral meridional row may consist of only 4—5 small plates, the ambulacral one on the other hand of 50—60.

Where the zigzag sutures of the two ambulacral rows of small plates meet, a radial main trunk of the water vessel runs in the interior and sends out at regular intervals horizontal alternating branches to both sides. The latter swell near the outer margin of the Af. into contractile ampullae and give off two tubes, which pierce the shell and unite again outside into a tubular tentacle (ambulacral foot) with a broad, transversely drawn-out base. Thus to each pair of pores of the ambulacral fields there corresponds one ambulacral foot. As a rule all ambulacral pores occur in pairs; only exceptionally (in the Spatangiden) do single pores also occur. For the most part the ambulacral tubes serve as organs of locomotion; they can be so greatly elongated that they overtop the spines, and are capable of dragging the whole body after them when they have attached themselves by suction with their tip and then contract. Sometimes, especially in the Seeigel with leaf-shaped ambulacra, the tentacles develop into gill feet and alter their shape correspondingly.

**[p. 467]**

The pore pairs, which bound the Af. laterally and there form meridional pore bands (Fühlergänge, zones porifères), consist either of equal, mostly round, or of unequal pores, of which the one is usually round, the other transversely elongated. If two pores are connected by a transverse furrow, they are called conjugate (gejocht). Each Af. is bordered by two pore bands formed of double pores; one therefore counts 10 of them on every Seeigel. The whole Af. together with the pore bands is also called simply the ambulacrum. An ambulacrum is called simple or perfect (amb. simple, amb. perfectum) when the pore bands run in a straight line from mouth to apex without essential change (Desmosticha); leaf-shaped or petaloid (Petalosticha, ambulacra circumscripta) when the pore bands at first diverge from the apex, but on the upper side incline toward one another again and in this way form around the apex leaf-shaped fields bounded by pores (petalodia). The latter are toward the lower end either closed or more or less widely open. Between the typically developed petaloid and the simple ambulacra there are intermediate stages. Thus the ambulacra are called subpetaloid when the petalodia are strongly elongated, open below, and composed of equal, round, non-conjugate pores.

If the Af. are provided with closed petalodia, the pores rarely cease completely below them, but rather can be traced as far as the mouth; but they are present in small number, small, often scarcely visible, and stand mostly at larger, irregular intervals (incomplete ambulacra).

In the Seeigel with simple ambulacra (Desmosticha) the pore bands consist either of a single double row of pore pairs (pori unigemini), or the pore pairs form 2 (p. bigemini), 3 (p. trigemini) or more (p. multigemini) double rows.

The part of the ambulacra enclosed by the pore bands is called the "interporiferous field or median field" (zone interporifère).

It was already mentioned above that the number and form of the small plates in the Af. and IAf. are subject to considerable differences. In the radially built so-called regular Seeigel all Af. and all IAf. are usually alike among themselves; in the bilaterally symmetrical or irregular forms, on the other hand, the anterior ambulacrum and the posterior interambulacrum frequently differ quite considerably from the other fields of the same name.

Those small plates of the ambulacra which are pierced by pores are called pore plates. These insert themselves beneath the apical **[p. 468]** shield and appear originally to have all been of the same size and form. During the growth of the corona, however, peculiar changes frequently occur. Individual small plates become stunted, remain small, do not reach the middle of the ambulacral field, but insert themselves as so-called half plates at the outer margin of the pore band between the larger pore plates. Sometimes the half plates are also pushed inward and stand either in the middle or at the inner margin of the pore bands between the larger plates. In the regular Seeigel even 2—10 primarily separate pore plates may fuse into compound large plates.

### The apical shield (appareil apical, abactinal system)

is always situated on the arched upper side and there occupies a central or subcentral position. It consists normally of 5 so-called ocular plates (plaques ocellaires) situated at the end of the ambulacra, and 5 or 4 genital plates (plaques génitales or oviducales, egg plates) situated over the IAf. (Fig. 332).

Fig. 332. Apical shields of a *Salenia*. b *Holectypus*. c *Holaster*.

The ocular plates mostly have a five- or three-sided form and are pierced by a very fine opening for the exit of the optic nerve. In some old forms from the Palaeolithic age (*Palaechinus*, *Melonites*) 2 pores were observed on each plate.

The typical form of the genital plates is an irregular hexagon; they are mostly somewhat larger than the ocular plates and provided with one (in the Palechinoideen with 3—5) round pore(s) (genital pores).

**[p. 469]**

In many cases only 4 (sometimes indeed only 3 or 2) genital plates are perforated, the fifth imperforate; indeed the latter, together with the associated genital gland, may atrophy completely and be absent. One of the genital plates is distinguished from the others by its porous, spongy texture; it serves as a sieve plate for the water entering the stone canal and is termed the madreporic plate or madreporite.

Through varying extension of the madreporic plate the apical disc can be considerably modified. In the Clypeastriden, for example, it pushes back all the other plates and occupies almost the whole apical disc.

In the regular Seeigel (Fig. 333) the apical disc forms a ring in which the genital and ocular plates alternate regularly. They enclose a central space in which lies the anal aperture, which in the Cidariten is covered by numerous small plates, in the Saleniden (Fig. 332a) and in young Echiniden by one (sometimes 2–3) large central plate(s). Even when the anal opening lies outside the apical disc (Echinoconidae, Spatangidae, etc.), supernumerary plates may be inserted, partly in the middle, partly between the posterior ocular plates.

Fig. 333. *Cidaris coronata* with completely preserved apical disc.

When the genital plates stand in a circle and the ocular plates are inserted in their re-entrant angles, the apical disc is called compact; it is elongated when the 4 anterior paired ocular plates stand in the same line as the genital plates and when the two parallel rows touch with their inner sutures. Sometimes the apical disc is elongated and at the same time disjunct (app. apical disjoint), when two ocular plates are separated from the rest by a more or less wide interval (Dysasteridae). In this case the ambulacra no longer meet in a common centre, but the three anterior ones separate from the two posterior and form in front a so-called trivium, behind a bivium (Fig. 334).

For orientation as to front and back, the position of the anus serves in the first place; whenever it is at all eccentric, it lies in the posterior half of the body. In all Seeigel with an eccentric anus, however, a longitudinal axis can be drawn through apex, mouth and anus **[p. 470]** which divides the body into two bilaterally symmetrical halves. The Seeigel in this position directs one ambulacrum of the trivium forward, so that the ambulacra arrange themselves into two lateral paired ones and one anterior unpaired one. The madreporic plate is then always situated above the anterior right interambulacral area and thus orients the test just as reliably as the anal opening. The decision as to front and back becomes more difficult in the Seeigel with apical anal opening and simple, band-shaped ambulacra, where the test apparently shows a strictly radial structure. Through comprehensive studies, however, Lovén has shown that these forms too possess a median axis, according to which the two halves arrange themselves symmetrically.

Fig. 334. *Collyrites elliptica*. Apical disc strongly elongated and disjunct. Ambulacra separated into trivium and bivium.

If one lays an irregular Seeigel (*Spatangus*, Fig. 335) on its back so that the underside is turned toward the observer and the mouth is directed forward, and if one then designates the ambulacra, starting from the posterior interambulacral area, from left to right as I, II, III, IV and V, then the first plates of the two rows of the ambulacra that take part in forming the peristome, of which the first is always designated a and the second b, show a striking and constantly recurring inequality. In ambulacrum I plate Ia is larger than Ib and bears two pores instead of one; ambulacrum II behaves in the same way. In ambulacrum III, on the other hand, the reverse relation prevails: IIIa is smaller than IIIb and provided with only one pore; in IV again a, in V b is larger than the primary plate of the neighbouring row. With this orientation the madreporic plate is always situated at the end of the anterior right interambulacral area lying between ambulacra III and IV. The regular Seeigel, however, behave in quite a similar way, in that here too smaller and larger plates close off the 5 ambulacra at the peristome in the order stated. Thus two ambulacra (bivium) always stand symmetrically, the three **[p. 471]** others (trivium) asymmetrically to the median plane of the unpaired ambulacrum and interambulacrum, and the madreporic plate here falls invariably at the upper end of the right anterior interambulacrum. The longitudinal axis of the regular Seeigel is thus determined at once by the madreporic plate.

Fig. 335. *Spatangus purpureus* O. F. M. Diagram of the underside and of a part of the upper side projected into one plane (after Lovén).

### The Peristome

The peristome (oral aperture, péristome, actinal system), often also called simply the mouth (although the actual mouth opening lies in the middle of a skin-like membrane covering the peristome, or of a plated oral covering), always lies on the underside of the test, very often in its middle, but frequently also eccentrically and then usually shifted somewhat forward. The form of the opening is round, pentagonal, decagonal, oval or bilabiate, its size very different according to the genera.

**[p. 472]**

Sometimes the corners of the peristome are provided with notches for the exit of the buccal gills, and the peristomial margin is thereby divided into 5 or 10 lip-shaped projections. In the Cassiduliden the ambulacra become depressed near the mouth and bear numerous well-developed double pores; between these so-called phyllodes rise lip-shaped swellings (bourrelets buccaux), and both together form an elegant five-leaved star around the mouth, which is called the floscelle. A transversely bilabiate mouth arises through the anterior transverse margin sinking down and the posterior one arching freely and somewhat bow-like over it.

A considerable number of Seeigel possess as a masticatory apparatus a powerful framework consisting of 5 pyramidal, usually bipartite, so-called jaws (Kinnladen or Kiefer), in which sharp teeth move; to these principal pieces are added some others, intended partly for attaching, partly for holding together the movable parts, which assume different characters according to the families and will be described more precisely later. In the regular Seeigel the jaw framework is termed the Laterna Aristotelis.

If a firm masticatory apparatus is present, the margin of the peristome is found to be more or less turned inward; as a rule there develop at the end of the IAf., rarely on the Af., ear-shaped, upright projections (auriculae), which either remain separate or incline together with their upper ends and form in the interior of the test an arched gateway over the Af.

### The anal aperture (periprocte)

is usually of rounded or oval form and in living specimens covered with a membrane, on which lie a greater or lesser number of calcareous plates. In the middle of the membrane is the actual anal opening (anus), which during excretion is extended into a tube. In Germany one usually calls the whole anal aperture simply "After" [anus], while the French and English distinguish sharply between the anal aperture (periprocte) and the anal opening (anus).

The Seeigel are divided into two large groups according to the position of the anal opening. In the Endocyclica it is situated diametrically opposite the mouth within the apical disc; in the Exocyclica, on the other hand, it leaves the apex and lies either on the underside in the median axis of the Seeigel between mouth and margin, or close beside the margin (anus inframarginalis), or in the posterior margin (a. marginalis), or above the posterior margin between this and the apex (a. supramarginalis).

**[p. 473]**

Among the ornaments and appendages of the surface, which play an important role in the Echinoideen, belong the tubercles, impressions, spines, fascioles, pedicellariae and sphaeridia.

### The tubercles (tubercles)

are elevations of the test plates and are divided according to their size into primary tubercles, secondary tubercles, granular tubercles and granulations or granules. The latter (granules) show no further differentiation; they are simple small knobs, frequently serving for the attachment of the pedicellariae. In the former one distinguishes the hemispherical or spherical tubercle head (mamelon), which is now smooth, now provided with a central pit and is then called perforate (mamelons imperforés et perforés), and the tubercle neck (breast, tubercle cone, col, conus, boss), the truncated-conical elevation on which the tubercle head rests. Both are separated by the ring-shaped upper margin (anneau) of the tubercle neck, which may be smooth or radiate (smooth and crenulate or radiate tubercles, tubercles lisses et crénelés). The primary and secondary tubercles rise as a rule in the middle of a smooth, somewhat depressed, roundish-oval surface, the so-called areole or scrobicule (areola, scrobicule), which is frequently bordered by a raised ring of granular tubercles (cercle scrobiculaire).

The space between two rows of primary tubercles in the Af. and IAf. is called the median zone (zone miliaire) and is now smooth, now beset with granules.

### Impressions (impressions)

of rounded or elongate form sometimes occur partly in the middle, partly in the corners of the plates; more frequently, however, they follow their sutures and form either smooth, depressed vertical stripes running in zigzag between two rows of plates, or parallel transverse furrows corresponding to the sutures. The various impressions are observed mainly in the regular Seeigel.

### The spines (radioles, piquants, épines)

are movable appendages, fastened by elastic ligaments to the tubercle heads and articulating with these, of rod-, club-, bristle-, thorn-, spatula- etc. shaped form, sometimes of very considerable size. Their depressed articular surface (facette articulaire, acetabulum), with which they rest on the tubercles, is surrounded by a smooth or crenulate margin and serves as base for the somewhat thickened spine head (tête, bouton); above, the head is bounded by a projecting smooth or crenulate ring (anneau), which serves for the attachment of ligaments, **[p. 474]** and then passes into the smooth or finely striated, sometimes constricted spine neck (collerette); on this follows the actual, usually rough, thorny or striated shaft or body (tige). Size and number of the spines correspond to the associated tubercles. In fossil Seeigel the spines are only exceptionally preserved in their natural position; usually tests and spines are found isolated.

Fig. 336. Various spines of *Cidaris* (a–e).

Among the surface ornaments that belong only to individual families (Spatangiden) are the so-called fascioles (semitae), i.e. smooth stripes beset with fine bristles, which, interrupting the tubercles or other sculpture of the surface, run in various directions and enclose areas of differing size and form. They will be described more closely under the Spatangiden.

The pedicellariae, microscopically small grasping organs, as well as the sphaeridia discovered by Lovén and interpreted as organs of taste, have no practical significance for the palaeontologist, since owing to their delicacy and small size they are not capable of preservation.

From the developmental history, those stages which the Seeigel passes through after the loss of the last remains of the larval skeleton deserve the attention of the palaeontologist. The facts belonging here, such as the development of the apical disc in the endocyclic forms, the changes in the position of the peristome and anus in individual exocyclic genera, the development of the petaloids from simple ambulacra, etc., which are in part of outstanding **[p. 475]** importance for systematics, are discussed in more detail in the special part.

All fossil Seeigel occur in marine deposits. The oldest forms appear already in the Silurian formation, and from there they pass, constantly increasing in richness of forms, through all formations up to the present day. They are frequently distinguished by excellent state of preservation and completeness above the fossils occurring with them, so that their structure can be studied with almost the same precision as in the living Seeigel. In geological importance too the Echinoideen are surpassed by few other fossils: they are fairly common, well preserved, comparatively easy to distinguish and mostly possess a short phyletic life span, so that particular species, indeed even genera, belong exclusively to certain deposits and characterize them sharply. Through the properties named, the Seeigel meet all the requirements of good index fossils.

But also in a purely morphological respect the fossil Seeigel, far surpassing the recent ones in number and richness of forms, have always played a weighty role. Already the two oldest fundamental works of the two Danzig naturalists Breyn and Klein, which appeared in 1732 and 1734, give excellent figures of fossil and living Seeigel. Breyn adopted the designation Echinus, already proposed by Aristotle and applied in 1554 by Rondelet to the living *Echinus granularis* Lam., calling the living forms Echini, the fossil ones Echiniden. The whole order of the Echiniden is divided into 7 genera: *Echinometra*, *Echinoconus*, *Echinocorys*, *Echinanthus*, *Echinospatagus*, *Echinobrissus* and *Echinodiscus*. Klein chose for our present class of the Echinoideen the name Echinodermata and proposed 20 new genera, which however were retained only in small part by later authors. In a second edition of Klein's work, provided with additions, Leske considerably extended knowledge especially of the fossil Seeigel. Good figures had been given much earlier by Lang, Walch and Knorr, among others. Lamarck's Animaux sans vertèbres and Desmoulins' Tableau analytique became important for systematics, but only with the fundamental works of the elder Agassiz and of Desor did fossil echinology obtain its present secure basis. For Germany the plate work of Goldfuss gives numerous and excellent figures of the native forms. A much more complete survey of the fossil Cretaceous and Jurassic Echinoideen of France is provided by the splendidly illustrated Paléontologie française, which since d'Orbigny's death has been continued in exemplary **[p. 476]** fashion by G. Cotteau. From the latter there are moreover numerous special monographs. No small influence on systematics was exerted by a small treatise on the fossil Echiniden of the Isère department by Alb. Gras, whose classification was partly adopted by Desor. In England Thom. Wright works on the fossil Seeigel, and in Switzerland P. de Loriol has worthily followed in the footsteps of his famous predecessors L. Agassiz and E. Desor. By F. A. Quenstedt, in a large plate work (Petrefaktenkunde Deutschlands, vol. III), mainly the species occurring in Germany were described and figured. G. Laube occupied himself with the fossil Echiniden of the Austro-Hungarian Tertiary, Jurassic and Triassic formations, Pávay with those of Hungary, W. Dames with the forms occurring in North Germany and in the Vicentine. To the Palaeozoic Seeigel M'Coy, Joh. Müller, F. Roemer, J. Hall, Meek and Worthen, Keeping, Etheridge and others devoted their attention.

Among the authors who have done most to elucidate the organizational relations and developmental history, the names Joh. Müller, Al. Agassiz and Lovén stand in the first rank.

For systematics the more recent works of Desor, Th. Wright, Cotteau, Loriol and Al. Agassiz are authoritative. Fairly generally the Palaeozoic forms, distinguished in many respects, especially by the varying number of interambulacral plate rows, are set against all the others as a special subclass, and the former are designated either as Perischoechinidae, Tesselati or, more suitably, as Palechinoidea, the latter as Euechinoidea.

The Euechinoideen divide naturally into 2 orders, of which the first, the Regulares or Endocyclica, contains all radially built forms with equal ambulacra, whose apical disc encloses the anal opening lying opposite the mouth. In the second order, the Exocyclica or Irregulares, the anus leaves the apical disc. The irregular Seeigel fall again, according to the presence or absence of a jaw framework, into the two suborders Gnathostomata and Atelostomata.

### Systematic Overview of the Echinoidea.

**I. Subclass. Palechinoidea. Zitt.**

Test composed of more, rarely of fewer, than 20 plate rows. Apical disc consisting of 5 or 10 multiply perforated plates.

**1. Order. Cystocidaridae. Zitt.**

IAf. broad with several plate rows. Anus eccentric.

**[p. 477]**

**2. Order. Bothriocidaridae. Schmidt.**

IAf. with a single plate row. Anus in the apical disc.

**3. Order. Perischoëchinidae. M'Coy.**

IAf., sometimes also Af., with more than 2 plate rows. Anus in the apical disc.

**II. Subclass. Euechinoidea. Bronn.**

Test formed of 20 plate rows. Plates of the apical disc only singly perforated.

**1. Order. Regulares. Alb. Gras. (Endocyclica Wright.)**

Anus in the apical disc. Ambulacra simple, all alike. Jaw apparatus present.

1. Family. Cidaridae (Holostomata).
Af. narrow, consisting of simple plates. IAf. broad with large tubercles. Peristome covered as far as the mouth opening with movable plate rows, of which those of the ambulacral rows bear pores.

2. Family. Salenidae.
Apical disc with supernumerary plates. Peristome with poreless calcareous scales and 10 buccal plates.

3. Family. Echinothuridae.
Test consisting of movable, overlapping plates; peristome covered with plates arranged in rows, partly bearing pores.

4. Family. Glyphostomata.
Af. broad, consisting of compound large plates or of plates united in groups. Peristome with skin-like membrane, with notches at the corners.

**2. Order. Irregulares. Alb. Gras. (Exocyclica Wright.)**

Anus outside the apical disc.

1. Suborder. Gnathostomata.
Mouth with jaw apparatus.

1. Family. Echinoconidae.
Ambulacra simple, all alike. Pores round.

2. Family. Conoclypeidae.
Ambulacra petaloid, narrow; pore bands developed also on the underside, but differing from the upper side. IAf. broad.

3. Family. Clypeastridae.
Ambulacra petaloid, broad, confined to the upper side. IAf. narrow on the underside.

2. Suborder. Atelostomata.
Mouth without jaw apparatus, pentagonal or bilabiate.

1. Family. Cassidulidae.
Mouth central, often with floscelle. Ambulacra simple or petaloid.
a) Ambulacra simple or subpetaloid. Echinoneinae.
b) Ambulacra petaloid. Echinolampinae.

2. Family. Holasteridae.
Mouth shifted far forward. Ambulacra simple.
a) Apical disc strongly elongated, disjunct into trivium and bivium. Dysasterinae.
b) Apical disc moderately elongated or compact; all ambulacra united at the apex. Ananchytinae.

**[p. 478]**

3. Family. Spatangidae.
Mouth shifted far forward. Ambulacra petaloid; the anterior unpaired one different from the rest. Fascioles as a rule present.
a) Mouth pentagonal. Palaeostominae.
b) Mouth bilabiate. Spatanginae.

## 1. Subclass. Palechinoidea. Zitt.

Seeigel whose test is composed of more, rarely of fewer, than 20 plate rows. Apical disc with 5 or 10 multiply perforated or imperforate plates.

In contrast to all the now-living, Cainozoic and Mesozoic Seeigel, the Palaeozoic Palechinoideen are distinguished by a striking inconstancy in the number of the meridional plate rows composing the test. In the preponderant majority of them (the Perischoechiniden) an increase of the plate rows takes place preferably in the IAf., more rarely in the Af., so that the number of rows, instead of amounting to 20, fluctuates between 35 and 60 and in the genus *Melonites* even exceeds 80. The otherwise so constant arrangement of the plates sometimes undergoes changes in this group within the genera and thereby sinks to a species character. With regard to plating, another family of the Palechinoideen, the Cystocidaridae, of which so far only a single Silurian genus with eccentric anus is known, behaves similarly to the Perischoechiniden. In the 3rd family (Bothriocidaridae), on the other hand, the plate rows fall short of the normal number. Striking is the manner in which the plates are joined together in most Palechinoideen. Instead of being immovably connected by straight sutures, they frequently lie over one another like roof tiles or scales, in such a way that one or more margins of a plate are obliquely truncated and overlap the neighbouring plates. There thereby arises a certain mobility of the test, which among the younger Echiniden occurs only in the family of the Echinothuriden and to a lesser degree also in certain Spatangiden.

All Palechinoidea possess a large mouth opening situated in the centre of the underside, which, as in the regular Euechinoideen, is armed with a so-called Laterna Aristotelis formed of powerful jaws (Fig. 337). Opposite the mouth, at the apex, lies, with the exception of *Cystocidaris*, the anal opening, which is bordered by an apical disc consisting of 5–10 plates. In the latter (Fig. 338) the ocular plates sometimes seem to be absent; the genital plates, on the other hand, are large and, instead of with one pore, are pierced **[p. 479]** by 3–5 pores. A distinct madreporic plate has been recognized only in *Cystocidaris* and *Bothriocidaris*.

Fig. 337. Jaw apparatus of *Archaeocidaris Rossica* Eichw. sp. from the Carboniferous limestone of Miatschkowa. a. Two jaw halves together with tooth, enlarged. b. Jaw apparatus from the side, c. from above, nat. size (after Trautschold).

Fig. 338. Apical disc of *Palaechinus*, from the Carboniferous limestone of Ireland, enlarged. (After Baily.)

The spine tubercles are usually only moderately developed and confined to the IAf.; beside them occur granular tubercles, which in individual genera form the sole ornament of the plates altogether. On the size of the tubercles depends also the strength of the spines, which only in a few genera equal those of the Cidariden.

For the systematic position of the Palechinoideen their similarity to juvenile Cidariten tests, emphasized by Al. Agassiz, is of high significance. The numerous small plates, in part overlapping one another like scales, which surround the mouth of the Cidariten and in the IAf. sometimes stand in more than two rows, occupy in young specimens a comparatively large space, and likewise the apical disc has a very large extent, so that the immovably connected test plates (coronal plates) lying between the two and arranged in 20 rows form only a narrow zone. If one now imagines the latter entirely suppressed and the oral plates continued up to the apex, one would finally obtain the picture of one of the Perischoechiniden, with scaly overlapping plates in several rows in the IAf. Thus in this respect the Palechinoideen represent persistent juvenile states of the later-appearing Cidariten.

With the exception of the incompletely known *Anaulocidaris* from the Alpine Trias, all Palechinoideen are confined to the Palaeozoic formations; most genera are rare, known only in few specimens, frequently even only in insignificant fragments. However unimportant these remains may otherwise be for the geologist, they nevertheless possess, in a zoological and genetic respect, as the precursors of the Euechinoideen, an outstanding interest.

**[p. 480]**

### 1. Order. Cystocidaridae. Zitt.

Spherical or egg-shaped bodies with narrow ambulacral areas. Interambulacral areas broad, with numerous thin, scaly and movable plates arranged in irregular rows, which bear larger and smaller spines on tubercles. Mouth on the underside, central, with powerful jaw apparatus; anus interradial between mouth and apex. Apical disc unknown. Madreporic plate large.

Wyv. Thomson (1) regards this remarkable group of Echiniden, unfortunately known only in incomplete specimens, as a transitional member between Cystoideen and Echinoideen. By the structure of the Af., by the powerful jaw apparatus and by the spine-bearing interambulacral plates they prove themselves true Seeigel. I have therefore changed the name *Echinocystites* W. Thoms. (which in 1864 was also given by Hall to a genus of Cystideen) into *Cystocidaris*.

***Cystocidaris*** Zitt. (*Echinocystites* Wyv. Thomson non Hall). Spherical or oval; mostly preserved only in crushed impressions or internal casts. IAf. broad, with 3–6 very irregular rows of thin, scale-like plates of rounded 3-, 4-, 5- or 6-sided form, which are frequently somewhat pushed over one another and bear, on primary tubercles, rod-shaped longitudinally striated spines. In addition secondary tubercles with smaller spines present. Af. narrow, straight, divided into two halves, on each side with 2 rows of alternating scaly plates beset with double pores; the rib-like raised middle of the Af. occupied by 2 rows of small poreless four-sided plates. Mouth five-sided; jaw apparatus with 5 pairs of powerful jaws. Anal opening large, covered with small plates forming a pyramid, situated in one of the IAf. between mouth and apex. Madreporic plate large, distinct, at the apex at the end of an IAf. Apical disc not preserved. Two species (*Echinocystites pomum* and *uva* Wyv. Thomson) from Upper Silurian beds of Leintwardine, England.

? ***Spatangopsis*** Torell (Acta Univ. Lundens. 1869). Wholly problematic body. Lower Silurian. Scania.

### 2. Order. Bothriocidaridae. F. Schmidt. (2)

Test spherical, regular. Anus in the apical disc, mouth in the centre of the underside. Interambulacral areas consisting of only one plate row.

---
(1) Wyv. Thomson, On a new palaeozoic group of Echinodermata. The Edinburgh new philosophical Journal 1861 vol. XIII p. 106.
(2) F. Schmidt, Miscellanea Silurica II. S. 36. Mém. Ac. imp. des Sciences nat. de St. Peterb. Tome XXI No. 11. 1874.

**[p. 481]**

***Bothriocidaris*** Eichwald emend. F. Schmidt (Fig. 339). Test spherical. Anus at the apex, opposite the mouth opening. Plates solid, immovably joined to one another in 15 vertical rows, of which 5 radii consist each of a double row of ambulacral plates, while the interambulacral areas possess only one row. Both kinds of plates similarly shaped, larger in the middle of the test, hexagonal, decreasing in size upward and downward.

Fig. 339. *Bothriocidaris Pahleni* Schmidt. Lower Silurian. Nömmis, Esthland. a nat. size. b apex, enlarged. c mouth, enlarged. (After F. Schmidt.)

Ambulacral plates with a circular pit in the middle, which contains 2 pores at its bottom. Finely longitudinally striated, very small spines sit in small number (2–4) on each ambulacral plate, and indeed on perforated small tubercles. (The latter are found also on the IAf. only in *B. globulus*.) In addition fine granulation present. Mouth opening surrounded by 10 reduced ambulacral plates (?), in front of which triangular oral plates are still recognizable. Jaw apparatus unknown. Apex with a ring of 5 larger and 5 smaller small plates. Within this ring lies the anal opening, which is closed by 6–8 elongate or egg-shaped anal valves, each of which bears a spine tubercle at its end. Madreporic plate known in one species.

Two species (*B. Pahleni* Schmidt and *globulus* Eichw.) from the Lower Silurian of Esthland.

### 3. Order. Perischoëchinidae. M'Coy (1)
(Tesselati Bronn.)

Test spherical, egg-shaped, regular. Anus in the apical disc. Mouth with jaw apparatus. Interambulacral areas (sometimes also Af.) with more than two plate rows.

---
(1) Literature.
M'Coy. Ann. and Mag. nat. hist. 1849. 2 ser. III. p. 253.
M'Coy and Griffith. Synopsis carbon. foss. of Ireland 1844. 2. Aufl. 1862.
Müller, Joh. Abh. Berl. Akad. 1856 S. 258 (*Lepidocentrus*).
Schultze, L. Monographie der Echinodermen des Eifeler Kalkes 1866. (*Lepidocentrus*.)
Roemer, F. Ueber *Melonites*. Wiegmann's Archiv 1855. I. S. 312.
Hall, J. Geological Survey of Iowa. Palaeontology 1858.

**[p. 482]**

To the Perischoechiniden belong those Palaeozoic Seeigel which are distinguished by a large number of plate rows in the IAf. and sometimes also in the Af. Mouth and anus lie diametrically opposite each other. The apical disc consists either only of 5 genital, or of 5 genital and 5 ocular plates; the former always bear more than one (2–5) pores, and the ocular plates too are sometimes doubly perforated. The jaw apparatus seems, so far as known, not to differ essentially from that of the Cidariten. A greater or lesser mobility of the test plates can be observed in most genera, and indeed the interambulacral plates mostly overlap one another with the upper (aboral) margin, while in the ambulacral areas the lower (adoral) margin projects over the next plate. Frequently in the many-rowed forms the plate rows decrease near the mouth and apex in such a way that only the outer ones form a whole meridian, while the inner ones, pushed back at both ends, reach neither mouth nor apex. For this reason one counts, as a rule, the greatest number of plates at the place of the greatest breadth diameter of the test. Quenstedt (loc. cit. p. 381) showed that the increase of the plate rows in *Melonites* sometimes takes place, as in the Crinoideen, by axillary bifurcation. Isolated plates from the inner rows of the IAf. of Perischoechiniden can be distinguished by their hexagonal form from the pentagonal plates of the younger Seeigel.

According to Lovén the following 3 families can be accepted:

#### 1. Family. Lepidocentridae. Lovén.

Interambulacral plates movably connected, scale-shaped, overlapping one another with the upper (aboral) and lateral margins. The adambulacral plates of the IAf. larger than the rest and beset with stronger and more numerous tubercles.

***Lepidocentrus*** Joh. Müll. (Fig. 340). Only incomplete fragments known. IAf. with 5, more rarely 9 rows of movable plates overlapping above and laterally; the lateral surfaces of the middle plate row unequal and bevelled on both sides. Lateral plates rather four- than six-sided, those of the **[p. 483]** outermost (adambulacral) row larger than the others, with rounded outer margin partly overhanging the Af. Af. narrow, consisting of 2 rows of low hexagonal, immovably connected plates, each of which bears one pore pair, these forming a simple vertical row. Spines very small, pointed; tubercles distant, small. Jaw apparatus present. The three species known so far come from Devonian deposits. *L. rhenanus* Beyr.

---

Fig. 340. a *Lepidocentrus Rhenanus* Beyr. Impression of the inner side of the test together with jaw apparatus in the sandstone of Wipperfürth, nat. size. (After Joh. Müller.) b–d *Lepidocentrus Muelleri* Schultze. Devonian limestone. Gerolstein, Eifel. (b ambulacral area, enlarged. c several interambulacral plates from outside, nat. size. d two isolated interambulacral plates with bevelled edges, nat. size.)

***Pholidocidaris*** Meek and Worthen. According to the preserved fragments up to 100 mm in diameter. Af. fairly broad, with 6 rows of small, unequal, oval or rhombic rounded plates, which overlap with their adoral margin and bear a tubercle of moderate strength. Double pores in a broad depression. IAf. with 5 or more rows of thin, rounded, convex, granular, unequal plates, which overlap one another laterally and with the aboral margin. Those of the adambulacral rows are 3–4 times larger than the rest. Apical disc unknown; an isolated plate that was found is provided with a central small tubercle and 6 or 7 pores, which stand parallel to the outer margin. On the underside (?) of the body the interambulacral plates bear a central perforated primary tubercle. The largest spines 25 mm long, finely striated. Single species (*Ph. irregularis* M. W.) in the Carboniferous limestone of Illinois.

***Perischodomus*** M'Coy. Spherical or roundish five-sided. Af. narrow, composed of numerous low plates, between which wedge-shaped plates are inserted in small number, reaching the lateral suture with their point. IAf. broad, consisting of 5 rows of unequal plates, of which the adambulacral ones are larger than the others and bear perforate, non-crenulate spine tubercles. Other tubercles small, granular, densely crowded. In the apical disc 5 genital plates with 6–8 pores (Keeping). Spines cylindrical, smooth. Single species *P. biserialis* M'Coy. Carboniferous limestone. Wexford, England.

**[p. 484]**

***Rhoëchinus*** Keeping. Small, spherical (?), IAf. of 5 rows of finely granulated plates, of which the adambulacral ones larger than the rest. Af. with 2 plate rows, narrow. Carboniferous limestone. Wexford, England. *Rh. irregularis* Keep.

#### 2. Family. Melonitidae. Zitt.
(Palaechinidae M'Coy.)

Interambulacral plates covered only with granules, without spine tubercles. Plates either normally connected or weakly overlapping.

***Palaechinus*** Scouler (Fig. 341). Spherically inflated. Af. narrow, with 2 rows of numerous low plates, each of which is provided with one pore pair. In the broad IAf. 4–7 plate rows beset with granular tubercles. Apical disc (known in 2 species) either consisting only of 5 genital plates (*P. sphaericus* de Kon.), of which 4 are provided with 3, the 5th with one pore, or formed of 5 genital and 5 ocular plates, which enclose an anal space covered with numerous small plates. In the latter case the genital plates are pierced by 3, the ocular plates by 2 pores (*P. elegans* M'Coy). One Upper Silurian (*P. Phillipsiae* Forbes) and 8 Carboniferous species in Ireland and North America.

Fig. 341. a *Palaechinus elegans* M'Coy. Carboniferous limestone. Nat. size. Ireland. b Apical disc of *Palaechinus*, enlarged. (After Baily.)

Fig. 342. *Melonites multipora* Norw. Carboniferous limestone. St. Louis, Missouri. Apex, enlarged. (After Meek and Worthen.) The pores on the ocular plates are not drawn by M. and W.

***Melonites*** Norwood and Owen (*Melonechinus* Meek and Worth., *Melechinus* Quenst.) (Fig. 342). Large, elliptical, with 5 broad meridional furrows, in which lie the depressed Af., somewhat raised in the middle. IAf. very broad and convex, with 7 (up to 8?) plate rows in the equatorial region, which reduce toward the apex to 4 or even **[p. 485]** to 2. The middle of these plates are hexagonal, the lateral ones pentagonally truncated. Af. divided into 2 halves, each of which contains 4–5 rows of small, rounded (? scaly overlapping) plates beset with double pores. Jaw apparatus present. Apical disc with 5 equally large genital and 5 ocular plates, the former with 2–5 pores, the latter almost quadrangular with 2 fine pores (after Roemer). Small tubercles small, crowded, imperforate. *M. multipora* Norw. and Ow. Very common in the Carboniferous limestone of St. Louis, North America; also demonstrated in the Carboniferous limestone of Moscow, Derbyshire and Ardoisière.

***Oligoporus*** Meek and Worthen. Like *Melonites*, but Af. on each half with only 2 rows of pore plates, between which one notices individual half plates. Carboniferous limestone of Illinois, North America.

***Lepidesthes*** Meek and Worth. Body ellipsoidal (?). Af. very broad, consisting of ca. 10 rows of small, alternating plates overlapping with the lower (adoral) margin. IAf. only half as broad as the Af., with 6 or 7 rows of plates overlapping with the upper (aboral) margin. Small tubercles very small. Jaw apparatus observed. In the Carboniferous limestone of Illinois. (*L. Coreyi* M. W.)

***Protoëchinus*** Austin. Af. with 3 rows of overlapping plates; very incompletely known. Carboniferous limestone. Wexford, England.

#### 3. Family. Archaeocidaridae. M'Coy.

All interambulacral plates provided with a large spine tubercle, more or less strongly overlapping.

***Archaeocidaris*** M'Coy (*Echinocrinus* Ag., *Palaeocidaris* Desor) (Fig. 343). Originally founded on isolated hexagonal plates with a large spine tubercle. The perforated tubercle head sits on a conical neck and **[p. 486]** rises out of a ring-shaped depression of the same. The spine tubercle is surrounded by a fairly large areole, which is bordered by a granulated ring. More complete specimens from the Carboniferous limestone of Burlington in Iowa were first described by J. Hall. According to these the IAf. consist of 3–8 rows of hexagonal plates adorned with a powerful spine tubercle, which overlap one another laterally and with the upper (aboral) margin. The adambulacral plates are pentagonal. Af. narrow, with 2 rows of small, irregular, smooth pore plates. Mouth with jaw apparatus. Spines powerful, long, cylindrical or angular, beset with spirally arranged thorns. Numerous species in the Carboniferous limestone of Ireland, Belgium, the Rhineland, Russia and North America.

Fig. 343. *Archaeocidaris Wortheni* Hall. Carboniferous limestone. Burlington, Iowa. (After Hall.) a fragment of the test from the underside with jaw apparatus, nat. size. b an interambulacral plate from above and from the side. c ambulacral area, enlarged. d a spine, nat. size.

***Eocidaris*** Desor. Like the preceding genus, but the tubercle heads of the IA plates not surrounded by a ring-shaped elevation. Areole with or without ring of granules. In the Devonian of North America (Hall); in the Carboniferous limestone and especially in the Dyas of Europe, but mostly only isolated plates. *E. Keyserlingi* Gein. Dyas.

***Lepidocidaris*** Meek and Worthen. Diameter 110–120 mm; spherical, somewhat depressed. Af. narrow, weakly convex, with overlapping plates, of which whole plates alternate with wedge-shaped half plates whose points are directed toward the median suture. IAf. very broad, with 8 (9?) or more plate rows, of which the middle ones are hexagonal, the outer pentagonal, and all overlap laterally and with the lower (aboral) margin. Each IA plate bears a strong, central, perforated spine tubercle surrounded by an areole. Spines cylindrical, thin, finely striated, perforated and swollen at the articular head. Jaw apparatus known. *L. squamosus* M. and W. Carboniferous limestone of Burlington, Iowa.

***Lepidechinus*** Hall. Af. narrow, with 2 plate rows which overlap one another with the upper (aboral) margin. IAf. very broad, with 9–11 rows of hexagonal plates overlapping with the lower and lateral margins, of which the adambulacral ones are smaller than the rest. Mouth small. Apical disc pentagonal; in one corner, instead of the madreporic plate, a ring of small plates. On the upper side the adambulacral IA plates bear the smallest spine tubercles, those of the inner rows ever stronger ones; on the underside tubercles are found only on the A plates. Devonian and Carboniferous limestone. North America. *L. imbricatus* Hall. Burlington, Iowa.

***Xenocidaris*** L. Schultze (*Palaeocidaris* Quenst.). Spines club-shaped; truncated at the upper end and beset with points and tubercles. Base depressed, perforated in the middle. Devonian. Eifel. *X. clavigera* Schultze.

***Anaulocidaris*** Zitt. (Fig. 344). Only isolated plates of transversely hexagonal **[p. 487]** or somewhat irregular form with bevelled lateral and lower (?) margins. Near the overlapping (aboral?) margin a perforated spine tubercle rises directly from the smooth surface, surrounded by no areole.

Fig. 344. *Anaulocidaris Buchi* Mstr., from the Upper Trias of St. Cassian, Tyrol. Two interambulacral plates and spines. Nat. size.

The two plates figured (Fig. 344) were communicated to me by Herr Goldschmidt and come from the Upper Trias of St. Cassian in Tyrol. Most probably the strangely flattened, broad and thin spines described as *Cid. Buchi* Mstr. belong to them.

## 2. Subclass. Euechinoidea. Bronn.
(Autechinidae Haeckel.)

Normal Seeigel whose test consists of 10 ambulacral and 10 interambulacral plate rows. Apical disc with 5 ocular and 5 or 4 mostly singly perforated genital plates. Jaw apparatus present or absent.

### 1. Order. Regulares. Desor.
(Endocyclica Wright; Reguläre Seeigel.)

Radial Seeigel of round (rarely elliptical) form. Peristome central, provided with jaw apparatus. Anal opening within the apical disc, opposite the mouth. Ambulacra simple, band-shaped, all alike.

The test of these markedly radially built Seeigel is circular, hemispherical, more rarely elliptical, composed, with the exception of a single genus (*Tetracidaris*), of 5×2 ambulacral and 5×2 interambulacral plate rows. In *Tetracidaris* the two interambulacral rows do indeed divide, at some distance from the apex, into 4 rows each, and thereby recall the Palaeozoic Perischoechiniden; since, however, the character of the apical disc, the ambulacra, the spine tubercles and all other essential characters agree completely with the typical Cidariten and especially with the genus *Diplocidaris*, one may indeed interpret the increase of the plate rows as an atavistic reversion to the older ancestral forms, but may not, on the basis of this single character, erect for *Tetracidaris* a special order between Perischoechinidae and Cidaridae.

Despite the pronounced radial structure there is also for the regular Seeigel an axis which divides the body into 2 equal halves and corresponds to the median plane of the irregular Seeigel. It is determined by the madreporic plate, which according to the acute **[p. 488]** observations of Lovén (cf. p. 470) always lies at the upper end of the right anterior ambulacrum. If a regular Seeigel is oriented accordingly, the principal axis falls through an anterior unpaired ambulacrum, through the apical disc and through the middle of the posterior interambulacral area. One thus obtains, as in the Spatangiden, an anterior trivium and a posterior bivium. The mouth is always bisected by the principal axis, whereas the anal opening located in the apical disc can be shifted somewhat sideways by supernumerary (suranal) plates (Salenidae). In all Regulares one finds in the apical disc 10 plates separated by sutures, of which all the genital plates are pierced by a usually clearly visible pore, the ocular plates on the other hand by a very fine pore, as a rule visible only with the lens. The anterior right genital plate functions as madreporic plate and is either of spongy porous structure or provided with a slit-like opening.

From the mouth opening project the pointed teeth of the powerful masticatory apparatus; on the inner side the peristome is strengthened by auriculae. The two lateral pieces of the auriculae are either grown onto the last interambulacral plates, which form the mouth margin (Cidaridae), or they rest on the last immovably connected ambulacral plates of the test (Glyphostomata). In the former, the older ambulacral plates, which are pushed toward the mouth by the plates continually newly inserted at the apex, pass unhindered under the arch of the auriculae, detach themselves, after passing these, from the firm test and, spreading out, form in the mouth region scaly plate rows provided with pores, which alternate with similar rows detached from the interambulacral areas and together with these cover the greater part of the mouth opening (Fig. 345). The actual mouth with **[p. 489]** the jaw apparatus lies in the centre of this space, whose loose plates only very rarely remain preserved in fossil specimens (*Cid. coronata* Quenst. Petref. 62. Fig. 100).

Fig. 345. *Cidaris tribuloides* Lam. Peristome with plate covering, enlarged.

If the auriculae stand, as in the Echinidae, on the last ambulacral plates of the firm test, then a pushing-through and a detachment of the older plates into the mouth region during further growth of the test is not possible. The latter is therefore not plated, but covered by a skin-like membrane, sometimes overlaid with scaly calcareous platelets.

With these relations the character of the ambulacral plates stands in the most intimate connection. In the first group (Cidaridae) the primarily formed plates do not change; each remains independent and bears on its side a pore pair (Fig. 346 a and b). In the Glyphostomen, on the other hand, the auriculae sitting on the last ambulacral plates of the test offer an insurmountable resistance to the pushing-through into the mouth region and form a firm boundary against the latter. Therefore, through the insertion of new plates at the apex, a pressure is exerted on those formed earlier, whereby 2, 3 and more of them fuse into compound so-called large plates, which increase in breadth and height the farther they move from the apex. From the number of pore pairs one can always determine exactly from how many primary plates a compound large plate has grown together. The sutures of the primary plates are either clearly visible and run either transversely across the whole large plate, or the primary plates are wholly or partly reduced to half plates of unequal size and unequal form, whose sutures do not reach the inner margin of the large plates (Fig. 347 c, d). Sometimes the sutures of the primary plates are completely effaced, so that the composition of the large plates is indicated only by the number of pore pairs. The double pores of the compound ambulacral plates lie close to the margin adjoining the interambulacral area and form either, on each side, a simple meridional band composed of pore pairs (pori unigemini), or, through lateral alternating displacement of the primary plates, 2 or 3 rows of double pores arise (pori bigemini and trigemini).

Fig. 346. a *Rhabdocidaris*. A row of primary ambulacral plates with yoked pores. b *Tetracidaris*. Primary ambulacral plates with oval pores arranged in two double rows.

**[p. 490]**

According to the number of fused primary plates Desor distinguishes Oligopori (Fig. 347 a, b), in which each large plate bears only 3–4 pore pairs, and Polypori, when their number becomes greater (Fig. 347 c, d). In the latter the pore pairs stand in 3 or 4 vertical rows, or they form arcs around the outer side of the spine tubercle of each large plate (Fig. 347 c).

The number and position of the pore pairs have been utilized for the systematics of the regular Seeigel, and thereby in fact good practical characters for distinguishing the genera have also been found. Since, however, in early youth (sometimes also in the adult state near the apex) the pore pairs of all compound large plates are in a single row, these characters evidently possess only secondary importance.

When the auriculae sit on the first ambulacral plates and prevent the passage of these into the peristomial region, one sees as a rule on both sides of the Af. more or less deep notches in the margin of the peristome, from which the buccal gills emerge. These notches are absent in all regular Seeigel with plated peristome; they thus furnish a valuable clue for the determination of the fossil forms, in which the original character of the peristome cannot be ascertained by direct means.

In general the regular Seeigel with plated peristome are distinguished by very narrow Af. (Angustistellati), while the forms with membranous mouth membrane possess fairly broad Af. (Latistellati). In the Saleniden the mouth region is indeed covered with scaly plates, but these form no direct continuation of the Af. and IAf., and the former are never provided with double pores. Corresponding to this intermediate position, narrow and broader Af. are found here. The mouth skin of the Latistellaten contains, immediately around the mouth opening, 10 pore **[p. 491]** platelets (buccal plates), which are probably to be interpreted as first primary plates.

Fig. 347. a *Pseudodiadema variolare*. An ambulacral large plate composed of 4 whole primary plates. b *Micropedina Cotteaui* Coq. Ambulacral large plate consisting of one half and 2 very unequal whole primary plates. c *Strongylocentrotus Droebachensis* Lovén. d *Stomopneustes variolaris*. Ambulacral large plate composed of 13 primary plates.

The jaw framework of the regular Seeigel (the Laterna Aristotelis or Diogenis) is composed of 5 jaw pairs standing upright in a circle (Fig. 348 a) (Kinnladen, alveoli) of three-sided pyramidal form, whose points are directed downward and which together form a hollow cone. Each jaw pair consists of 2 equal halves (jaw halves), whose broad, horizontally striated outer surfaces lie directly against the next jaw pair. They enclose a long, narrow, somewhat curved tooth (b), whose enamel-covered point projects below. At the upper broad end of a jaw stand so-called supplementary pieces (c) (articular epiphyses), which sometimes unite above the median plane of the jaw and fuse completely. Thus each of the 5 principal pieces of the dentition can be divided into 4 paired pieces (2 jaw halves and 2 supplementary pieces) and one unpaired (tooth). To the 5 three-edged jaws are now added, on the upper broad base of the hollow cone, 5 radial horizontal bars (d) (rotulae, falces, Schaltstücke), which roof over the line of junction between two jaws, turn their smooth surface upward and rest externally on the supplementary pieces (articular epiphyses). If the dentition is complete, one sees above the rotulae 5 more thin, curved bones, somewhat thickened at both ends, the bow pieces (Fig. 348 e) or compasses, which easily fall apart into 2 pieces and belong to the suspensory apparatus of the jaw framework.

The jaw apparatus undergoes various deviations in the different genera, especially in the form of the supplementary pieces and rotulae. For the palaeontologist, however, these have no practical interest, since jaw apparatuses are altogether rarely found and even then are mostly preserved only fragmentarily. As a rule one finds only jaws, **[p. 492]** teeth and bars, either in connection or isolated.

Fig. 348. Jaw framework of *Sphaerechinus*. a upper base of the hollow cone (a jaw halves, b tooth, c supplementary pieces, d radial bars [rotulae], e bow pieces [compasses]. The jaw marked z lacks all the covering pieces of the base; on those marked w only the supplementary pieces rest; at y the suture of two jaws is concealed by a bar, and at x the compasses are also present above the bars. b a single jaw, from the side; c from outside, nat. size. (Letters as in Fig. a.)

The complementary pieces and the compasses [Bügelstücke] occur extremely rarely as fossils.

Characteristic of the regular Seeigel is the powerful development of the tubercles and of the spines seated upon them. Of the primary tubercles there always stands only a single one on each small plate; its head is sometimes perforated, sometimes imperforate, enclosed by a smooth or radially crenulated ring of the tubercle neck (smooth or crenulated tubercles), and surrounded by a smooth, circular or elliptical areole (scrobicule). The latter is in turn bounded by a raised ring of small tubercles (cercle scrobiculaire).

Besides the primary tubercles there also occur secondary tubercles and imperforate granular tubercles (granules).

Although the regular Seeigel form a natural and sharply delimited group, it is nevertheless difficult to distinguish well-characterised families within them. Albin Gras divided them into Angustistellées and Latistellées, and this division was retained, with some modifications, by Desor, who however placed beside them the Salenidae as a special tribe distinguished by supernumerary plates in the apical disc. Th. Wright distinguishes 5 families: Cidaridae, Hemicidaridae, Diadematidae, Echinidae and Salenidae; G. Cotteau: Salenidae, Cidaridae, Diadematidae and Echinidae. Taking into account the most recent works of Lovén, Al. Agassiz and P. de Loriol, I have adopted the following 4 families:

1. The family of the Cidaridae (Cotteau and Wright) corresponds exactly to the tribe of the Angustistellées of Desor and contains mostly large Seeigel with narrow Af. [ambulacral areas] and broad IAf. [interambulacral areas], which bear rather few, large spine-tubercles. The ambulacral plates continue as scaly pore-plates over the margin of the peristome as far as the actual mouth opening. Each ambulacral plate bears only a single pair of pores. Notches in the corners of the peristome are absent. According to Loriol this family forms a group standing opposed to all the other regular Seeigel (Holostomes).

2. Salenidae Desor. Apical disc with supernumerary plates. Af. mostly very narrow (*Salenia*), more rarely moderately broad (*Acrosalenia*). Corners of the peristome with weak notches. Peristome with poreless calcareous scales and 10 perforated buccal plates. Ambulacral plates mostly simple (*Salenia*), more rarely fused into large plates with single-rowed pore-plates.

3. The third family (Echinothuridae Wyv. Thoms.) was only recently discovered. It is distinguished by movably overlapping plates of the test. The peristome is covered with rows of small plates, some of them bearing pores, and is without notches. Af. fairly broad, primary plates all separate, with one pair of pores.

4. Glyphostomata Pomel. Apical disc normal. Peristome with a membranous skin and 10 small perforated buccal plates. Corners of the peristome with branchial **[p. 493]** notches. Af. broad, consisting of compound large plates or of primary plates united in groups, mostly by threes.

Here belong the subfamilies Diadematidae, Echinidae.

The regular Seeigel begin in the Dyas formation, attain a very considerable development of forms already in the Upper Jurassic and the Cretaceous, and continue through the Tertiary formation into the present day.

### 1. Family. Cidaridae. Wright.

(Angustistellées Desor, Holostomes Pomel.)

*Test round, often globular. Af. narrow, more or less wavily curved, covered only with granular tubercles, consisting of simple primary plates, whose pore-pairs form one (rarely two) rows. IAf. very broad, with 2 rows of large spine-tubercles. Apical disc formed of 5 large, simply perforated genital and 5 triangular ocular plates; the right anterior genital plate is at the same time the madreporite plate and of porous texture. Peristome without notches, covered with scale-like overlapping plates, which are arranged in rows and pass directly into the Af. and IAf. The ambulacral rows are provided with pore-pairs as far as the mouth opening (Fig. 345). Spines very powerfully developed, distinguished by size and manifold ornamentation.*

***Cidaris*** Klein emend. Lam. (*Coronocidaris* Quenst., *Papula* Bayle, *Gymnocidaris*, *Prionocidaris*, *Chondrocidaris*, *Phyllacanthus* p. p. Al. Ag.) (Fig. 349. **[p. 494]** 350). Medium-sized, circular, globular, apex and underside equally flattened. Pore-zones wavily curved, formed on each side of one row of unconjugate, roundish pore-pairs, mostly somewhat projecting. Af. narrow, curved, with 4 (rarely 2 or 6) rows of granular tubercles. IAf. at least four times as broad as the Af. and provided with 2 rows of very large spine-tubercles. Tubercles perforated (rarely imperforate, *C. clavigera* Ram.), crenulated or smooth. Areoles large, sometimes strongly depressed. Apical disc very large, not very solid and rarely preserved fossil. Genital plates elongate, six- to four-sided, ocular plates five- to three-sided. Peristome roundish, of nearly the same size as the apical area. Spines powerful, of very varied form, rod-shaped, cylindrical, club-, acorn-, spindle-shaped, prismatic, etc., often beset with points and granules which are arranged in longitudinal rows.

To this genus, rich in forms, belong over 200 species, of which the oldest appear in the Trias. The main development takes place in the Upper Jurassic and the Cretaceous. Of many species, moreover, only the spines are known.

Various spines and isolated plates in the Carboniferous limestone and the Dyas have indeed been assigned to *Cidaris*, but their determination is quite uncertain. The extra-Alpine Trias has so far yielded no complete specimen of *Cidaris*, but individual plates and thin rod-shaped spines of *C. grandaeva* Goldf. and *C. subnodosa* Meyer are found not too rarely in the Muschelkalk. The Alpine Trias is very rich in remains of Cidariten, particularly in the region of St. Cassian in Tyrol. The short, thick, club-shaped spines of *C. dorsata* Mstr. (Fig. 350 b), *Roemeri* Wissm., *alata* Ag. (Fig. 350 a) lie about in thousands, whereas here too whole tests or **[p. 495]** even isolated fragments of the test are among the rare finds. All the species are distinguished by small size.

Fig. 349. *Cidaris coronata* Goldf., from the White Jurassic (γ) of Hossingen, Württemberg. a Specimen with completely preserved apical disc, from above; b from the side. c Piece of an Af., strongly enlarged. d partly restored view from the side with spines.

Fig. 350. Spines of various *Cidaris* species. a *C. alata* Ag. and b *C. dorsata* Braun. Upper Trias. St. Cassian. c *C. florigemma* Phil. Coral Rag. Wiltshire. d *C. cyathifera* Ag. Cretaceous. St. Agnan. e *C. glandaria* Lang. Cretaceous. Palestine.

In the Rhaetic stage of the Alps tests and spines of *Cidaris Cornaliae* Stopp. and some other species occur in Lombardy, near Hindelang in the Allgäu, and elsewhere.

The Lias is poor in *Cidaris*; on the other hand the Middle and especially the Upper Jurassic are distinguished by numerous, mostly excellently preserved species of various sizes. In South Germany and Switzerland *C. coronata* Goldf. (Fig. 349) with short, granulated, cylindrical spines, the small *Cid. elegans* Mstr. and *Cid. marginata* Goldf. with uncrenulated tubercles, in North Germany, France and England *C. florigemma* Phill. (Fig. 350 c) are the most important and most widely distributed forms.

The Lower Cretaceous contains a fairly large number of species in Switzerland and France; in Germany and England chiefly spines occur. In the Middle Cretaceous (Cenomanian) of Palestine there occur, according to Fraas (Aus dem Orient II. S. 22), the acorn-shaped spines of *C. glandaria* Lang (Fig. 350 e), known under the name "Judensteine" [Jews' stones], which frequently reached Europe by way of pilgrims. Individual species (*Cidaris sceptrifera* Mant., *C. subvesiculosa* d'Orb., *C. perlata* Sorignet etc.) occur in excellent preservation in the Upper, White Chalk. In comparison with the Cretaceous formation the Tertiary is poor in Cidariten; they are still most frequent in the so-called Nummulitic formation; in the Miocene and Pliocene they recede more and more. The Recent species live mostly in the tropics.

***Dorocidaris*** Al. Ag., ***Stephanocidaris*** Al. Ag. Recent.

***Rhabdocidaris*** Desor (Fig. 351. 352). Like the preceding, but pore-zones broader and consisting of conjugate pores; spine-tubercles perforated and crenulated (very rarely smooth); the granular tubercles of the Af. small and not very regularly arranged. Spines enormously large, rod-shaped, thorny or granular. The mostly very large species of this genus are found chiefly in Jurassic and Cretaceous deposits. Main distribution in the Brown (*Rh. maxima* Goldf. sp.) and White Jurassic **[p. 496]** (*Rh. nobilis* Münst., *Rh. princeps* Desor). Fine silicified specimens occur especially near Streitberg in Franconia and Nattheim in Württemberg.

Fig. 351. *Rhabdocidaris nobilis* Desor, from the uppermost White Jurassic of Kelheim-Winzer, Bavaria. a Fragment of test in nat. size. b Ambulacral plates, enlarged.

Fig. 352. Spine of *Rhabdocidaris horrida* Merian. From the Dogger.

***Leiocidaris*** Desor (*Phyllacanthus* Brandt). Like *Rhabdocidaris*, but spine-tubercles uncrenulated; Af. narrow and curved. Spines rod-shaped, smooth or ornamented with fine rows of granules or longitudinal striae. Rare in the Cretaceous, more frequent in the Tertiary, and 6 living species.

***Polycidaris*** Quenst. Medium-sized, high, round. Pore-zones straight. Pores simply paired, conjugate. Af. very narrow, beset with only 2 rows of granular tubercles. IAf. with 2 rows of crenulated and perforated primary tubercles, which stand close beside one another in great number and whose areoles touch directly and are not enclosed by a ring of small tubercles. Zigzag sutures of the IA plates smooth, without small tubercles. Upper Jurassic. *P. multiceps* Quenst. Lochen near Balingen.

***Goniocidaris*** Desor. Recent.

***Temnocidaris*** Cotteau. Large, circular; pore-zones fairly broad, consisting of 2 conjugate simple rows of pores. Af. narrow, weakly curved, with numerous granular tubercles. IAf. broad, with 2 rows of large perforated primary tubercles. On both the ambulacral and the interambulacral plates there are numerous roundish, pore-like impressions between the tubercles. Uppermost Cretaceous. *T. magnifica* Cott.

***Porocidaris*** Desor (Fig. 353). Areoles of the perforated and crenulated primary tubercles enclosed by a circle of pores, which lie in shallow radiating depressions. Spines flattened, with sharp, serrated lateral margins. Eocene (in the Vicentin, Egypt). Miocene, and two living species.

Fig. 353. *Porocidaris Schmideli* Goldf. Interambulacral plate and spine from the Nummulitic limestone of Mokattam near Cairo.

***Diplocidaris*** Desor. Body large, circular. Pore-zones broad, consisting of pore-pairs which are shifted alternately now to the right, now to the left, so that they form 2 rows. Af. very narrow, mostly with only 2 rows of granular tubercles. IAf. broad, with very large primary tubercles arranged in 2 rows, crenulated and perforated, surrounded by broad areoles. Middle and Upper Jurassic. *D. gigantea* Ag. sp., *D. alternans* Quenst. Nattheim.

***Tetracidaris*** Cotteau (Fig. 354). Test large, circular. Pore-zones straight, broad, pores unconjugate, the pairs form 2 rows in each zone. Af. in the middle with 2 rows of granular tubercles and fine granulation. IAf. composed of 4 rows of plates, which near the apex suddenly reduce to 2 rows. Each plate bears one large, crenulated, perforated primary tubercle provided with an areole. Spines thin, long, subcylindrical. Single species in the Lower Cretaceous (Neocomian) of southern France.

***Orthocidaris*** Cotteau. Test medium-sized, globular. Pore-zones straight, narrow, consisting of 2 simple rows of pores. Af. fairly narrow, with **[p. 497]** numerous, very small, unordered granular tubercles. IAf. broad, finely granulated, with 2 rows of fairly small, distantly spaced, smooth, perforated primary tubercles. Spines unknown. The single species (*O. inerme* Cotteau) occurs in the Lower Cretaceous (Valanginian).

Fig. 354. *Tetracidaris Reynesi* Cotteau from the Neocomian of Vergons near Castellane, Basses-Alpes. a Specimen ½ nat. size. b Fragment of test, nat. size. c Section of the test. d Ambulacral plates, enlarged. (After Cotteau.)

### 2. Family. Salenidae. (1) (Desor.) Wright.

*Test circular or roundish pentagonal. Pore-zones narrow, straight or wavily curved, pores roundish, in a single double row on each side. Ambulacral areas narrow, or moderately broad, consisting of simple or compound plates, beset either only with granules or with small spine-tubercles. Interambulacral areas broad, with 2 rows of large, crenulated spine-tubercles. Apical disc large, solid, consisting of 5 genital, 5 ocular and one, two or more supernumerary plates, of which the latter are enclosed by the 10 others and push the anal opening more or less out of the centre. The right anterior genital plate, which functions as madreporite plate, is mostly difficult to recognise, often distinguished only by a simple slit or a distorted pore, more rarely of distinctly porous structure. Peristome roundish, large, decagonal, with weak notches in the corners, covered by imperforate calcareous scales; around the mouth opening with 10 perforated buccal plates.*

The constitution of the apical disc, in particular the presence of one or more central plates, by which the anal opening situated in the apex becomes excentric, characterises very sharply this family, which contains almost only small forms.

---
(1) Cotteau, Note sur la famille des Salénidées. Bull. Soc. géol. de France 1861. 2 Sér. vol. XVIII p. 614. Duncan M., On Salenidae. Ann. Mag. nat. hist. 1877. XX. 1. 70. 245; 1878 vol. II p. 59.

**[p. 498]**

They were long known only as fossils, until five living species were discovered through the deep-sea researches of modern times. All Jurassic forms have perforated, the Cretaceous and living ones smooth tubercle heads.

Since, according to Al. Agassiz, in the regular Seeigel with mouth notches the anal opening is closed in early youth by a single plate, which is later as a rule resorbed, the Saleniden bear, through the persistence of this plate, an embryonic stamp and represent, as it were, permanent juvenile states of the Cidaridae and Echinidae.

For the orientation of the Saleniden, as in all other Seeigel, the madreporite plate serves, which, moreover, was long overlooked in the fossil forms. L. Agassiz sought to accomplish the orientation by means of the supernumerary central plate and the anal opening. Since according to him both must fall in the bisecting axis of the body, he drew a straight line through the anus and the central plate to an ambulacrum, which was then designated as the anterior one. In this way Agassiz obtained forms in which the supernumerary plate stands behind, and others in which it stands in front of, the anal aperture (Ana- and Kata-Salenier Quenst.).

***Acrosalenia*** Ag. (*Milnia* Haime) (Fig. 355). Small or medium-sized. Af. moderately broad, with 2 rows of small, crenulated and perforated spine-tubercles. IAf. likewise with 2 rows of larger, crenulated, perforated tubercles. Apical disc little projecting, fairly large, granular, with 1 or several supernumerary central plates in front of the anus. Madreporite plate porous. Notches in the corners of the mouth deep. Spines thin, cylindrical, smooth. Lias. Middle and Upper Jurassic. Lower Cretaceous.

Fig. 355. *Acrosalenia hemicidaroides* Wright. Dogger. Stanton, Wiltshire. Nat. size. (After Th. Wright.)

***Pseudosalenia*** Cotteau. Like the preceding, but Af. narrow, curved and covered only with small granules. Apical disc somewhat projecting, provided with impressions, not granulated; distinguished from *Pseudocidaris* solely by the constitution of the apical disc. Upper Jurassic. (*Acrosalenia aspera* Ag.)

***Poropeltaris*** Quenst. Like the preceding, but tubercles of the IAf. uncrenulated and imperforate. Upper Jurassic. (*P. sculpto-punctata* Quenst. of Nattheim.)

***Heterosalenia*** Cotteau. Like *Pseudosalenia*, but anal opening shifted somewhat sideways out of the axis. Single species *H. Martini* Cotteau. Middle Cretaceous (Provencien).

**[p. 499]**

? ***Psilosalenia*** Quenst. Upper Jurassic.

***Peltastes*** Ag. (*Hyposalenia* Desor, *Salenocidaris* Al. Ag.) Test small, round, apex often somewhat flattened. Af. very narrow, wavily curved, with 2 rows of granular tubercles. IAf. broad, with 2 rows of crenulated, imperforate primary tubercles. Apical disc large, more or less ornamented, with a single central plate in front of the anal opening. Madreporite plate with a simple slit. Cretaceous, very rare in the uppermost Jurassic; most species (9) in the Lower, a single one in the Upper Cretaceous, and one (*Salenia varispina* Al. Ag.) living.

***Salenia*** Gray (*Cidarella* Desm.) (Fig. 356). Like *Peltastes*, only the anal opening shifted to the right out of the axis by the supernumerary apical plate. Main distribution in the Upper Cretaceous; rare in the Lower Cretaceous, in the Eocene (*S. Pellati* Cotteau), in the Miocene of Australia, and living at great depth (*S. Goësiana* Lovén).

Fig. 356. *Salenia scutigera* Gray. White Chalk. Charente. (After Cotteau.) a Specimen in nat. size from the side and from above. b Apical disc, enlarged.

***Goniophorus*** Ag. Like *Peltastes*; apical disc ornamented with small ridges which form triangles; at the base of the granular tubercles in the Af. small pore-like impressions. Middle Cretaceous.
