Peristome transversely egg-shaped, indistinctly two-lobed, shifted far forward. Anus marginal, on the hind side. Fascioles absent. Very common in the Lower, Middle and Upper Cretaceous. Tertiary in Australia.

*Fig. 394. a. b Holaster subglobosus Ag. Cenomanian. Rouen. Nat. size. c H. suborbicularis Defr. Apical shield, enlarged.*

***Cardiaster* Forbes.** Like *Holaster*, but heart-shaped, anterior Amb. situated in a deep furrow, frequently bounded by sharp edges, anus surrounded by a flattened area. Marginal fasciole present. Middle and Upper Cretaceous.

**[p. 536]**

***Homolampas* Al. Ag., *Palaeotropus* Lovén, *Pourtalesia* Al. Ag., *Platybrissus* Grube** (*Lissonotus* Al. Ag.), ***Aceste* Wyv. Thomson, *Aerope* Wyv. Thoms., *Calymne* Wyv. Thoms., *Cionobrissus* Al. Ag., *Spatagocystis* Al. Ag., *Echinocrepis* Al. Ag., *Genicopatagus* Al. Ag., *Urechinus* Al. Ag., *Cystechinus* Al. Ag.** Recent.

***Infulaster* Hagenow.** Like *Holaster*, but inflated, apex quite at the anterior end of the upper side. Unpaired anterior Amb. in a deep furrow bounded by sharp edges. Fasciola subanalis present. Upper Cretaceous.

***Hemipneustes* Ag.** Very large, inflated, just as high as broad, thick-shelled. Anterior Amb. in a deep, broad furrow reaching to the central apex. Paired Amb. not sunken, very broad, bounded by two very unequal pore-zones. In the anterior zone the pores are small and round, in the posterior the outer row is transversely elongated and the pairs are conjugate. Anus in the hind margin. Apical shield elongated. Numerous granular tubercles. Uppermost Cretaceous. *H. radiatus* Lam. sp. Maestricht.

b) Ambulacra subpetaloid.

***Palaeopneustes* Al. Ag.** The genus forms, together with *Asterostoma*, the transition from the Ananchytinen to the Spatangiden. Large, arched, elongate egg-shaped; flat below. Anterior margin without furrow. Anterior Amb. slightly diverging, with pore-pairs which pierce the middle of each plate. Paired Amb. subpetaloid, lanceolate, reaching to half the height of the upper side, but continuing to the mouth by distantly spaced pore-pairs. Apical shield compact, nearly central. Peristome transversely two-lipped. Anus on the truncated hind side. Upper side of the test covered with distantly spaced tubercles, which are arranged in regular horizontal rows. Tubercles more crowded and larger on the underside. Fascioles absent. Recent and Tertiary (*P. conicus* Dames) in the Scutella beds of Castel Zies in the Vicentino.

***Asterostoma* (Lam.) Ag. emend. Cotteau.** Very large, elongate or roundish, highly inflated above, flat below. Amb. subpetaloid; the anterior one different from the rest. Pore-zones in the latter with pairs of large round pores, which toward the lower margin abruptly become minute and scarcely visible, but enlarge again in the vicinity of the mouth and place themselves somewhat more crowded in faint depressions. In the anterior Amb. each plate bears only a very small pore. Peristome transverse, indistinctly two-lipped, sometimes subcentral, sometimes shifted strongly forward. Anal opening large, oval, somewhat above the hind margin. Apical shield round, not elongated. Tubercles small, crenulate and perforate. Miocene. West Indies.

### 3. Family. Spatangidae. Ag. emend. Loriol.

*Bilaterally symmetrical, usually heart-shaped Seeigel with peristome shifted far forward, transversely two-lipped or pentagonal, and unequal, leaf-shaped (petaloid) ambulacra. Apical shield compact. Anus supramarginal. Spine-tubercles of various sizes.*

Among all Seeigel the Spatangiden depart furthest from the radial structure; they are decidedly bilaterally symmetrical and are **[p. 537]** regarded as the highest-standing forms of the Echinoideen. Their mouth frequently moves quite into the vicinity of the anterior margin, the anus lies without exception in the truncated hind surface of the test. The anterior unpaired ambulacrum always differs by its narrower form and its more feebly developed pores from the rest, which however frequently also correspond only two and two. In the apical shield one counts 4 genital plates, of which as a rule 4, more rarely 3 or even only 2 are perforate; the right anterior one serves as madreporite plate and at the same time fills the whole middle of the apical shield; the fifth, posterior genital plate is absent. The 5 ocular plates are always provided with a fine pore. Through the extension of the madreporic body toward the middle and toward the rear, in the typical Spatangiden the lateral genital plates and the ocular plates of the bivium are separated from one another. Only some older genera (*Micraster*, *Epiaster*, *Hemiaster*) behave like the Holasteriden, in which the madreporic body never reaches the posterior interambulacrum, so that not only the two posterior ocular plates, but frequently even the lateral genital and ocular plates also touch one another.

*Fig. 395. Apical shield a of Micraster coranguinum Lam.(1), b of Brissopsis lyrifera Forb., strongly enlarged. (After Lovén.)*

*Fig. 396. Apical shield of Schizaster fragilis D. u. K., strongly enlarged. (After Lovén.)*

A characteristic peculiarity of most Spatangiden is formed by the fascioles (semitae), i.e. smooth or very finely granulated bands, which run at various places on the test between the tubercles and in living specimens are beset with fine bristles thickened at the end.

---
(1) This woodcut is erroneously designated as *Holaster* on p. 468.

**[p. 538]**

According to their position one distinguishes:

*fasciolae (semitae) peripetalae*, surround the leaf-shaped ambulacra;
*f. internae*, surround the apex together with the anterior ambulacrum;
*f. marginales*, run around the test at half its height or above the margin;
*f. subanales*, form a ring beneath the anus;
*f. laterales* (or *laterales-subanales*), branch off from the *f. peripetalae* and unite beneath the anus.

Lovén designates the Spatangiden provided with a subanal fasciole as Prymnodesmier, and as Prymnadeten those forms in which it is lacking. Strangely enough, the *fasciolae laterales* and *subanales* are mutually exclusive.

Through the advance of the mouth into the vicinity of the anterior margin, the distribution of the small plates in the Af. [ambulacral areas] and IAf. [interambulacral areas] becomes highly irregular, **[p. 539]** and in particular the posterior IAf. acquires an unusual extent on the underside. The term *plastron* is applied to the part of the posterior IAf. lying on the underside, bordered by tubercles and frequently somewhat raised. The IAf. reach the margin of the mouth with only a single small plate; behind this follow 2 large plates designated as *sterna*, and after these the paired *episterna*, which are likewise still fairly large. Sometimes the paired IAf. are pushed back entirely from the peristome by the ambulacral plates; under all circumstances, however, the posterior unpaired IAf. takes part in the formation of the peristome. In the bilabiate forms the first broad plate of the latter shows a crescent-shaped form and constitutes the usually somewhat raised lower lip of the peristome.

Fig. 397. Underside and part of the upper side of *Spatangus purpureus*, schematically projected into a plane.

For important information on the development and constitution of the peristome we are indebted to Lovén and Al. Agassiz. In young Spatangiden it occupies an almost central position and only gradually moves toward the anterior margin; the excellently transverse-bilabiate form, too, is acquired only late; at first it is always pentagonal. A single living genus (*Palaeostoma* Lovén) retains the pentagonal mouth opening even in the adult state, which is closed by 5 easily detached triangular valves. A number of fossil genera with a pentagonal peristome attach themselves to *Palaeostoma* (*Leskia*) and form a special group, to which stand opposed the typical Spatanginen with a bilabiate peristome covered by numerous small plates.

The determination of the fossil Spatangiden, which begin in the Lower Cretaceous but attain the full height of their development of forms only in the Tertiary and the present, is subject to considerable difficulties, because the fascioles, which are used preferentially for the distinction of the genera, are frequently scarcely recognizable any longer.

#### 1. Subfamily. Palaeostominae. Loriol.

Mouth pentagonal. Anus on the truncated posterior side.

Here belong chiefly fossil genera from the Cretaceous formation.

***Toxaster*** Ag. (*Echinospatagus* p. p. Breyn) (Fig. 398). Heart-shaped; paired Amb. unequal, broad, the pore-bands somewhat unequal, especially on the two anterior ones. Pores transversely elongated, especially those of the outer rows. Unpaired Amb. in a broad groove, bordered by two narrow, equal pore-bands with yoked double pores. Mouth approaching the **[p. 540]** anterior margin. Anus oval. Tubercles very small, crenulate and perforate, in addition fine granules. Fascioles lacking. Widespread in the Lower, rarely in the Middle Cretaceous. Individual species, e.g. *T. complanatus* Ag., very common.

Fig. 398. *Toxaster complanatus* Ag. Neocomian. Auxerre, Yonne. (Nat. size.)

***Heteraster*** d'Orb. Like the preceding, but the outer pore-rows of the anterior, unpaired Amb. unequal, now transversely elongated, now short. Lower Cretaceous. *H. Couloni* Ag. sp.

***Enallaster*** d'Orb. Heart-shaped; apex central. Paired Amb. unequal, mostly depressed. The two pore-bands of the two anterior paired Amb. very unequal, the anterior bands consisting of small equal pores which are inclined toward each other like a roof; in the posterior bands the pores of the anterior row are small, those of the outer row transversely slit-like and elongated. Pores of the two posterior Amb. uniform. Unpaired Amb. in a groove, the pore-bands formed of unequal, alternating pore-pairs. Peristome almost bilabiate, anus oval. Fascioles lacking. Cretaceous.

***Palaeostoma*** Lovén (*Leskia* Gray non Desor). Recent.

#### 2. Subfamily. Spatanginae. Loriol.

Peristome bilabiate. Anus marginal.

***Micraster*** Ag. (Fig. 399 and 395a). Large and medium-sized, heart-shaped or oval. Paired Amb. depressed, closed below, the two anterior longer than the posterior. Pore-pairs yoked, of one kind. Unpaired Amb. in a depressed groove with round pores arranged in distant pairs. Anus on the truncated posterior side, beneath it a subanal fasciole. Very widespread and usually excellently preserved in the Middle and Upper Cretaceous (*M. coranguinum* Ag.). Tertiary in Australia.

Fig. 399. *Micraster cortestudinarium* Goldf. sp. White Chalk. Meudon near Paris. Nat. size.

***Epiaster*** d'Orb. Like *Micraster*, but without fasciole. Middle and Upper Cretaceous.

**[p. 541]**

***Isaster*** Desor. Like *Epiaster*, but anterior Amb. not in a depressed groove and anus inframarginal. Middle and Upper Cretaceous. *I. aquitanicus* Ag. sp.

**?*Megalaster*** Duncan. (Quart. journ. XXXIII. p. 61). Low heart-shaped, large, flat below. Anterior Amb. effaced, in a groove which notches the lower margin especially deeply. Paired Amb. sunken, narrow and short, diverging obliquely forward and backward, pores yoked. Apex in front of the middle with 4 genital pores. Anus large, elliptical, high in the posterior margin. Tubercles minute. Fascioles? Tertiary. Australia.

***Hemiaster*** Desor (*Abatus* Trosch.) (Fig. 400). Medium-sized and small, now short and inflated, now broadly heart-shaped. Posterior paired Amb. much shorter than the two anterior, the unpaired one lying in a shallow groove. Amb. as in *Micraster*. Only *fasciola peripetala* present. Very widespread in the Cretaceous and Tertiary formation. Several living species recently discovered. *H. bufo* Brngt. sp. Cenomanian, *H. prunella* Lam. sp. Upper Cretaceous of Maestricht.

Fig. 400. *Hemiaster d'Orbignyanus* Desor. Middle Cretaceous. Martigues, Provence. (After d'Orbigny.) a, b, c specimen in nat. size from below, above and the side. d pores of the anterior unpaired ambulacrum. e pores of the paired ambulacra. Enlarged.

***Rhynobrissus*** Al. Ag., ***Tripylus*** Phil. Recent.

***Cyclaster*** Cotteau (*Brissopsis* p. p. Desor, *Pleraster* Quenst.). Like the preceding, but anterior Amb. not in a groove and, in addition to the *fasciola peripetala*, an *f. subanalis* also present. Eocene.

***Brissus*** Klein. Large, elongate heart-shaped. Apex excentric, moved far forward. Anterior Amb. almost effaced, either lying in a shallow groove or on the same level as the rest of the test. Paired Amb. somewhat depressed, the anterior strongly diverging, the posterior meeting at a very acute angle; anus large, situated in the middle of the posterior side. *Fasciola peripetala* very strongly inflected, following the Amb. *Fasciola subanalis* approaching the anus. Four genital pores, of which the two posterior are larger. Tubercles and spines fairly uniform. Tertiary. (Eocene, Miocene and Pliocene.) Recent.

***Meoma*** Gray (*Hemibrissus* Pomel, *Rhyssobrissus* Al. Ag.). Like the preceding, but paired Amb. deeply sunken and nearly equal. Recent.

***Brissopsis*** Ag. (*Kleinia* Gray, *Toxobrissus* Desor, *Deakia* Pavay, *Veerbekia* Fritsch). Unpaired Amb. lying in a groove with very small, distant pore-pairs. **[p. 542]** Paired Amb. somewhat depressed, unequal; the anterior longer and more strongly diverging than the posterior, frequently arcuate in form. Pores in the vicinity of the apex atrophied, so that a smooth surface arises around the very small apical disc. *Fasc. perip.* and *suban.* present. Tubercles very small. Tertiary and Recent.

***Metalia*** Gray (*Plagionotus* Ag., *Xanthobrissus* Al. Ag.). Like the preceding, only the surface enclosed by the subanal fasciole is provided at the margin with radial grooves, which proceed from the pores of the adjoining posterior Amb. Tertiary. Living.

***Linthia*** Merian (*Desoria* Gray non Ag., *Periaster* d'Orb.) (Fig. 401). Heart-shaped or egg-shaped. Apex more or less moved forward. Paired Amb. broad and depressed, the posterior shorter and less diverging than the anterior. Pore-pairs alike in all 4 Amb. Anterior Amb. deeply sunken in a groove, with 2 short pore-bands consisting of very small pores. Anus high in the posterior margin. *Fasc. perip.* narrow, complete, following the outer margin of the Amb. and projecting into the IAf. *Fasc. lateralis* departing in the vicinity of the posterior Amb. and running beneath the anus. Tubercles small, not sunk in areoles. Middle and Upper Cretaceous, Eocene, Miocene and living.

Fig. 401. *Linthia Heberti* Cott. Eocene. Lonigo near Vicenza. (After Dames.)

***Faorina*** Gray (*Atrapus* Troschel). Recent.

**[p. 543]**

***Schizaster*** Ag. (*Brisaster* Gray, *Nina* Gray) (Fig. 402). Like *Linthia*, but apex excentric, moved backward; paired Amb. very unequal, the anterior directed strongly forward, somewhat curved and approaching the groove of the unpaired Amb. The latter is deep and excavated at the margins. Pore-pairs of the anterior Amb. densely crowded and numerous. Principal distribution in the Tertiary, more rarely living. *Sch. rimosus* Desor. Eocene.

Fig. 402. *Schizaster Archiaci* Cott. Eocene. San Giovanni Illarione near Vicenza.

***Moira*** Al. Ag. (*Moera* Mich. non Leach.). Like the preceding, but Amb. very narrow, slit-shaped. Pliocene and Recent.

***Pericosmus*** Ag. Broadly heart-shaped. Apex excentric, moved forward. Anterior Amb. effaced, lying in a broad groove, with minute pores. Paired Amb. slightly sunken, differing little from one another. Pore-bands equal, with yoked double pores. Anus high on the posterior surface. *Fasc. perip.* moderately inflected; in addition a *fasc. marginalis*. Tubercles small. Eocene. Miocene.

***Prenaster*** Desor. Egg-shaped, rounded in front, truncated behind. Apex far forward. Anterior Amb. effaced, not depressed. Paired Amb. unequal, the anterior very strongly diverging, almost at right angles to the longitudinal axis of the body, slightly depressed; the posterior close to each other. Pores yoked, median field of the Amb. very narrow. Anus high up on the posterior side. The *fasc. perip.* developed only with its posterior half; the *fasc. marg.* bends upward beneath the anus and in front crosses the lower margin. A single species in the Upper Cretaceous, all the others Tertiary. *Pr. Alpinus* Desor.

***Agassizia*** Valenciennes. Small, inflated, egg-shaped. Apex in front of the centre. Unpaired Amb. not depressed, effaced. Paired Amb. slightly depressed, unequal, subpetaloid, the anterior longer than the posterior. Pore-bands of the anterior paired Amb. very unequal, the anterior narrow, linear, almost atrophied, consisting of minute paired pores; the posterior bands broader, with yoked pores. On the posterior paired Amb. the two pore-bands are nearly equal, broad, with yoked pores. Tubercles numerous, densely crowded, unequal, more strongly developed in the posterior half of the body. Peristome semicircular, in the vicinity of the anterior margin. Anus high in the posterior margin. *Fasc. perip.* developed only on the posterior half, connected with the *fasc. marg.* by a lateral branch. Eocene (*Periaster Souverbyi* Cott.), Miocene and living.

***Brissomorpha*** Laube. Large, heart-shaped, drawn out toward the rear like a beak. Apex moved forward. Amb. not depressed, the anterior effaced, the two anterior paired ones almost at right angles to the axis. Pore-bands narrow, pores round, not yoked, median region of the Amb. broad. Anus high **[p. 544]** in the posterior margin, roofed over by a projecting beak. *Fasc. perip.* and *suban.* present. Tubercles and spines small. Miocene.

***Brissopatagus*** Cotteau. Medium-sized, oval. Apex moved forward. Groove of the anterior Amb. slight on the upper side, deep at the margin. Paired Amb. short, very distant, lying in very broad, roundish depressions narrowed toward the front; the posterior longer, straight, the anterior somewhat curved, strongly diverging. Anus elliptical, on the posterior side. Fascioles? Eocene of Biarritz and Miocene of Java.

***Echinocardium*** Gray (*Amphidetus* Ag., *Amphidotus* Forbes, *Echinospatagus* p. p. Breyn). Heart-shaped, inflated. Apex behind the middle. Paired Amb. short, triangular, pointed below, unequal. A broad *fasc. interna* divides them into 2 unequal sections; in the inner, very short one near the apex the pores are minute, scarcely perceptible, in the outer one large, round or transversely elongated. Anterior Amb. long and distinct, lying in a groove. Anus oval, large, high in the posterior margin. *Fasc. interna* complete, encompassing the apical disc and running along the anterior Amb., which it crosses fairly far down. *Fasc. subanalis* enclosing a so-called "cushion" and sending off 2 branches which surround the anus. Tubercles very small, larger on the upper side than on the underside. Tertiary from the Eocene onward, and living.

***Gualteria*** Desor. Like the preceding, but without *fasc. subanalis*. Mouth surrounded by coarse folds, between which the ambulacral pores are situated. Eocene.

***Breynia*** Desor. Very large, like *Echinocardium*, but in addition to the *fasc. interna* and *subanalis* also a *fasc. peripetala*. The part of the upper side circumscribed by the latter is covered with large tubercles. Eocene, Miocene and Recent. *B. carinata* Haime.

***Lovenia*** Ag. (*Hemipatagus* p. p. Laube). Thin-shelled, elongate heart-shaped, low, truncated behind. Anterior Amb. in a shallow groove. Paired Amb. broad, (leaf-shaped or) almost triangular, pointed below. Anus sunken, high up in the posterior margin. Upper side, with the exception of the posterior IAf., with very large, scattered tubercles surrounded by deep areoles. Underside with larger and smaller tubercles, which form a dense pavement on the sides. *Fasc. interna* and a spectacle-shaped *fasc. analis* present. Tertiary (Miocene) and living.

***Macropneustes*** Ag. (Fig. 403). Large, heart-shaped. Apex central or in front of the centre. Paired Amb. equal, not or only slightly depressed. Unpaired Amb. effaced, lying in a groove. Apical disc compact, with 4 genital pores, the madreporic body band-shaped in the centre. Anus high in the posterior margin. Tubercles very unequal, on the upper side large, scattered, on the underside small and more crowded. Only *fasc. perip.* present. Tertiary, chiefly in the Eocene.

***Peripneustes*** Cotteau. Like the preceding, but anterior Amb. in a deep groove. Paired Amb. narrow and sunken. In addition to the *fasc. perip.* also a *fasc. suban.*; **[p. 545]** the former runs close beside the Amb., bends strongly inward and bounds the large tubercles. Eocene. Miocene.

Fig. 403. *Macropneustes Meneghinii* Desor. Eocene. Monte Spiado near Vicenza. (Nat. size.)

***Eupatagus*** Ag. (*Euspatangus* Cott.). Medium-sized or large, elongate heart-shaped, low. Anterior Amb. effaced, in a slightly depressed groove. Paired Amb. not depressed, broad, rounded and closed. The two anterior pore-bands reduced toward the apex. The *fasc. perip.* bounds large tubercles of the IAf. surrounded by areoles, which however are more weakly developed in the posterior IAf. The remaining tubercles outside the fasciole are smaller and more crowded. In addition a *fasc. subanalis* present. Eocene. Miocene. Pliocene and Recent. *E. ornatus* Ag. Biarritz.

***Maretia*** Gray (*Plagiopatagus* Lütken). Medium-sized or small, heart-shaped, low. Anterior Amb. scarcely visible in a slightly depressed groove. Paired Amb. leaf-shaped, long, almost closed toward the bottom, not depressed. Upper side, with the exception of the posterior IAf., with very large, distantly placed tubercles surrounded by deep areoles. Underside with smaller, more closely placed tubercles. *Fasciola subanalis* present. Plastron smooth. Tertiary and living.

**[p. 546]**

***Hemipatagus*** Desor (Fig. 404). Like the preceding, but fascioles lacking. Plastron of the underside covered with tubercles. Tertiary.

Fig. 404. *Hemipatagus Hofmanni* Goldf. Oligocene. Doberg near Bünde. a from above, b from the side, c from below. (Nat. size.)

***Spatangus*** Klein. Large, inflated, heart-shaped. Unpaired Amb. effaced, with very small pores, in a more or less deep channel. Paired Amb. broadly leaf-shaped, pointed below, pore-bands a little depressed. All the IAf. of the upper side are ornamented with large, scattered, perforate and crenulate tubercles sunk in areoles, and in addition with smaller, more crowded tubercles, which also cover the underside. Only a *fasc. suban.* present. Tertiary and living.

### Spatial and temporal distribution and phylogeny of the Echinoidea.

From the massed occurrence of individual fossil Echinoideen species it follows that the majority of these animals, like their still existing relatives, lived gregariously. Only rarely does one find Seeigel singly on our coasts; more often they keep in great numbers on rocky or sandy bottoms and sometimes literally cover the sea floor. Among the regular Gnathostomen, individual species, such as *Strongylocentrotus lividus*, bore cavities in rocks by means of their enamel-covered jaw tips, in which they remain for life. Thus the hard granite rocks of Brittany are in many places hollowed out, and according to Al. Agassiz one observes on the coast of California extensive stretches which appear perforated like honeycombs. In general the Seeigel love shallow water in the vicinity of the shore; certain families, such as the Clypeastriden, are even almost entirely restricted to sandy, shallow coastal stretches. Nevertheless the Echinoidea can by no means be designated as inhabitants of the beach or of shallow water, for individual genera live, as the deep-sea investigations have shown, in **[p. 547]** the greatest depths reachable at all. Frequently the deep-sea forms either belong to genera which already existed in the Mesolithic age, especially during the Cretaceous period, or they at least bear an antique stamp. Only a few years ago the family of the Saleniden was regarded as extinct and restricted to the Jurassic and Cretaceous formation; now 5 living species are known, which keep between 100 and 1850 fathoms depth. Still more strikingly does the agreement of the present-day deep-sea inhabitants with Mesozoic genera come to the fore in the subfamily of the Ananchytinen; this consists predominantly of Cretaceous genera and until most recently was held to be completely extinct. The dredge and the sounding lead, however, brought to light from depths between 375 and 2900 fathoms a considerable number of new genera, which place themselves directly at the side of those old forms. In many families, especially among the regular Seeigel, there are genera which predominantly inhabit the shallow waters of the coast, and others which inhabit rather the depths. Thus, for example, the genera *Dorocidaris* and *Porocidaris* are found in depths of ca. 4–600 fathoms, while *Strongylocentrotus*, *Echinus*, *Sphaerechinus*, *Echinometra*, *Acrocladia* and others live mainly in shallow water. Individual genera, e.g. *Cidaris*, possess species which occur only at great depth and others which occur only in the vicinity of the coast.

In the year 1874 Al. Agassiz enumerated 112 living species of regular Seeigel, 37 Clypeastridae, 11 Cassidulidae and 46 Holasteridae and Spatangidae, thus in all 206 living species. No fewer than 42 new forms, mostly living at great depth, have since been added to this list by the Challenger Expedition alone, and probably just as many species may have been discovered since 1874 by various other expeditions (1).

On the basis of geographical distribution Al. Agassiz distinguishes four great regions for the present-day Seeigel. 1. The Atlantic Realm comprises the whole arctic circumpolar region, the North Atlantic Ocean with the exception of the American coast, the Mediterranean and a piece of the North Pacific Ocean. Characteristic genera of this realm are: *Echinus*, *Sphaerechinus*, *Schizaster*, *Strongylocentrotus*, *Dorocidaris*, *Spatangus*, *Echinocyamus*, *Echinocardium*. 2. The American Realm extends from the Gulf of Mexico and the coast of Brazil right across the Atlantic Ocean to the west coast of Africa; to it belong further the North American east coast, the whole Pacific coastal strip of America, as well as the east coast **[p. 548]** of northern Asia as far as North China. Characteristic genera are: *Echinarachnius*, *Arbacia*, *Encope*, *Mellita*, *Hemiaster*. 3. To the Indo-Pacific Realm are reckoned the Indian Ocean (together with the Red and Arabian Seas) and the tropical parts of the Pacific Ocean. The most characteristic genera here are: *Phyllacanthus*, *Podophora*, *Acrocladia*, *Parasalenia*, *Fibularia*, *Echinostrephus*, *Laganum*, *Maretia*. 4. The Australian Realm surrounds the South Pole; to it belong the southern and a part of the western and eastern coasts of Australia and New Zealand, the Cape of Good Hope and the southern tip of America. The Seeigel fauna of this realm is very sharply distinguished from that of the three others. As typical genera are named: *Goniocidaris*, *Stephanocidaris*, *Centrostephanus*, *Salmacis*, *Holopneustes*, *Amblypygus*, *Eupatagus*, *Breynia*, *Linthia*.

---
(1) Al. Agassiz. Preliminary report on the "Challenger" Echini. Proceed. Americ. Acad. of Arts and Sciences 1879. vol. XIV. p. 190.

In each of these realms several littoral districts can again be distinguished, which are characterized by particular characteristic forms of restricted geographical distribution. On the whole, moreover, most Seeigel possess considerable areas of distribution, and as a rule the larger, the more considerable the depth at which they live. The cold regions are much poorer in genera and species than the tropical ones.

In comparison with the fossil forms, the still living Seeigel constitute only a modest fraction of the total number of all species. In the year 1860 Bronn counted 1465 fossil species; now their number may have risen to about 2000.

The oldest Echinoideen appear already in the Silurian formation. They are three rare genera, each of which represents a special family of the Palechinoideen. The Lower Silurian *Bothriocidaris* from Estonia represents perhaps the simplest form among all known Seeigel; *Palaechinus Phillipsiae* is the forerunner of the Perischoechiniden, and *Cystocidaris* seems to bring the Palechinoideen into connection with the later exocyclic forms.

In the Devonian formation various Perischoechiniden are found (*Lepidocentrus*, *Eocidaris*, *Xenocidaris*), upon which in the Carboniferous limestone a still greater number of genera follows. The western United States of North America (Illinois, Missouri, Iowa etc.), the region of Moscow, England, Belgium and the locality Ardoisière near Vichy recently discovered by Julien furnish the nonetheless scanty material on which our knowledge of the oldest Seeigel rests.

In the Zechstein only a few meagre remains of *Eocidaris* and *Archaeocidaris* occur, and probably one last straggler of the Palechinoideen passes over into the Alpine Trias.

**[p. 549]**

With the beginning of the Triassic formation (perhaps already in the Dyas) the normal Seeigel take the place of the Palechinoideen. However, the Triassic Echinoideen fauna is meagre; only the Keuper in the region of St. Cassian in Tyrol, as well as the *Avicula contorta* beds in the Bavarian Allgäu, in Lombardy and southern France have furnished a greater number of species, which without exception belong to the Regulares, and indeed to the families of the Cidaridae and Glyphostomata.

During the whole Palaeozoic age, and also still in the Trias, the Seeigel play a quite subordinate role in comparison with the Crinoideen and Mollusken; in the Jurassic formation the relation changes suddenly. Numerous new genera from the most varied families emerge all at once, and indeed frequently with such a lavish wealth of individuals that several geological horizons have received their names from Seeigel. (Cidariten beds, *Crenularis* beds, *Clypeus* beds.) This upswing of the Echinoideen begins, to be sure, only in the Lower Oolite, for the Lias period was evidently little favourable to the Seeigel. Apart from some Cidaridae, the Lias contains only a limited number of Diadematidae and Salenidae, as well as the three oldest irregular Seeigel (*Pygaster Reynesi*, *Galeropygus priscus* and *agariciformis*). The most widespread are the genera *Cidaris* and *Diademopsis*.

In the Middle Jurassic there join the already existing families Cidaridae, Salenidae and Glyphostomata, which increase considerably in genera and species, numerous exocyclic Seeigel, such as the Echinoconidae with the genera *Holectypus* and *Pileus*, the jawless Cassiduliden with *Hyboclypus*, *Galeropygus*, *Pyrina*, *Echinobrissus*, *Clypeus* and *Pygurus*, and the Holasteriden with the genera *Collyrites*, *Dysaster* and *Metaporhinus*. In spatial respect these are distributed over the whole area where Middle Jurassic beds are developed at all, yet France, Switzerland and England are distinguished by particular wealth of species.

The Jurassic Echinoideen fauna reaches the culmination of its development in the upper horizons of this formation, especially in the deposits designated as Coral Rag. Only a few genera, it is true, are added (*Pseudosalenia*, *Poropeltaris*, *Pileus*, *Pachyclypus*, *Infraclypeus*, *Pseudodesorella*, *Grasia*) to those already handed down from the Dogger, but various of the latter, such as e.g. *Cidaris*, *Rhabdocidaris*, *Diplocidaris*, *Hemicidaris*, *Acrocidaris*, *Pseudodiadema*, *Glypticus*, *Hemipedina*, *Pedina*, *Stomechinus*, *Pygaster*, are distinguished by greater wealth of species or individuals. Besides the coral facies the sponge facies was evidently favourable to the Seeigel; on the other hand they are found **[p. 550]** in smaller number in marly and clayey deposits. Here the delicate, thin-shelled forms predominate, while in the coral limestone are found chiefly the large, thick-shelled Cidaridae armed with large spines. As a noteworthy peculiarity of most Jurassic and Triassic Cidaridae, Salenidae and Diadematidae, the crenulate and perforate constitution of the spine tubercles deserves to be emphasized.

The upper Jurassic beds have many genera in common with the Lower Cretaceous (Valanginian, Neocomian, Urgonian, Aptian), such as *Cidaris*, *Rhabdocidaris*, *Hemicidaris*, *Acrocidaris*, *Pseudodiadema*, *Hemipedina*, *Leiosoma*, *Cyphosoma*, *Magnosia*, *Stomechinus*, *Acrosalenia*, *Holectypus*, *Pygaster*, *Echinobrissus*, *Phyllobrissus*, *Pygurus*, *Collyrites*, *Dysaster*, *Metaporhinus*. To these are added, among the Cidaridae, the remarkable forms *Tetracidaris* and *Orthocidaris*, among the Saleniden *Peltastes*, *Salenia* and *Goniopygus*, among the Glyphostomata *Pseudocidaris*, *Codiopsis* and *Psammechinus*. Considerably stronger is the increase of the irregular Seeigel. To the Jurassic Echinoconiden, which all together continue into the Lower Cretaceous, is added the genus *Discoidea*. Among the Cassiduliden, *Hyboclypus*, *Galeropygus*, *Galeroclypeus* and *Clypeus* do not cross the limits of the Jurassic formation; in their place in the Lower Cretaceous come *Bothriopygus*, *Pygaulus*, *Clypeopygus* and *Catopygus*, among which the 5 first are distinguished by wealth of species and individuals.

Among the Holasteriden, *Collyrites*, *Dysaster* and *Metaporhinus* continue; to them is added as a new type the genus *Holaster*. Of particular interest is the appearance of the oldest Spatangiden *Toxaster*, *Heteraster* and *Enallaster*, to which in the Upper Aptian are added also the oldest species of *Epiaster* and *Hemiaster*.

The Gault contains a small but characteristic Seeigel fauna, which consists mainly of species of the genera *Cidaris*, *Peltastes*, *Pseudodiadema*, *Discoidea*, *Echinoconus*, *Catopygus*, *Pygurus*, *Collyrites*, *Holaster*, *Epiaster* and *Hemiaster*.

The Echinoideen undergo an essential transformation in the Middle and Upper Cretaceous. Among the Regulares the genera *Cidaris*, *Temnocidaris*, *Salenia*, *Goniophorus*, *Echinothuria*, *Heterodiadema*, *Pseudodiadema*, *Cyphosoma*, *Codiopsis*, *Goniopygus*, *Micropsis*, *Glyphocyphus*, *Orthopsis*, *Echinocyphus*, *Leiosoma* and *Magnosia* are especially to be emphasized. Among the irregular Gnathostomen, *Echinoconus* and *Discoidea* stand in fullest development and have taken the place of the already extinct genera *Holectypus* and *Pileus*; the Clypeastriden, too, present themselves for the first time with the two **[p. 551]** small genera *Fibularia* and *Echinocyamus*. The Cassiduliden are still abundantly represented by *Pyrina*, *Caratomus*, *Pygaulus*, *Nucleolites*, *Catopygus*, *Cassidulus*, *Pygorhynchus*, *Faujasia*, *Archiacia* and *Claviaster*.

Most characteristic of the Middle and Upper Cretaceous is the extraordinary development of the Holasteriden and Spatangiden. Among the former, the whole group of the Collyritiden has indeed ceased to exist, but in return the subfamily of the Ananchytinae acquires an extraordinary wealth of forms. The genera *Ananchytes*, *Stenonia*, *Offaster*, *Holaster*, *Cardiaster*, *Infulaster* and *Hemipneustes* are partly restricted to the Middle and Upper Cretaceous, partly have their principal distribution here. Of the Spatangiden the older genera *Toxaster*, *Heteraster* and *Enallaster* have either disappeared or at least become very rare; on the other hand the genera *Micraster*, *Epiaster*, *Hemiaster* furnish an abundant number of mostly very common and gregariously occurring species, while *Linthia* (*Periaster*) and *Prenaster* reach their culmination only in the Eocene.

With regard to the spatial distribution of the Cretaceous Seeigel, the same holds as has already been said of the Jurassic ones. They are found everywhere where the Cretaceous formation appears in a favourable facies. On the whole the extra-Alpine deposits in North Germany, Poland, Belgium, northern France, in the Jura mountains and in England surpass the Alpine areas in wealth of Echinoideen, yet certain Mediterranean stretches such as Provence, eastern Egypt and Palestine do not lag behind northern Europe; indeed Algeria seems during the Cretaceous formation to have been positively the area richest in Seeigel. Comparatively poor in Echinoideen are the East Indian and American Cretaceous deposits.

With the Tertiary formation a fundamental transformation makes itself felt among the Echinoideen. The deep-sea forms prevailing in the Cretaceous are replaced by littoral types, and in place of the older forms come genera which for the most part still exist today. Already in the Eocene the regular Seeigel have ceased to play a leading role. In the once so species-rich families of the Cidaridae and Salenidae only isolated species still appear, from the genera *Cidaris*, *Leiocidaris* and *Salenia*, to which *Porocidaris* is added as a new type. Among the Glyphostomata, *Pseudodiadema*, *Cyphosoma*, *Micropsis* and *Psammechinus* pass over from the Cretaceous formation into the Eocene, and to these are joined some new genera, such as *Echinopsis*, *Opechinus*, *Salmacis*, *Echinopedina*, *Leiopedina*, *Echinus*.

**[p. 552]**

Among the irregular Gnathostomen the Echinoconiden cease with the close of the Cretaceous formation; they are replaced in the Eocene by the Conoclypeidae with the two extinct genera *Conoclypus* and *Oviclypeus*. Of Clypeastriden, *Echinocyamus*, *Sismondia*, *Scutellina*, *Lenita* and *Clypeaster* show a greater or smaller number of species; on the other hand the subfamily of the Scutelliden is still completely lacking in the Eocene, at least in Europe, Africa and Asia. The jawless Seeigel become more frequent the nearer one approaches the present. In the Eocene are found, of Cassiduliden, chiefly *Amblypygus*, *Echinanthus*, *Pygorhynchus* and *Echinolampas*; of Holasteriden only *Palaeopneustes*; of Spatangiden, on the other hand, *Cyclaster*, *Brissus*, *Brissopsis*, *Metalia*, *Linthia*, *Schizaster*, *Pericosmus*, *Prenaster*, *Agassizia*, *Brissopatagus*, *Echinocardium*, *Gualteria*, *Breynia*, *Macropneustes*, *Peripneustes*, *Eupatagus*, *Spatangus*, *Maretia*.

In contrast to the Jurassic and Cretaceous formation, one meets the Eocene Seeigel in greater number in deposits of the Mediterranean province than in northern Europe. The beds of the so-called "Nummulite formation" in their wide extension through southern Europe, North Africa and Asia have furnished by far the majority, the North European ones a more limited quantity, of the Eocene Echinoideen.

If the Echinoideen of the older Tertiary period already show manifold echoes of the fauna of the present, this approximation becomes ever more decided in the Miocene and Pliocene, so that in the latter, in the main, everywhere the same genera occur which still inhabit the neighbouring seas today. The Seeigel of the younger Tertiary deposits are distinguished fairly definitely from the Eocene ones. The latter still show many a similarity to the forms of the Cretaceous formation; the former are evidently forerunners of the present Echinoideen fauna. If one considers the families individually, the diminution of the Cidaridae and Salenidae in the younger Tertiary period is striking; Echinothuriden are unknown in the whole Tertiary period; among the Glyphostomata the Diadematiden recede more and more and are displaced by the subfamily of the Echinidae. The younger Tertiary deposits are most sharply distinguished from the older by the preponderance of Clypeastriden. Precisely the large genera, such as *Clypeaster* and *Laganum*, are represented by the most species, and the Scutellinen too appear with a series of in part very stately genera, such as *Scutella*, *Amphiope*, *Echinarachnius*, *Runa*.

The Cassiduliden have decreased considerably, yet individual genera such as *Nucleolites*, *Echinolampas*, *Echinanthus* still furnish a number of species. In Europe young-Tertiary Holasteriden are entirely lacking; on the other hand in Central America, the West Indies and Australia *Ananchytes*, **[p. 553]** *Asterostoma* and *Holaster* have been found. Besides the Clypeastriden, the Spatangiden play the principal role among the Echinoideen of the younger Tertiary period. They are in the main the same genera as in the Eocene, and among these again *Brissus*, *Brissopsis*, *Metalia*, *Linthia*, *Schizaster*, *Echinocardium*, *Breynia*, *Eupatagus*, *Spatangus* and *Maretia* are especially widespread.

If one compares the various formations with one another, the Middle and Upper Cretaceous may stand foremost in wealth of forms. With the exception of the Clypeastriden and Spatangiden, all families of the Euechinoideen are there at the full height of their development. Little poorer are the Eocene and the Jurassic formation. The latter, to be sure, is richer in species than in genera.

As regards the duration of life of the species, the best connoisseurs of the fossil Seeigel, such as Desor, Cotteau and Loriol, agree that only rarely is one and the same species common to several stages, and that not a single one crosses the boundary of a formation. The species are mostly short-lived, sharply characterized, and rarely connected with one another by transitional forms. Nonetheless many facts point to the Echinoideen of the present and of the earlier periods of the earth standing in a genetic connection.

Attention has already been drawn above to the great agreement of a number of still existing deep-sea forms, especially from the families of the Holasteriden, Echinothuriden, Saleniden and Glyphostomata (*Pourtalesia*, *Palaeotropus*, *Platybrissus*, *Aceste*, *Aerope*, *Homolampas* etc., *Asthenosoma*, *Phormosoma*, *Salenia*, *Peltastes*, *Hemipedina*, *Cyphosoma* etc.), with Upper Cretaceous types. Various genera (*Catopygus*, *Salenia*, *Hemipedina*, *Cyphosoma*, *Pygaster* etc.), which were held to be long extinct, have been shown by the deep-sea investigations to be still existing, and speak for a connection of the Cretaceous and the present Echinoideen fauna. Likewise the Tertiary Seeigel of Australia stand on the one hand very close to the Recent species of the Australian province, which bear a much more antique stamp than their contemporaries in other seas; on the other hand they recall, as Duncan (Quart. journ. geol. Soc. vol. 33. p. 63) has shown, through the occurrence of *Rhynchopygus*, *Echinobrissus*, *Catopygus*, *Holaster*, *Micraster* and *Megalaster*, the Cretaceous Echinoideen fauna in Europe and the East Indies. In a similar relation stand the Seeigel of the East Indian Nummulite deposits (Eocene) to the genera still existing today in the West Indies.

But a comparison of the morphological characters of the fossil Seeigel with their temporal succession and with the ontogeny of their **[p. 554]** Recent relatives also leads to genetic series in which phylogeny, ontogeny and morphology harmoniously agree.

On the origin of the Echinoideen, palaeontology, it is true, affords us no information. Even if a common descent of all Echinodermen from a primordial type may be highly probable, since according to Al. Agassiz there is in the development of the Ophiuren, Seesterne and Seeigel a stage where the embryos, after resorption of the pluteus, show no essential differences, those common primordial forms would nevertheless have to lie in beds from which no recognizable fossils have been handed down to us. However imperfectly known and however rare the oldest Seeigel from the Silurian period may be, they nevertheless already bear the typical characters of the class and are sharply separated from the Asteroideen and Crinoideen.

In the Palaeolithic age the Palechinoideen or Tesselaten prevail, which (apart perhaps from the problematic *Cystocidaris*) may with full right be regarded as the forerunners of the Regulares, since they agree with these in the principal characters and in a certain sense (cf. p. 479) represent juvenile states of the Cidaridae. With the beginning of the middle age there sets in the most radical transformation of the Seeigel that has ever taken place. Possibly the Tesselaten still continue in the genus *Anaulocidaris* into the Trias, but in the Lias they have definitively disappeared and the Regulares have taken their place (cf. table pp. 556 and 557). Among these the Cidaridae, Salenidae and Glyphostomata begin simultaneously; somewhat later, in the Middle Jurassic and in the Cretaceous, the Echinothuridae follow. The latter, and still more the genus *Tetracidaris*, deserve in phylogenetic respect every attention as atavistic reversions to the Palaeozoic Perischoechiniden. Among the Cidariden and Salenidae individual genera such as *Cidaris*, *Rhabdocidaris*, *Leiocidaris*, *Salenia* and others make themselves noticeable by a certain rigidity, whereby they hold fast to their essential characters through several formations, in part even into the present; on the other hand the Glyphostomata show a greater fluidity and capacity for transformation, which expresses itself in the unfolding of numerous, in part very short-lived genera. As against the Cidariden, the Glyphostomata with their compound large plates are indisputably the more differentiated and higher-standing forms, and among these the Echinidae, as the most complicated, may again occupy the highest rank. In this sequence, however, they also develop in the various formations: the Diadematiden form the oldest group of the Glyphostomata, and among the Echinidae the Oligopori again precede the Polypori. In the **[p. 555]** Tertiary period and the present the many-pored Echiniden and Echinometriden quite decidedly predominate, in the Jurassic and the Cretaceous the Diadematiden and few-pored Echiniden. This phenomenon too rests upon a phyletic and ontogenetic agreement, for the Recent Polypori pass through an "Oligopori stage" in their development.

The exocyclic Gnathostomata form an independent branch. On their descent from the Palechinoideen we have no firm points of support; perhaps they are to be derived from *Cystocidaris*, but then all the intermediate members up to the Lias would have to have been lost. The oldest genus *Pygaster* begins abruptly in the Lias; upon it follow the remaining Echinoconiden, which prevail in the Jurassic and the Lower Cretaceous. In the Upper Cretaceous a splitting seems to occur. The genus *Echinoconus* leads gradually to the older Tertiary family of the Conoclypeidae with petaloid ambulacra, while *Discoidea* with its weakly developed internal radial septa points toward the Clypeastridae. The latter show themselves in the uppermost Cretaceous with two small forms (*Fibularia* and *Echinocyamus*) of embryonic stamp, and only in the Eocene develop genera with excellently petaloid ambulacra. The principal development of the Euclypeastrinen, moreover, falls in the later Tertiary period, and still younger are the Scutellinen, which in Europe begin in the Miocene and display their greatest wealth of forms in the present.

If one takes into account the great agreement of the oldest Cassiduliden, such as *Galeropygus* and *Hyboclypus*, with *Pygaster*, one can hardly resist the supposition that the Cassiduliden sprang from the same source as the Echinoconiden and went their own ways only after atrophy of the jaw apparatus. With *Galeropygus*, however, there begins in the Lias the jawless subfamily of the Echinoneen, which by their band-shaped ambulacra and by the simple structure of the peristome stand as embryonic types to the true Cassiduliden of the subfamily of the Echinolampinen. While the Echinoneen have already almost exhausted their form-producing power in the Upper Jurassic and continue only in the genera *Pyrina* and *Echinoneus*, the Echinolampinen reach full unfolding only in the Cretaceous and in the Eocene.

The two last families of the Atelostomen, the Holasteriden and Spatangiden, can be conceived phylogenetically as two developmental stages of one and the same type, for the former behave in all essential characters like persistent juvenile forms of the Spatangiden. Their geological succession also agrees with this. The [text interrupted here by the table on pp. 556–557, given below; it resumes on p. 558] Holasteriden begin already in the Middle Jurassic and reach their culmination in the Upper Cretaceous; the Spatangiden begin in the Lower Cretaceous and characterize chiefly the Tertiary period and the present. Their origin is for the present still enigmatic; they do indeed possess some relationship with the Cassiduliden, yet direct points of connection between the two families are lacking.

**[p. 558]**

As foreign elements the Dysasterinen first emerge in the Middle Jurassic as forerunners of the true Holasteriden; they develop a small number of genera and become extinct in the Lower Cretaceous, where they make way for the Ananchytinen, which for their part reach their culmination at the end of the Cretaceous formation and hand down descendants into the Tertiary period and the present only in deep-sea deposits.

As a special side branch the Spatangiden may have arisen from the Dysasterinen or possibly from a still older unknown ancestral form; among them the oldest, the Palaeostomen beginning in the Lower Cretaceous, again represent persistent juvenile forms of the later typical Spatangiden, which in the Tertiary and in the present, together with the Clypeastriden, form about three quarters of the whole Echinoideen fauna.

If one surveys in the accompanying tabular (2) compilation (pp. 556 and 557) the chronological succession of the more important Echinoideen genera, some persistent types (such as *Cidaris*, *Salenia*, *Holectypus*, *Pyrina*) first strike the eye, which are usually distinguished by great wealth of species and pass through several formations without essential transformation of their principal characters. Besides these conservative types there arise in each family a greater or smaller number of genera with a moderate, in part even very short, duration of life. The strongest changes and the most abundant appearance of new types almost always coincide with geological boundaries, i.e. with essential changes in the external conditions of existence. In what manner the transformation of the organisms was brought about by divergent distribution of water and land, by climatic change, ocean currents, migrations, isolation, struggle for existence, etc., will, to be sure, be demonstrable today in only the fewest cases.

According to present experience, "re-mintings" among the Echinoideen seem to have taken place in an unusually energetic manner at three different epochs. The first and most thoroughgoing, at the beginning of the Mesolithic age, has already been mentioned above; a **[p. 559]** second, less vigorous one occurred at the close of the Cretaceous formation, the third at the beginning of the Middle Cretaceous period, in the Cenomanian and Turonian stages. Had the Seeigel been decisive for the delimitation of the formations, the Jurassic formation would close only with the Aptian and the Cretaceous would begin with the Gault. According to Al. Agassiz, the phylogenetic "periods of re-minting" would also have their repetition in the developmental history of the Recent Seeigel, in that certain stages of the metamorphosis are passed through with greater speed than others.

---
(2) In the table, the genera recurring in two adjacent formations are connected by braces.

If one applies to the Echinoideen the principles decisive for the determination of rank in the animal kingdom, according to which greater differentiation of one and the same organization, concentration and reduction of equivalent organs signify a perfecting, and according to which persistent juvenile forms stand behind the further developed mature ones in rank, then the Regulares, on account of their radial, indifferent structure, occupy the lowest, and the Spatangiden, on account of their pronouncedly bilateral, strongly differentiated structure, the highest grade. Between the two lie the exocyclic Gnathostomen, which gravitate more toward the Regulares, as well as the Cassiduliden and Holasteriden, which attach themselves more closely to the Spatangiden.

The whole phyletic development of the Echinoideen shows unmistakably, in the appearance of the various orders and families and in the succession of the individual genera, not only an increase in wealth of forms and a gradual approximation to the present, but also an ascending development from the imperfect to the more perfect, a steady progress from the lower to the higher.

## 4. Class. Holothurioidea. Seegurken, Seewalzen [sea cucumbers].

The capacity for preservation of these cylindrical and club-shaped bodies, which in their whole appearance recall large worms rather than Echinodermen, is an exceedingly slight one, because the Holothurien lack a coherent calcareous skeleton and because the isolated calcareous bodies scattered in the leathery skin are so minute and so fragile that they easily escape observation, if they are capable of fossilization at all. Those calcareous bodies, moreover, usually have a very regular and characteristic form; they are anchors, little wheels, small net-like plates, which can be well determined even when they occur singly. What has, moreover, been reckoned up to now among fossil remains to the Holothurien is for the greater part of doubtful origin.

**[p. 560]**

Certainly a crude impression of a body from the lithographic slate of Bavaria, which Rüppel (Abbildung und Beschreibung einiger neuer oder wenig gekannter Versteinerungen aus der Kalkschieferformation von Solenhofen. Frankfurt 1829) ascribes to a Holothurie, derives rather from a naked Cephalopode or some other animal than from a Holothurie, and the remains described by Giebel as *Protoholothuria* from the same beds are also thoroughly problematic bodies. The three-pronged anchors from the Franconian Scyphien limestone figured by Count Münster as *Synapta Sieboldi* (Münster. Beiträge zur Petrefactenkunde. VI. 92) are spicules of Spongien, and whether the worm-shaped bodies which Goldfuss described under the name *Lumbricaria* from the lithographic slate are to be regarded as intestines of Holothurien, as Giebel thinks, appears more than doubtful.

The only remains which may with some certainty be referred to fossil Holothurien are the little wheels with radial spokes first demonstrated by Conr. Schwager (Württembergische Jahreshefte für vaterländische Naturkunde 1865. Heft I. S. 114) in various horizons of the White and Brown Jura, which belong either to *Chirodota* or to a closely related genus. Waagen and Terquem (Terquem et Jourdy Monographie de l'étage Bathonien de la Moselle. p. 148) have described similar little bodies from the *Sowerbyi* zone, the Lias and the Bathonian. According to R. Etheridge (Quart. journ. geol. Soc. vol. 50. p. 316), remains of *Synapta* and *Chirodota* are said to be found even as early as the Carboniferous limestone of Scotland. From quite young Tertiary beds Nicholson mentions small plates of *Psolus*, and from Recent mud samples, finally, Ehrenberg has figured various little bodies which could derive from Holothurien.
